第二天傍晚，麦克劳克林家已经收拾得灯火通明，单等客人上门。暮色里透出来的那点光亮，来自凡能从邻居家借来的新煤油灯——有多少盏，点多少盏。屋里，床都挪了出去，好腾出跳舞的地方；只是伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林（Isobel McLaughlin）叹息着想，顶好也不过是个拉小提琴拉得平平常常的提琴手，断不会再有一个一举一动都合着舞曲节拍的人——像她的艾伦那样。屋里，蚊子在灯光和灯油的气味里嗡嗡打转；屋外，谁要是转身离开那几堆烟熏火，就要挨蚊子咬——房子和谷仓之间的这几堆烟是小伙子们堆起来的，他们小心地守着，不让它们烧出明火。天一擦黑，一辆辆坐满道贺之人的马车赶到了；每来一家，卧房里椅子上那摞拼布被子就高出一截，前院里的结婚礼物也越发闹腾起来，挣脱了绊索，撒腿就跑，被聚在一处的半大小子们喊叫着追来追去。有的客人送来鸭子，有的送来带着雏鸡的母鸡。有的送来哭哭咧咧的鹅，有的送来惊慌失措、直着嗓门抗议的小猪。乡绅（Squire）送来一头小母牛犊。基思家——可怜的心慌意乱的莉比姑妈（Aunt Libby）和约翰·基思姑父（Uncle John Keith）——送来两把沉甸甸的椅子，是他去年冬天用胡桃木打的。

===Original===

The bride was not visible. Wully had guarded her carefully, even from a minute alone with his mother, ever since he had arranged her wedding. He told his mother now that Chirstie had consented, she was worried about what her father would say when he heard about it. And because it was so soon after her mother’s death. Isobel McLaughlin reassured her. The wedding was the best possible solution of the situation. Let them just leave Chirstie’s father to her! She comforted the girl earnestly, being distressed by her face. She hoped in her heart that the marriage would put an end to the girl’s newly developed and stubborn depression. She couldn’t understand why now that the guests were arriving, the bride should still seem just terrified. No less word described her condition. Isobel McLaughlin could do nothing but leave her with Wully. In his room, where he sat holding her close against him, every time she said, “I can’t do this, Wully! I won’t!” he kissed her again, powerfully. She must go through with it now, he whispered to her. Even the minister was waiting for them now.

He led her forth, at last, into the parlor. She was wearing the white dress her mother had made for her the summer before, which Mrs. McLaughlin had ironed that day, and freshened with her daughter Mary’s cherry-colored ribbons. Wully, harassed by the trivial necessity for respectable garments, was wearing the suit his mother had made for his brother John to wear to college in the fall. It didn’t fit Wully altogether, but then, it scarcely fitted John at all. In a space in the midst of their unsuspecting kinsmen they stood, the bride as pale as death, the groom nervously hiding his fear that at the critical minute his bride might altogether reject him.

===Chinese===

新娘子不露面。自打定下她的婚事，伍利就把她看护得严严实实，连跟他母亲单独待上一分钟都不让。这会儿他告诉母亲，克丽丝蒂（Chirstie）既然已经应允，心里正愁她父亲听说了这件事会怎么说呢。又加上，她母亲下世才没多久。伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林给她宽心。这场婚礼，是眼下这个局面顶好的解决办法。克丽丝蒂的父亲，只管交给她去对付！她恳切地安慰这姑娘，为她那张脸心里难受。她暗自指望，这场婚事能把姑娘新近生出来的、又犟又拗的心病给了结掉。她不明白，客人都到了，新娘子怎么看着还只是吓坏了的样子。再找不出第二个字眼能形容她这副光景。伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林没有法子，只好把她交给伍利。在他屋里，他搂着她紧紧贴着自己，她每说一回“这个我做不来，伍利！我不肯！”他就再吻她一回，重重地吻。事到如今，她非得把这桩事撑到底不可，他附在她耳边悄声说。眼下连牧师都候着他们了。

他到底领着她出来，进了客厅。她身上那条白裙子，是去年夏天她母亲给她做的，这一天麦克劳克林太太把它熨得平平整整，又添上她女儿玛丽的樱桃色缎带，叫它添了新鲜气色。伍利叫体面衣裳这桩小小的必需搅得心烦，穿的是母亲给他弟弟约翰（John）做下、预备秋天上大学穿的那一套。那套衣裳穿在伍利身上不全合身，不过话说回来，它在约翰身上也从来没怎么合过身。他们站在一屋子浑然不觉的亲戚当中空出来的一小块地方上，新娘子脸白得像死人，新郎提心吊胆，藏着自己心里的害怕——怕到了要紧的那一刻，新娘子会把他整个儿回绝掉。

===Original===

He kept watching her covertly as the minister tried the patience of man and God by the length of his prayer. He tried to stand near enough her to support her. When the invocations ceased, everyone in the room lifted his head—except the bride. The minister explained interminably the nature of holy matrimony. He exhorted the pair to mutual faithfulness. Wully felt her tremble.

“Will you have this man to be your husband?” he asked at length.

She kept silent. She couldn’t raise her head. Wully felt his heart beginning to beat furiously. She was going to refuse him, in spite of all he had done.

There was an awful moment. The room seemed to be hushed and waiting. It was terrible, the length of that moment of silence. At last he spoke forth simply.

“You wouldn’t think she would. But she will. Won’t you, Chirstie?”

===Chinese===

牧师那篇祷告，长得把人和上帝的耐性都熬炼着，这工夫，伍利一直偷偷照看着她，想站得离她近些，好扶住她。祷告的呼求停了，满屋子的人都抬起了头——只有新娘子没有。牧师没完没了地讲解神圣婚配的道理。他劝诫这一对要彼此忠贞。伍利觉出她在发抖。

“你肯要这个男人做你的丈夫吗？”牧师终于问道。

她不作声。她抬不起头来。伍利觉出自己的心开始狂跳。她要回绝他了，不顾他做下的这一切。

有那么吓人的一瞬。满屋子像是屏住了气，在等着。那阵沉默竟拖得那样长，真可怕。末了他简简单单地开了口。

“你们不会想到她肯的。可她肯。克丽丝蒂，你说是不是？”

===Original===

Those standing near heard his words, and as the outraged divine whispered sternly, “Answer!” he bent down and kissed her.

She looked around like one in a nightmare. Her lips moved. The minister accepted the sign. He proceeded with the ceremony. The smile which Wully’s words had occasioned spread from those standing nearest even to those who were looking in at the windows—those who pretended to be leaving room for the rest, but were really thinking of their unsuitable bare feet.

The minister had made them man and wife.

The crowd gathered around them. The Squire gave Chirstie a resounding smack on her cheek. Girls were pressing around her, the roomful was gathering near her. But she swayed, and fell against her husband, and fainted quite away.

Of course that fainting was altogether the smartest feature of the hurried wedding. Not many hardworking prairie women had bodies which permitted such gentility. It was a distinguished thing to do. The women who saw it forgot for a while to comment on the strange appearance of the bride, which they understood more fully later. At the time it seemed no more than a proper honor to pay Jeannie McNair’s memory. When she was herself again, Wully found a place for her out of doors. Planks laid on boxes and chairs made seats for supper out there where the smoke defended them, and since there was no back for her to lean against, she having just fainted and all, it was only proper that Wully’s arm do its duty around her. And it was necessary that it give her little strengthening messages, while inside the more zealous young things danced to the fiddle that was not Allen’s. Out in the warm starlight and the smoke, the older guests talked to the bride and groom.

===Chinese===

站得近的人都听见了他的话；那位受了冒犯的牧师厉声低喝“回答！”的时候，他俯下身去，吻了她。

她像做噩梦的人似的四下张望。她的嘴唇动了动。牧师认下了这个表示，把仪式进行了下去。伍利那句话引出来的笑意，从站得最近的人身上，一直传到那些扒着窗户往里张望的人——那些人装着是在给里头的人腾地方，心里其实在惦记自己那双不相宜的光脚板。

牧师已经把他们结成了夫妻。

一群人围拢到他们跟前。乡绅在克丽丝蒂腮帮上响亮地亲了一口。姑娘们往她跟前挤，满屋子的人朝她凑过来。可她身子一晃，倒在她丈夫身上，昏死过去了。

不用说，这场仓促办成的婚事里头，那一场昏厥十足要算最体面的节目。草原上辛劳的妇人，没有几个有这样一副身子，行得出这样的斯文来。这是桩露脸的事。看见的婆娘们一时忘了议论新娘子那副古怪的模样——那模样，她们后来才更加明白了。在当时看来，那不过是对珍妮·麦克奈尔（Jeannie McNair）的在天之灵恰如其分的一份敬意。等她缓过神来，伍利给她在外头安顿了个座儿。木板搭在箱子和椅子上，就在外头烟气护着的地方摆出了晚饭的座席；她背后没有可以靠的东西——她刚晕过去来着——那么，叫伍利的胳膊搂在她身上尽它的本分，就再应当不过了。里头那些格外起劲的年轻人，正踏着那把不是艾伦的提琴跳舞，这就更得叫那条胳膊时不时地传给她些添力气的悄悄话了。外头，暖暖的星光底下，烟气里头，年长的客人们同新娘新郎攀谈。

===Original===

Aunt Libby joined them again, when by chance they were for a moment alone.

“Tell me again what it was Peter said, Wully!” she begged.

He felt Chirstie shrinking against him.

“He told me in the morning that he had decided to go this time for sure. I told him he was foolish. And I rode over again to give him some advice in the evening.”

Chirstie’s hand stirred nervously within his, and he held it more firmly.

===Chinese===

有一回，他们两个凑巧得着片刻独处，莉比姑妈又凑了过来。

“再给我说说彼得说了什么来着，伍利！”她央求道。

他觉出克丽丝蒂往他身上缩。

“早上他跟我说，这一回他是打定主意非走不可了。我说他傻。傍晚我又骑马过去，想劝他几句。”

克丽丝蒂的手在他手里紧张地动了动，他把那只手握得更紧了。

===Original===

“And did he not say where he was going?”

“He only said west.”

“That’s all he said in his note!” She sighed brokenheartedly. “It’s a strange thing he wouldn’t heed you, Wully!”

Wully gritted his teeth. “He certainly heeded me that time!” he thought grimly to himself. He had already told his aunt those nicely dovetailing lies half a dozen times, and each time he had felt them crushing his wife. He wished his aunt would go away and leave them in peace. After all, her cursed Peter hadn’t got a taste of what he deserved!

Finally the wedding was over. Time, however it drags, must eventually pass. They had driven away together, after he had changed John’s good clothes for a fresh hickory shirt and jeans, leaving Dod at the McLaughlins’. They had had twenty-four hours of the unfathomable luxury of unhindered intimacy. The baby sister was asleep. It was bedtime again.

===Chinese===

“那他没说要去哪儿吗？”

“他只说了声往西。”

“他字条上也就写了这么一句！”她伤心欲绝地叹了口气。“他竟不肯听你的话，伍利，真是怪事！”

伍利咬紧了牙关。“那一回他可是听足了我的话！”他心里狠狠地想。那些严丝合缝、互相咬合的谎话，他已经对姑妈说了六遍了，每一遍，他都觉出它们在把他妻子压碎。他巴望姑妈走开去，让他们清静清静。说到底，她家那个该死的彼得，还没尝着他该受的报应呢！

婚事总算完了。时间这东西，不管拖得多磨蹭，到头来总得过完。他脱下约翰那身好衣裳，换上一件干净的条纹粗布衬衫和粗斜纹布裤子，两人一道赶车走了，把多德（Dod）留在麦克劳克林家。他们已经享了二十四个钟头那种深不见底的奢侈福气——无人搅扰的两情缱绻。小妹妹已经睡着了。又到睡觉的时候了。

===Original===

The new family sat down for prayers. Not that Wully was a man deeply religious. But, as far as he knew, daily family prayers was one of the things a decent man does for his family. They had read that morning, according to custom, the first chapter of Genesis, and that had been most satisfactory, even quite personally interesting now, all about male and female created He them. It had come over Wully with a chuckle that divine commands have seldom been as satisfactory to humans as that first one was. And now, in the evening, he had read the first chapter of the New Testament. He resented that. He wouldn’t have read it if he had remembered what was in it. That story of Mary’s humiliation might seem ever so slightly to reflect upon his wife. And that right he denied even to the Word of God.

They were sitting together on the doorstep, and his lips were not far from her ear.

===Chinese===

这个新立的小家坐下来做祷告。倒不是伍利是个虔信很深的人。可就他所知，天天带一家人做祷告，是一个正派人替家里做的本分之一。那天早上，他们照着老规矩读了《创世记》（Genesis）第一章，那一章读着顶叫人称心，如今甚至相当切身有趣，通篇讲的是上帝造男造女。伍利心里咯咯直乐地悟出来，上帝的吩咐，很少有哪一道像头一道那样合人心意。到了晚上，他读的是《新约》头一章。这一章他满心不痛快。要是记得里头是什么内容，他压根儿不会去读它。那个马利亚（Mary）受屈辱的故事，听上去未免有那么一丝一毫，像是在影射他的妻子。这份权利，他连上帝的话语也不肯给。

两人并排坐在门槛上，他的嘴唇离她的耳朵不远。

===Original===

“Yon was a strange man, now, Chirstie!” he began.

“What man?”

“That Joseph in Matthew. I fear he hadn’t very good sense.”

“Why, Wully! And him a man in the Bible!”

“I don’t care! He didn’t know much! He didn’t know enough to take his own lassie till an angel told him! A man like that! He was daft. Or else—”

===Chinese===

“方才那一个，可真是个怪人呢，克丽丝蒂！”他起了话头。

“哪个人？”

“《马太福音》里那个约瑟（Joseph）。我怕他脑子不大灵光。”

“哎呀，伍利！那可是《圣经》里的人物！”

“我不管！他就是懂得太少！天使不来告诉他，他连把自己的姑娘娶到手都不敢！像他那样的男人！他准是昏了头。要不然——”

===Original===

“I wonder at you, Wully! Or else what?”

“I doubt the lassie wasn’t really bonnie. Not like mine!”

A deeper embrace. More kisses.

“Oh, *Wully*!”

===Chinese===

“亏你说得出口，伍利！要不然怎么着？”

“我瞧那姑娘八成不怎么俊。可不像我的这一个！”

搂得更紧了。吻得更密了。

“哦，伍利！”

## VII / 第七章

===Original===

It was growingly inevitable that the news, the determined news, must be broken. Wully, with his whole heart shrinking from the task, made light of it to Chirstie. Wasn’t having her better than anything he had ever imagined! He hadn’t really known at all at the time how greatly he was enriching himself. If he had been ready then to shoulder whatever blame there might be, he was ready now to do it a dozen times over. He didn’t mind in the least telling his parents about it. Accidents of the sort happen among even the most respectable people from time to time. It was in vain that he tried to reassure her. It might be all very well for him to talk so, but when everyone knew about her—Oh, what should she do then! Was it that she doubted him, then? Wasn’t he going to be with her? If by chance there should be one neighbor rash enough to see anything not perfect about his marriage, he would tell her for sure there would never be another! It was his mother she thought most about! What would his mother ever do when she heard it? That was nothing! Wully would go and explain it all to her, after his fashion—falsely, his wife insisted on saying wretchedly. His mother would be angry, of course, at first, and give him the scolding of his life. But she’d soon get over it, and come over bringing Chirstie a lot of baby clothes. Chirstie would see if she wouldn’t! Why hadn’t he explained it to her then, the last time he went over for that purpose, if it was so light a matter? The children happened to be all at home that day because the teacher was ill, and he had got no word alone with her. He didn’t add that he had been highly relieved to find them all there. He would go over at once, so that the burden would be off Chirstie’s mind.

===Chinese===

那消息——那已经定下的消息——越来越瞒不住了，非说破不可。伍利一颗心直往回缩，怕的就是这桩差事，可到了克丽丝蒂（Chirstie）跟前，他偏把它说得轻描淡写。得了她，不比他这辈子想过的什么都强吗！那时候他实在全然不曾明白，自己是在怎样地给自己添家底。那时节他既然甘愿把不管什么样的责难都往肩上扛，如今他就更甘愿把它扛上十二回。把这事去告诉父母，他一点儿也不打怵。这样的意外，顶体面的人家里也时不时出呢。他想法子给她宽心，全然是白费。他这样说自然轻巧，可等人人都知道了她的事——哦，到那时候她可怎么办呢！这么说，她是疑心他了？他难道不是要跟她在一起的吗？万一真有哪个邻居莽撞得竟看出他的婚姻有哪点儿不圆满，他就告诉她，管保绝不会再有下一桩！她心里最放不下的，是他的母亲！他母亲听说了，究竟可怎么好啊？那算不得什么！伍利自会过去，按他的路数，把一切对她解释过去——撒谎，他的妻子惨然地一口咬定说。他母亲开头当然要生气，少不得把他这辈子没受过的训斥都补给他一顿。可她很快就会缓过来，过来时还会给克丽丝蒂捎上好多娃娃衣裳。克丽丝蒂就瞧着吧，她不这样才怪！既然是这么轻省的一桩事，上一回他为这个专门过去，怎么没跟她说明白呢？那天碰巧孩子们全都在家，因为老师病了，他没得着机会跟她单独说上话。他没添上说，见他们全都在家，他心里倒大大松了一口气。他这就过去，好叫克丽丝蒂把心头的这副担子卸下来。

===Original===

Having arrived at the scene of his humiliation the next morning, he saw his father coming from the cornfield with his hands and pockets full of chosen ears of seed corn. Wully met him in the path just behind the barn, and they greeted each other without a sign of affection. What did Wully think of these ears? Wully felt them critically, one after another, with his thumb, and found them good. His father started on towards the barn.

“I want to tell you something, father.”

He stopped without a word, and stood listening.

===Chinese===

第二天早上，他到了那处等着叫他受辱的地方，望见父亲从玉米地里出来，两只手连口袋里都装满了挑拣好的种子玉米。伍利就在谷仓后头的小径上迎住他，父子俩打了个照面，没露出一丝儿亲热。这些玉米棒子，伍利觉得怎么样？伍利用拇指一个挨一个挑剔地捏了捏，觉得都好。他父亲便接着朝谷仓走去。

“爸，我有桩事要告诉你。”

他一声不响地站住了，立在那里听。

===Original===

“We’re going to have a baby.”

“’Tis likely.”

“I mean—in December.”

“December? In December!”

“Yes. That’s what I mean.”

===Chinese===

“我们要有个孩子了。”

“多半是。”

“我是说——在十二月。”

“十二月？十二月里！”

“是的。我就是这个意思。”

===Original===

John McLaughlin’s long keen face, which changed expression only under great provocation, now surrendered to surprise. He stood still, looking at his son penetratingly a long time. Wully kicked an imaginary clod back and forth in the path. Presently the father said, with more bitterness than Wully had ever heard in his voice,

“It seems we have brought the old country to the new!”

Wully pondered this unexpected deliverance without looking up.

After a little the older man added, sighing,

“I prayed my sons might be men who could wait.”

===Chinese===

约翰·麦克劳克林（John McLaughlin）那张瘦长而冷峻的脸，不受大刺激是绝不变色的，这会儿也向吃惊降服了。他一动不动地站着，目光锐利地把儿子望了好半晌。伍利在小径上把一块压根儿不存在的土坷垃踢过来、踢过去。过了一会儿，父亲开口了，声音里带着的苦味，是伍利从来没有听他有过那么重的，

“看来咱们是把旧国也带到新地方来了！”

伍利没有抬头，心里掂量这句意外从宽的发落。

又过了一小会儿，老人家叹了口气，添上一句，

“我祷告过，愿我的儿子们都是能等得住的男人。”

===Original===

“A lot he knows about waiting!” thought Wully, half angrily. “Thirteen of us!”

“You tell mother about it, father,” he pleaded, knowing his entreaty useless.

“I will not!”

“I wish you would. I can’t—very well!”

“You’d best!”

===Chinese===

“他倒懂得什么叫等！”伍利半带恼怒地想。“咱们可是十三口！”

“爸，这事你去跟妈说吧，”他央求道，心里知道这央求没有用。

“我决不去！”

“我巴不得你去。我没法子——说不好！”

“你顶好自己去！”

===Original===

Wully stood watching him tie the yellow ears into clusters on the sheltered side of the barn. He was trying with all his might to gather courage to face his mother. He hadn’t felt such a nervous hesitancy since the first time he went into action. He remembered only too well the last time he had really stirred her displeasure. Allen and he had quarreled, and had nursed their anger, in spite of her remonstrances, for two days. He had growled out something to his brother across the supper table, and after that, she had put the little children to bed, and had set her two sons down before the fireplace—it was in the first house they were living then. She had drawn her chair near them, and had proceeded quietly and grimly to flay them with her tongue. She had continued with deliberateness till they were glad to escape half crying to bed. He remembered still how she had begun. It might be natural, she said, for brothers to quarrel. But she believed that it would never again be natural for her sons to quarrel in her presence. And she had been perfectly right about that. What she would say now, upon an occasion like this with her dismaying self-control, he couldn’t even imagine. It would be nothing common, he felt sure.

===Chinese===

伍利站着，看他把黄澄澄的玉米棒子一把一把拴在谷仓背风的那一面。他正拼尽全力，要把面对母亲的勇气聚拢来。打头一回上阵以来，他不曾有过这样神经发紧的踌躇。上一回他真正撩起她的恼怒，他记得再清楚不过了。艾伦（Allen）同他吵了架，不顾她的劝解，两个人把那口闷气足足养了两天。他在晚饭桌上隔着桌子朝兄弟咕哝了一句什么，过后，她把小的们打发上床，把她两个儿子按在壁炉跟前坐下——那时他们还住在头一座房子里。她把椅子拖到他们近旁，然后不动声色、板着铁青的面孔，开始用舌头剥他们两个的皮。她不慌不忙地数落下去，直数落到他们半哭半啼地巴不得逃上床去。她起头的话，他至今还记得。她说，弟兄们拌嘴，也许本是常情。可她相信，她的儿子们再要在她跟前拌嘴，就绝不会再是常情了。这一层她料得丝毫不差。如今遭上这么一桩事，凭她那份叫人心惊的自持，她会说出什么来，他连想象都想象不出。反正绝不是寻常的话，他拿得准。

===Original===

On the bed which she had just finished spreading with a “drunkard’s path” quilt, they sat down together in a low room of the second story, where three beds full of boys were accustomed to sleep. She kissed him fondly when he came to her, saying it was a lonely house with him away so much. She wondered why they had not been at church. Was Chirstie not well again?

“I have something to tell you, mother,” he stammered.

“I’m listening,” she said encouragingly, her eyes studying him tenderly. How beautiful a head he had! How beautiful a man he was!

“We’re going to have a baby! In December, mother!”

===Chinese===

在二楼一间低矮的屋子里——屋里那三张床，一向睡满男孩——他们一齐在她刚铺好的一床“醉汉小径”（drunkard’s path）花样的被子上坐下。他来到她跟前时，她疼爱地吻了他，说他在家的时候这样少，房子显得冷清。她纳闷他们怎么没上教堂。是克丽丝蒂又不爽利了吗？

“妈，我有桩事要告诉你，”他结结巴巴地说。

“我听着呢。”她鼓励道，眼睛温柔地端详着他。好一个俊秀的脑袋！好一个俊秀的男人！

“我们要有个孩子了！十二月里，妈！”

===Original===

Over her face there spread swiftly a smile of soft amusement. She had always looked that way when one of her children said something especially innocent and lovable.

“You don’t mean December, Wully! Dinna ye ken that? The wee’uns can’na just hurry so!”

He couldn’t look at her.

“I know what I mean!” he said, doggedly. “I mean December. I understand.” The silence became so ominous that at length he had to steal a look at her. Her incredulous face was flushed red with shame and anger. He rose to defend his love from her.

===Chinese===

她脸上倏地漾开一个和软的、觉得好玩的笑。她的孩子里谁说出顶天真顶可疼的话来时，她向来就是这副神情。

“你说的不会是十二月，伍利！你连这个都不明白吗？小不点儿们哪能这样赶的呀！”

他不敢看她。

“我知道我说的是什么！”他执拗地说。“我说的是十二月。我心里有数。”静默变得越来越不祥，到末了他只得偷眼看她一下。她那张不肯相信的脸，臊得气得通红。他站起身，要护住他的爱情，不让它受她的责难。

===Original===

“You aren’t to say a word against her. It wasn’t her fault!”

Then the storm broke.

“Do you think I’m likely to say a word against the poor, greetin’ bairn!” she cried. “Her sitting there alone among the wolves and snakes, and a son of mine to bring her to shame! I’ll never lift my head again!” Her rush of emotion quite choked her.

“My fine, brave soldier of a son!” she burst out, recovering herself. “You did well, now, to choose a lassie alone, with neither father nor mother to defend her from you!”

===Chinese===

“你不许说她半个不字。这不是她的错！”

风暴就此发作了。

“你以为我会去说那可怜的、哭哭啼啼的娃娃半个不字！”她喊道。“她孤零零一个人坐在那里，四围是狼是蛇，我倒养出个儿子去给她丢脸！我这颗头再也抬不起来了！”这一阵感情冲上来，把她完全哽住了。

“我那好样的、英勇的当兵儿子！”她缓过神来，又迸发出来。“你可真行啊，偏挑一个孤零零的姑娘，爹不在，娘也不在，没有人替她挡着你！”

===Original===

“Mother!” he cried.

“Jeannie’s wee Chirstie!” she went on. “No one else could please you, I suppose! Oh, she did well to die when her son was but a laddie!”

Wretchedly ashamed of his deceit as he was, he was not able to take more of her reproof without trying to defend himself.

“I didn’t mean any harm!” he mumbled. “I didn’t think.” That was what Peter had said.

===Chinese===

“妈！”他喊道。

“珍妮（Jeannie）家的小克丽丝蒂！”她接着说。“想来除她谁也入不了你的眼！唉，她死得可真好，儿子才刚是个小小子儿她就去了！”

他为自己的欺瞒臊得半死，可她再责备下去，他实在没法不试着替自己分辩几句了。

“我不是存心使坏！”他嘟嘟囔囔地说。“我没想过。”那正是彼得说过的话。

===Original===

“And why did you not think!” she demanded, furiously. “Have you no mind of your own! You didn’t know what you were doing, I suppose! Oh, that I should have a son who is a fool!”

How terrible mothers are! Fool was a word she hated so greatly that she never allowed her children to pronounce it. It was her ultimate condemnation. He had never heard her use it before. And now she used it for him!

“This is why you have been ailing all summer! You’d reason to be! Did you think you could do evil and prosper?”

He wasn’t going to stand any more of that tone. He got up.

===Chinese===

“那你到底为什么不想！”她厉声逼问，怒不可遏。“你没有自己的脑子吗！想必你是不知道自己在干什么了！哦，我竟养出这么个傻儿子来！”

做母亲的多么可怕呀！“傻”这个字是她恨之入骨的，她从不许自己的孩子们说出口。那是她至重的判词。他从前从没听她用过。如今她却用它来判他！

“怪不得你一夏天都恹恹的！你是有缘由的！你以为做了恶事还能兴旺吗？”

那种腔调他再也不打算忍受下去了。他站起身。

===Original===

“I’ll be going,” he exclaimed. “There’s no place for me here!” No sooner had he used those words than he regretted them. They might seem to appeal to her pity. That was what he had said once when he was a little lad, upon seeing a new baby in her arms, and afterwards, whenever she had shown him a new child, she had reminded him of it gaily.

“Don’t go!” she answered, unrelenting. “There is always a place for you, whatever you elect to do. This is a sore stroke, Wully!” Then she added, wearily and passionately,

“When I was a girl, I wanted to be some great person. And when you all were born, I wanted only to have you great men. And when you grew up, I prayed you might be at least honest. And I’m not to have even that, it seems.”

===Chinese===

“我走了，”他嚷道。“这里没有我的地方了！”话一出口他就后悔了。这话听着倒像是在求她可怜。他还是个小小子儿的时候，一见她怀里又多了个新生的孩子，就说过这么一句；打那以后，每逢她抱个新生的孩子给他看，她总要笑嘻嘻地把他这句话重提一遍。

“不许走！”她答道，半分不让。“不管你拣定要走什么路，这里永远有你的地方。这一下打得可真痛，伍利！”跟着她又添上一句，声音里又倦又烈，

“我做姑娘的时候，一心要做个大人物。等你们一个个生下来，我一心只想你们都成大人物。等你们长大了，我祷告你们至少做个诚实人。到头来，连这个我也得不上了，看来是这样。”

===Original===

He had heard her say that before. He was so sorry for her pain that he hardly knew what to do. If only there had been any other way out! Maybe Chirstie had been right in demanding he tell at least his mother the truth. But he would not! He would share his wife’s blame.

“I’m sorry about it, mother,” he pleaded. “I’m sick about it. I’ve done what I could to make it right!”

“To make it right! Do you think you can ever make wrong right! You have spoiled your own marriage. You’ll never be happy in it!”

“Don’t worry about that!”

===Chinese===

这话他从前听她说过。她的痛楚叫他难受得不知怎么才好。要是当初还有别的路可走就好了！也许克丽丝蒂要他至少把实话告诉他母亲，是要对了。可他不肯！他要同他的妻子分担这一份责难。

“这事我心里难受，妈，”他央告道。“我难受得像害了病。能叫这事归正的，我什么都做了！”

“归正！你以为错了的事就能归正成对的吗！你把自己的婚姻给毁了。你在里头再也不会有幸福！”

“这个不用你操心！”

===Original===

“And you the oldest!” she added, suddenly. “I suppose the other six will be doing the same, now!”

“If a brother of mine did a thing like that, I’d kill him!” cried Wully fiercely.

It soothed her to have something not tragic to reprove him for.

“Wully,” she said severely, “don’t you speak words like them here! ’Tis something you learned in the army! A fine one you’d be to say who should live and who should die! We dinna say the like here!”

===Chinese===

“你还是老大呢！”她忽然又添上一句。“我看往后另外六个都会照着学的！”

“我兄弟里头有谁干出这样的事来，我宰了他！”伍利凶狠狠地喊道。

有件不算悲惨的事好让她申斥他，这倒叫她心里舒缓了些。

“伍利，”她厉声说，“不许你在这里说这样的话！这是你在军队里学来的！好哇，你倒成了配说谁该活谁该死的人物了！这里可不说这样的话！”

===Original===

“I can’t please you anyway!” he cried, stung by her upbraidings.

“Strange ways you have of trying!” she retorted. He said nothing. She cried again, presently,

“If only it had been some other girl, Wully! Not Jeannie’s!”

What could he answer?

===Chinese===

“反正我横竖不能叫你称心！”他被她的数落刺得喊了起来。

“你讨好的法子倒也新鲜！”她回敬道。他没言语。过了一会儿她又嚷道，

“要是别的哪个姑娘也罢了，伍利！偏偏是珍妮家的！”

他能答什么呢？

===Original===

“Mother, you come and see her! She needs someone!”

“Thanks to you! To my son! I won’t can speak to her, that shamed I’ll be of you!” She thought a bitter moment. “Alex McNair’ll be home before December. You’d best come here to me! Wully, if any other mouth in the world had told me this, I wouldn’t have believed it! You were always a good boy. Always! Before the war!”

“I’ve got to go!” he cried in answer. He rushed away, damning Peter Keith into the nethermost hell. The open air was some relief. If only women wouldn’t take these things so hard! Well, that was over. The worst part. Any taunt that he might ever have to defend himself from would be easy, after that.

===Chinese===

“妈，你去看她吧！她需要有人照应！”

“多亏了你！亏你还是我的儿子！我是没法儿开口跟她说话了，你臊得我抬不起头来！”她苦楚地想了片刻。“亚历克斯·麦克奈尔（Alex McNair）十二月以前就到家了。你顶好上我这儿来！伍利，这世上要是换任何一张嘴告诉我这话，我才不信呢！你向来是个好孩子。向来是！打仗以前！”

“我非走不可！”他这样回答，冲了出去，把彼得·基思（Peter Keith）咒进了最底层的地狱。户外的空气叫他松泛了些。但愿女人家不把这类事看得这样重才好！好了，这一关算是过了。最难的一关。往后不管什么奚落要他招架，比起这个来都容易了。

===Original===

After her unkissed son had gone, Isobel McLaughlin, reeling from the blow he had dealt her, sat with her hands covering her face. Nothing but Wully’s own recital could ever have made her believe such a story! It was even thus incredible. If only it had been any other girl but Jeannie’s! And her dead! Scarcely dead, either, till her son, betraying years of trust, had shamed her daughter! If Jeannie had been alive, she would have gone to her, in humiliation, though it killed her! Now there was not even that comfort! There was only Chirstie left, and her in such a state! It was not possible to believe her good, beautiful son had done such a base thing! If it had been any boy but Wully! Had he ever given her a moment of anxiety before? Did not the whole clan like him, knowing him for a quiet, honorable, sweet-tempered boy, eminently trustworthy! And now a thing like this to fall upon her! She refused to remember that Allen’s irresponsibility, his extravagant pleasure in the society of women, of any size or kind of woman, had made her anxious many an hour. That son, from the time he was twelve, had fairly glowed when there was a woman about to admire him. But Wully had only chuckled over his brother’s kaleidoscopic love affairs, things so foreign to his nature. His mother, remembering Allen’s escapades, exempted the dead loyally from blame. If Wully had been like that, she might have understood this tale. But he was not like that. He had never been at all like that. It must be the army that had wrought such evil changes in him. That was what had undone her years of teaching. That was what had made all this frontier sacrifice barren. Was it not for the children’s sake they had endured this vast wilderness, and endured it in vain if the children were to be of this low and common sort? In their Utopia it was not to have been as it had been in the old country, with each family having a scholar or two in it, and the rest toilers. Here they were all to have been scholars and great men. And now the war had taken away Wully’s schooling and Allen’s life—and not only Wully’s schooling, which was after all, not essential to life, but that ultimate gift, his very sense of being a McLaughlin.

===Chinese===

她那没得着亲吻的儿子走了以后，伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林（Isobel McLaughlin）叫他这一击打得头昏眼花，双手蒙着脸坐着。不是伍利亲口招认，这样的事她死也不会信！即便如此，也还是叫人难以置信。若换成随便哪个别的姑娘都好，偏是珍妮家的！而且珍妮人已经故去了！说故去，也还没故去多久，她的儿子就背弃了多年的信托，把她女儿给羞辱了！珍妮若还活着，她就是拼了命也要忍辱到她跟前去一趟！如今连这点安慰都没有了！只剩下克丽丝蒂一个人，还是那样一副光景！她那好儿子、俊儿子竟做出这样下作的事，叫她怎么信！换成随便哪个小子都好，偏是伍利！他从前可曾叫她操过一刻的心？整个宗族谁不喜欢他？谁不知道他是个安静、正派、性子甜和的孩子，顶顶靠得住！如今偏偏这样一桩事落到她头上！她不肯记起，艾伦那副没肩架的性子，他在女人堆里——不管什么身量、什么路数的女人堆里——那份不知节制的快活，曾叫她操过多少个钟头的心。那个儿子，打十二岁起，只要有女人在跟前赏他的脸，他整个人就亮堂堂地放光。而伍利，对兄弟那万花筒似的恋爱把戏，不过是抿着嘴好笑——那些事同他的天性隔得多么远。做母亲的记着艾伦那些荒唐事，忠心耿耿地免了死人的责备。伍利若是那样的人，这个故事她兴许还懂得。可他不是那样。他从来全然不是那样。必定是军队在他身上作成了这样歹毒的变化。就是它毁了她多年的教养。就是它叫这一切边疆的牺牲荒芜了，结不出果子。他们不正是为了儿女的缘故才熬住这片莽莽荒野的吗？倘若儿女竟是这样低贱庸常的一路，那不就白熬了吗？在他们的乌托邦里，本不该再像旧国那样，一家子里出一两个读书人，其余都是受苦的人。在这里，他们本该个个都是读书人，都是大人物。如今战争夺走了伍利的学业、艾伦的性命——还不止伍利的学业，那说到底原算不得性命的根基——还有那件最终的馈赠：他作为一个麦克劳克林家的人、骨子里的那份自觉。

===Original===

Some Americans might have smiled to know that this immigrant family never for a moment considered Americans in general their equal, or themselves anything common. They were far too British for that. Until lately it had never occurred to them that anyone else might manage some way to be equal to a Scot. Until the war, when some young McLaughlin had shown signs of intolerable depravity, his father had entirely extinguished the last glimmer of it by saying, as he took his pipe out of his economical mouth, “Dinna ye act like a Yankee!” So withering was that reproach that no iniquity ever survived it. Now that that Yankee of the Yankees, Harvey Stowe, had been a very brother to Wully through campaigns and prisons, that denunciation was to be heard no more. But surely, Isobel McLaughlin moaned, her husband and herself had not let the children think that they were anything common. Had she not hated all that democracy that justified meanness of life, and pointed out faithfully to her children its fallacy? She remembered the first time she had taken them all to a Fourth of July celebration in the Yankee settlement, where a barefooted, tobacco-spitting, red-haired orator of the day, after an hour of boastings and of braggings, had shouted out his climax, saying that in this free land we are all kings and queens. “A fine old king, yon!” she had chuckled again and again, explaining his folly to her flock. A man like that had no idea what a king was! He most likely had never even seen a gentleman!

===Chinese===

若是知道这个移民人家从来没有一刻把美国人大众当作自己的对等，也从不把自己当作庸常之辈，有些美国人也许会发笑。他们是太不列颠了，断乎不肯。直到不久以前，他们从来不曾想到，别人居然也有法子变得同苏格兰人对等。直到打仗——家里哪个小麦克劳克林露出不可容忍的堕落苗头，他父亲就把烟斗从那张俭省的嘴里拿下来，说一句：“别学得像个北方佬！”这一声申斥真正有枯萎草木的威力，什么罪过也活不过它。如今，北方佬里的头号北方佬哈维·斯托（Harvey Stowe），经过行军，经过坐牢，同伍利处得亲兄弟一般，这顿斥责自然是再听不见了。可是，伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林呻吟着说，她和她丈夫总不至于让孩子们觉得自家是庸常之辈吧。那种为卑贱生活张目的民主，她不恨透了么？它的谬误，她不是忠忠实实地指给孩子们看过么？她记得头一回领他们全体去扬基人定居点看独立日（Fourth of July）庆典，当天那位演说家赤着脚，喷着烟草沫子，一头红发，吹嘘夸口了整整一个钟头，末了扯着嗓子喊出他的高潮，说什么在这片自由的土地上，我们人人都是国王和王后。“好一个老国王啊，那家伙！”她一次又一次地笑着，把她那窝孩子领着看他的糊涂。那样的人压根儿不知道国王是什么！他多半连一位绅士都没见过呢！

===Original===

She recalled that Wully, once when he was quite a small boy, had alone and unaided found and identified a gentleman whose team was struggling in a swamp. He was a poor old gentleman, trying manfully to get an orphan grandson to a son’s home farther west, and Wully had brought him proudly home, and his mother had “done” for him till he was able to travel on. Having him in the house had been like having a pitiable angel with them. When he was better, they had called all the neighbors in, and the old New Englander had preached them a sermon. He had preached to the children about the Lamb of God, using as his text the lamb tied near the door, and they had never forgotten how gentleness, he said, had made God great. And when he had been starting on, John McLaughlin had taken a bill from his pocket—and bills were things not often seen by the children—and given it to him humbly, for the benefits his presence had bestowed upon the family. Afterwards when his mother had asked Wully how he had known the stranger would be welcome, he had said he knew he was some great man by the way he spoke to his floundering horses. Oh, surely in that wilderness Wully had known the better ways of living. And he had chosen despicable ways! She was only an old, tired, disappointed woman.

===Chinese===

她记起伍利还是个小小子儿的时候，单枪匹马，无师自通，发现并且认出了一位绅士——他那队马正陷在沼泽里挣扎。那是位穷苦的老绅士，正拼着一股男子汉的气力，要把一个孤孙送到更西边一个儿子的家里去。伍利把他骄傲地带回家来，母亲把他当上宾侍候了一场，直到他能重新上路。家里有他在，就像家里住着一位叫人怜惜的天使。他好些了的时候，他们把四邻都请了来，那位老新英格兰人给他们布了一回道。他给孩子们讲上帝的羔羊，经文就用拴在门口的那只羊羔，孩子们从此忘不了他说过的那句话：温柔，使上帝成为伟大。到他动身的时候，约翰·麦克劳克林从口袋里掏出一张钞票——钞票这种东西，孩子们是难得见着的——恭恭敬敬地递给他，谢他这一场造访赐予全家的恩惠。过后母亲问伍利，怎么就知道那生客一定受欢迎，他说，凭那位先生同自己那两匹扑腾乱挣的马说话的样子，他就知道他准是个大人物。哦，在那片荒野里，伍利分明是识得更好的活法的。可他偏偏拣了卑贱的活法！她不过是个老了的、乏了的、失望了的女人。

===Original===

If her firstborn, that lad Wully, had done a thing like this, what might not the rest of them choose to do! Pride did not let her remember that if the family had been in no generation without a man of more or less eminence, neither had it been without a precedent for Wully’s conduct. She was a woman who had sympathy with the mother of Zebedee’s sons. If she had been there with Christ, she would have asked unashamed for four places on his right, and for four on his left, the nearest eight seats for her eight sons. What dreams she had dreamed for them! Once she had beheld the President of the United States consulting his cabinet, and behold, her Wully was the President, and Allen the Vice-President, and the Cabinet consisted of her younger lads, even young Hughie sitting there, still only nine, with a freckled little nose, and a wisp of a curling lock straying down from his cowlick towards eyes shining with contemplated mischief. She had felt at the time that such a dream might be somewhat, perhaps, foolish, and profiting by Joseph’s distant but well-known experience, she had told it only to her husband. He diagnosed her case in one instant. “You dreamed that wide awake, woman!” She had thought at times that Allen was to be another Burns, a maker of songs for a new country. In her dreams, to be great was to be one of three things, a Burns, a Lincoln, or a Florence Nightingale. And now one dream, her first and longest, was permanently over. Wully was a man now, and a man who brought women to ruin. Sometimes it seemed to her as she lay there moaning that surely the girl must have enticed him into this evil. Then she came swiftly to blaming the whole thing on Alex McNair. If he had come home when he should have, if he had not left the girl unprotected there, this would never have happened. Blaming Alex violated no fond loyalty. In time it came to seem to her that the whole fault was his.

===Chinese===

她的大儿子，那个小伙子伍利，既然干出了这样的事，其余的人还有什么拣不出来做的！自尊心不许她记起：这一家固然代代不缺一个多少有点名望的人物，却也代代不缺伍利这种行为的前例。她是个与西庇太（Zebedee）儿子的母亲心气相通的女人。她若当年也在基督跟前，她会毫不害臊地求他，右手边给她四个位子，左手边再给四个，把她八个儿子最近便的八个座位都要下来。她为他们做过多少梦啊！有一回她看见美国总统正同内阁商议大事，看哪，她的伍利就是总统，艾伦是副总统，内阁成员全是她的小儿子们，连小休伊也坐在那里，才九岁，雀斑的小鼻子，一绺鬈曲的头发从额前发涡里斜逸下来，直垂向一双眼睛，那双眼睛正闪着盘算恶作剧的光。她当时就觉得这梦也许有几分痴，有约瑟（Joseph）那桩年代虽远却尽人皆知的前车之鉴，她只讲给丈夫一个人听。他一眼就把她的病诊断了。“你是睁着眼睛做的这个梦，婆娘！”她有时想，艾伦该成为又一个彭斯（Burns），为一个新的国度做歌的人。在她的梦里，所谓大人物，不出三种：一个彭斯，一个林肯（Lincoln），或者一个弗洛伦斯·南丁格尔（Florence Nightingale）。如今有一个梦——她头一个、也是做得最久的一个——永远地完了。伍利如今是个男人了，是个把女人往毁灭里领的男人。她躺在那里呻吟的时候，有时倒觉得，那姑娘八成是勾引他堕入这桩罪恶的。跟着她又飞快地掉转头，把整桩事都归到亚历克斯·麦克奈尔头上。他若是应当回家的时候就回了家，若不是他把姑娘孤零零撇在那里，这事断乎不会发生。怪罪亚历克斯，并不有违她哪一份深挚的情爱。到后来，在她看来，整桩过错竟全成了他的。

===Original===

But that afternoon, the small McLaughlins coming home from school found a state of affairs new in their experience. There was absolutely no sign of a baby in the house, and yet their mother was in bed! Once she said when they asked her anxiously, that her head ached. And once she said that her heart was troubling her.

===Chinese===

可是那天下午，放学回来的麦克劳克林家的小的们，撞见了一桩他们阅历里从未有过的局面。家里压根儿没有一丝要有娃娃的动静，他们的母亲却躺在了床上！他们焦急地问过她，她有一回说，是头疼。又有一回说，是心里不好受。

## VIII / 第八章

===Original===

The autumn seemed to set itself against the house that Wully had determined to have ready for occupancy before winter. Week after week the roads continued so deep in mud that six oxen could not manage to haul a load of lumber the mere twenty-six miles. Chirstie was not as much disappointed by the delay as her husband; she rather liked being hidden away, just then, on the outskirts of the settlement, in her father’s lonely cabin. She had seen no one but Wully’s mother, and her aunts into whose chagrined ears the humbled Isobel McLaughlin had poured a story as sympathetic as possible, blaming Alex McNair for this fruit of his unfatherly desertion. Mrs. McLaughlin had come at once to see Chirstie after Wully’s revelation, apparently utterly pleased over the prospect of a grandchild, never intimating by a syllable that she saw anything deplorable in the unchristian haste of his advent. Her kindness had naturally humbled the girl more than any reproof could have done, and after a long cry the two had been friends, both relieved that estrangement was a thing of the past.

One afternoon late in November Mrs. McLaughlin came as far as Chirstie’s with her husband, who was going on to the Keiths’ on an errand. It seemed to Chirstie then, and often afterwards, that one who had not seen loving-kindness incarnate in her mother-in-law, had never seen it at all. Her own mother had been a sad, repressed woman, well-loved, indeed, by her children, but as far different as possible from this great, cordial, brimming woman, who seemed so capable of anything that might ever be required of her. One couldn’t imagine her hesitating, complaining, broken in spirit.

===Chinese===

这个秋天，像是存心跟伍利决意要在入冬前收拾停当的那座房子作对。一个星期接着一个星期，路上的泥始终深得六头牛也没法把一车木料拉完那区区二十六英里。对这样的耽搁，克丽丝蒂倒不像她丈夫那样失望；她反倒有点喜欢这时候躲起来，躲到定居点的边上，躲进她父亲那座冷清的小屋。她谁也没见过，只见过伍利的母亲，还有她的几位姑姨——丢尽了脸面的伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林，把事情尽量往体恤里讲给她们那懊恼的耳朵听，把这一切都怪到亚历克斯·麦克奈尔头上：正是他不尽为父之道、撇下家一走了之，才结出这么个果子。伍利把事情挑明之后，麦克劳克林太太立刻就来看克丽丝蒂，分明为快抱上孙子欢喜极了，半个字也不曾露出，说她觉得这孩子来得这样急、不合基督徒的规矩有什么可叹。她的好意自然比任何责备都更叫这姑娘羞愧；痛痛快快哭过一场之后，两个女人成了朋友，都庆幸隔阂已成往事。

十一月里的一天下午，麦克劳克林太太同丈夫一道来到克丽丝蒂这里；他还要赶去基思家办件事。克丽丝蒂那时就觉得，往后也常常觉得：谁要是在自己婆婆身上没见过慈爱化作血肉的模样，那就是压根儿没见过。她自己的母亲原是个忧郁、压抑的女人，儿女们自然也疼她，可同眼前这位高大、热诚、满溢的女人相比，简直不是一路人——这位婆婆，仿佛天下不管要她做什么，她都担得起。你想象不出她会迟疑，会抱怨，会灰心丧气。

===Original===

Chirstie sat beside her sewing, an awe-filled pupil in the things of maternity. It was comforting, when one was feeling daily more wretched, to be assured by the mother of thirteen huskies that a baby is just nothing whatever but a joy, no trouble worth speaking of. Did Chirstie remember that her brother Jimmie had been just Wully’s age? Many was the time Jeannie McNair and Isobel McLaughlin had sat together waiting for those two, and sewing, and Jeannie had said so-and-so, and Isobel had answered thus-and-thus. Once she had said to Chirstie’s grandmother that she wouldn’t like to have just a common bairn, and the old woman had replied that there was not the least chance of it, for no woman yet had mothered just a common child. In Scotland, too, when a baby was born, one had to lose the flavor of joy wondering where its food was to come from. But in this land crying aloud to the heavens for inhabitants, there was no anxiety of that sort to dull one’s happiness. What had it been to them but an omen of the new home’s abundance, that the John McLaughlins had had twins born the year of their arrival, that the Squires had had twins within six months, and that before the year was gone, the Weirs, from the same Ayrshire village, were also blessed in the same way. To be sure, Squire McLaughlin had uttered a word which might not have been taken to signify altogether pure satisfaction with these godsends, the morning after the double increase in his family. He had gone to his barn, and finding that his dearly-bought cow, which was to have furnished him milkers, had given birth to twins, he had sighed a sigh which became a tradition, and murmured, “Bull calves, and lassie wee’uns!” The men had laughed at that, but the women considered it a rather cheap thing of the old wag, even as a joke.

And so they talked on, until the clouds covered the sun again, and they heard the wind rising noisily as they drew near the fire to consider their knitting in the light of it. The elder Mrs. McLaughlin, who was, as usual, doing most of the talking, looked enviously around the kitchen from time to time. She knew she was considered a capable woman. And she had a fine family﻿—yes, certainly, a fine family﻿—in spite of this﻿—affair of Wully’s. But she could never keep house as Jeannie did, or even Chirstie. She could, of course, polish her kitchen to some such a degree of luster for special occasions, but to maintain such a brightness was out of the question for her. There had been no white sheets on the wall here for some time now. But each little pane in the window glowed from its daily polishing. The bits of rag carpet seemed always scarcely yet to have lost the marks of their folding, so recently had they been spread down after washing. Even the fireplace was more kept than any other fireplace. The back of it had always just been scraped and scrubbed and whitewashed. Isobel wondered if her son realized the degree of this beautiful neatness.

===Chinese===

克丽丝蒂坐在针线旁边，像个满心敬畏的学生，学习着做母亲这门学问里的种种事情。一个人一天比一天觉得狼狈的时候，听一位拉扯大十三个壮实儿女的母亲向你担保，说娃娃根本算不得什么，就是个欢喜，是不值一提的麻烦，这实在宽心。克丽丝蒂还记不记得，她兄弟吉米本来正跟伍利同岁？珍妮·麦克奈尔和伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林不知有多少回坐在一处，等着那两个孩子出世，一面做针线，珍妮说这个那个，伊莎贝尔答这样那样。有一回珍妮对克丽丝蒂的祖母说，她可不愿意生个平平常常的娃娃，老太太回答她说，那是一点儿指望也没有的，因为还从来没有哪个女人养活过平平常常的孩子。在苏格兰，娃娃落了地，人也得先愁他往后的口粮打哪儿来，欢喜的味道就淡了。可在这片向苍天大声呼喊着要人烟的土地上，没有那种愁闷来败人的兴致。约翰·麦克劳克林家落脚那年就得了双胞胎，乡绅（Squire）家不出半年也得了双胞胎，还没等一年过完，同一个艾尔郡村子里来的韦尔（Weir）家也照样得了这样的福——这对他们，除了是新家业兴旺的好兆头，还能是什么呢？自然，乡绅麦克劳克林也说过一句什么话，听那话，你未必敢说他对这些上天赐下的福分全然是纯粹的欢喜——那是在他家里一下添了双份人口的第二天早上。他走到谷仓去，见他那头花大价钱买来、指望给他添几头奶牛的母牛也下了双胞胎，便叹了一口气——那声叹气从此传为典故——咕哝道：“公牛犊子，外加丫头小娃！”男人们听了直笑，女人们却觉得，这位爱逗趣的老爷子说出这话来未免小气了些，就算当玩笑听也小气。

两人就这样说着话，直到云又把太阳遮住；她们挨近火边，就着火光看手里的针线，听见风声呼呼地起来了。老麦克劳克林太太照例是说话最多的那个，一面说，一面不时艳羡地打量这间厨房。她知道自己算是人人夸的能干女人。她也有一个好人家——是啊，当然，好人家——尽管有伍利这桩——那档子事。可她持家永远赶不上珍妮，甚至赶不上克丽丝蒂。逢上特别的日子，她自然也能把自己的厨房擦出几分这样的光亮，可要长年累月保持这样的明净，她是断断做不到的。这屋里墙上，已经好些时候没有白布单了。可窗子上每一小块玻璃都亮得放光，全靠天天擦。那几块拼布地毯，看上去总像才拆了折叠不多久——洗过之后铺下去，还没有几天呢。连壁炉也比谁家的壁炉都拾掇得勤。炉膛后壁永远是刚刮过、刚刷过、刚粉刷过的。伊莎贝尔心里纳闷，不知她儿子明白不明白，这份好看的整洁到了怎样的地步。

===Original===

After a while they heard a wagon drive in, and Mrs. McLaughlin, thinking it was her husband, rose and began leisurely wrapping her knitting. There was no hurry about going. Her man had best come in and warm himself. She stood buttoning her old gray faded coat about her. It had been made, mantle-fashion, in Scotland, before she had grown so large, and she had increased its capacity by the simple device of putting broad black strips of cloth down either side of the front, where it fastened. Afterwards it had needed new sleeves, and hadn’t apparently sulked about having new ones of a brownish gray homespun woolen. It had nothing to sulk about, in fact. It was still given plenty of honor as a good serviceable garment. Mistress McLaughlin was wrapping round and round her throat a knitted scarf, pulling it carefully up around her ears, when the door opened.﻿ ﻿…

And in walked﻿—not John McLaughlin, but that tall, gaunt, thin-faced Alex McNair! With those little round, black, piercing eyes shining out from under straight black brows!﻿ ﻿…

And after him, a woman!

A woman in olive-green silk, with black fringe around a puffy overskirt, and such fur and gloves as Isobel McLaughlin had seen only in her travels, and Chirstie never remembered seeing in all her life! The two of them! Coming right into the room!

===Chinese===

过了一阵，她们听见一辆大车进了院子。麦克劳克林太太当是她丈夫回来了，站起身，不慌不忙地收起针线活。走，用不着忙。她男人顶好进来暖和暖和身子。她站在那儿，扣着她那件褪了色的旧灰大衣。那大衣是当年在苏格兰照斗篷的样式做的，那时她还没有发福到如今这般；后来她用了顶简单的法子把它放宽——在前襟系扣子的两边，各缝上一条宽宽的黑布。再后来它又添了新袖子，换了棕灰色家织毛呢的新袖子，它似乎也不曾为此闹过别扭。其实它也没什么可闹别扭的。作为一件又好又顶用的衣裳，它至今照旧备受优待。麦克劳克林太太正拿一条毛线围巾一圈一圈裹上脖子，小心地拉上去掩住耳朵，门开了。……

进来的却——不是约翰·麦克劳克林，而是那个高挑精瘦、一脸皮包骨的亚历克斯·麦克奈尔！一双又小又圆的黑眼睛，锐利逼人，在两道笔直的黑眉毛底下灼灼放光！……

他身后，还跟着一个女人！

一个女人，穿一身橄榄绿绸衣，蓬起的罩裙四围镶着黑流苏，那样的皮毛和手套，伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林只在自己出门远行时见过，克丽丝蒂更是活了半辈子，记都不记得见过！他们两个！就这么一直进了屋子！

===Original===

McNair, seeing Isobel standing there, cried, blinking,

“Weel, weel! You here, Isobel! Weel, weel! This is Barbara, Isobel!”

Chirstie had shrunk in fear and confusion, back into her seat. But the elder woman showed no signs of confusion. She looked the grand wee body over majestically and replied:

“Is’t, indeed! I hope she fares better than Jeannie, Alex, dying here alone.”

Alex had bent down to kiss his daughter, and seemed to be not so much impressed by this greeting as the little woman was. She continued:

===Chinese===

麦克奈尔看见伊莎贝尔站在那儿，眨着眼睛喊道，

“哎哟，哎哟！你在这儿，伊莎贝尔！哎哟，哎哟！这位是芭芭拉（Barbara），伊莎贝尔！”

克丽丝蒂又怕又乱，早缩回自己的座位上去了。可那位上了年纪的女人没有半点慌乱的形迹。她威风凛凛地把这位华贵的小妇人从头到脚打量了一遍，答道：

“是嘛，当真！但愿她的日子过得比珍妮好些，亚历克斯——珍妮可是孤零零死在这儿的。”

亚历克斯俯下身去亲他的女儿；对这句见面话，他的触动似乎不似那位小妇人那样深。她接着说：

===Original===

“I have just been sitting a while with my son’s wife. You may not remember Chirstie was married, you having so grand a time in Scotland!”

“Warm yourself!” he said to his wife, indicating a chair. “I’ll be bringing in the kist.” He went out of the door, which had not yet been shut, so suddenly and quickly had it all happened. Mrs. McLaughlin’s manner changed at once, and she began helping the amazing stranger out of her wraps. How could those two who watched, so impressed by the richness of them, and so unbetraying of their impressions, how could they have imagined, seeing her, the deceitfulness of those little innocent hesitating airs! The garments were scarcely laid gingerly on the bed until Alex returned, carrying, with Bob McNorkel’s help, a great box, which they seemed to plan to leave in the middle of the floor. Chirstie remonstrated and gave them directions. It seemed from Alex’s grunting and hard-breathing words as the box was put in the only possible place for it, that he and his bride had ridden out with Bob, who had to be hurrying on. Alex went out of the door with him, and after Alex, Isobel the avenger.

===Chinese===

“我方才正陪我儿媳妇坐了一会儿。你兴许不记得克丽丝蒂已经成亲了吧——你在苏格兰不是正风光着嘛！”

“烤烤火吧！”他对自己的女人说，指了指一把椅子。“我把大木箱（kist）搬进来。”门还没来得及关上——这一切来得就是这样突然、这样快——他已经出了门。麦克劳克林太太的神气立刻变了，动手帮这位叫人吃惊的陌生客卸披裹。旁观的两个女人，叫那身衣物的华贵震住，又都丝毫不露声色——她们看着她，哪里想得到，那一副怯生生、拿不定主意的天真小样儿，竟是这样靠不住！那些衣物还没小心翼翼地在床上安放停当，亚历克斯就回来了，由鲍勃·麦克诺克尔（Bob McNorkel）帮着，抬着好大一口箱子，看那样子，是打算就撂在屋子正当间。克丽丝蒂连连拦阻，指点他们该搁在哪儿。从亚历克斯哼哧哼哧、喘着粗气挤出来的话里听得出来，他和他的新娘是搭鲍勃的车出来的，鲍勃还得赶路。亚历克斯跟着他出了门；亚历克斯之后，是报仇的伊莎贝尔。

===Original===

“I’ll just have a word with you!” she said to him, stepping inside the barn to be out of the wind. It was a powerful word. Had she not planned it many a night as she lay sleepless thinking of Jeannie and her daughter! “I mind the day you brought Jeannie home a bride,” she began. “ ’Twas no day like this.” None of them would ever forget the day she died deserted. Never had Isobel McLaughlin had an occasion worthier of her tongue, and never a stronger motive for making the best of the occasion. McNair was a slow-moving, slow-thinking man, not without tenderness. Isobel’s recital of grim detail after grim detail as he stood there amazed, remorseful, humiliated, angry, tired of his journey, and chilled to the bone, overwhelmed him. He could scarcely follow her. It seemed that the whole clan was bitter against him, not only because of his wife’s death, but because, some way, his absence had brought disgrace beyond disgrace upon the McLaughlins. He could scarcely understand. Wully and Chirstie had waited and waited for him to come home, and he *would* not, and fine results these were of his delay! They were married now, but not soon enough.﻿ ﻿… The girl feared to marry without his permission.﻿ ﻿… If he had only come when they wrote for him to.﻿ ﻿… He wasn’t to blame the Keiths or any of the neighbors for this. They had done what they could. He was to be very careful what he said to Wully, none too pleased with him, and always hotheaded﻿ ﻿… and to Chirstie.﻿ ﻿… It was all his own fault, he was to remember.﻿ ﻿…

The man was staggered. He liked this news all the less because all the day the little new wife’s spirits had been sinking as they traveled over the prairies away from the world. Now to bring her into a disgrace of this sort! He was shivering. He wanted to get in to the fire.

“I have nothing against Wully!” he murmured to the woman who bearded him. “He’s a fine man for the lassie!”

===Chinese===

“我跟你说句话！”她对他说，一面走进谷仓里去避风。这话厉害得很。多少个夜里，她想着珍妮和她闺女，躺着睡不着，这番话岂不早在心里备下了！“你把珍妮娶进门那天，我还记得呢，”她开口道。“那天可不像今天这样的天。”她叫丈夫撇下、孤零零死去的那一天，这一家人谁也忘不了。伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林活了半辈子，还不曾有过一回更配她那片舌头的场合，也不曾有过更足的理由，要把这场合用个十足。麦克奈尔是个行动迟缓、心思也迟缓的人，却不是没有温厚之情。他站在那儿，惊愕、悔恨、羞惭、恼怒，旅途乏透了，寒气冻透了骨头；伊莎贝尔一桩接着一桩把严酷的细节数下来，直把他压垮了。他几乎跟不上她的话头。听着意思是，整个宗族都对他恨透了，不但为他妻子的死，还因为他这一走，不知怎么，竟给麦克劳克林家带来了一重又一重的耻辱。他简直弄不明白。伍利和克丽丝蒂等他回家，等了又等，他偏不回来，他这一耽搁，收场就是这么好的收场！两人如今是成了亲，可成亲成得不够快。……姑娘没有他许可，不敢嫁人。……当初他们去信叫他回来，他要在那时候回来，该多好。……这件事，基思家也好，哪个街坊也好，都怪不着人家，人家能做的都做尽了。他对伍利说话千万要小心，伍利本就对他没有多少好气，又是火爆性子……对克丽丝蒂也要小心。……横竖都是他自己的不是，这一点他要记牢。……

这汉子叫这一下打懵了。这消息叫他添堵，还因为一路上他们赶着车穿过草原、越来越离开人烟，他那小新娘的兴致就一路往下沉。这会儿再把人家领进这样一桩丢脸事里！他直打哆嗦。他想到火边上去。

“我对伍利没有二话！”他朝这个当面捋他胡子的女人咕哝道。“这小伙子配那丫头，好得很！”

===Original===

Nevertheless, when they were inside again, Isobel watching saw his face darken with anger as he realized Chirstie’s condition. She saw too that the girl had seen it, and she determined not to leave the house till Wully would come. She busied herself to make tea for the strange woman, sparing her daughter-in-law with the consideration which so beautiful and so fruitful a woman deserved. She sat herself to make the wee body feel at home. Dod came in from school, and she noticed without relenting the warmth of his father’s greeting. Even the little lassie was persuaded to go to his lap. Alex was probably wishing Isobel would go home and leave his family in peace. But she would wait.

McNair was telling something about the passage across when Wully opened the door. He paused a moment, seeing the room full. He looked at them in surprise, and they looked at him with various degrees of admiration. He came from cutting and hauling home wood for the winter and the wind had made his cheeks as red as the fringe of the scarf around his neck, and his eyes as blue as the knit wool of it. In the old coat wrapped about him, he filled the door, a huge young man one would not like for an enemy. His mother had just begun to tell the strange woman that this was her son, when Alex rose and stretched out his hand.

“Come away, man! Come away!” he cried cordially. It was not the kind of meeting Wully had anticipated. But what could he do, with his mother and the women right there, but acknowledge the little woman’s salutation, and give his hand to Chirstie’s father? And taking his cue from his mother, he smiled so warmly down upon the wee body, that then and there she began liking her stepson-in-law. His mother began at once giving him instructions. He and Chirstie had best begin packing their things. His father would be along any minute now, and they would all go home together. Wully would no longer be needed at McNair’s, and with all that work to be done on his own house﻿—

===Chinese===

话虽如此，他们再进屋的时候，伊莎贝尔在旁边看着，见他明白过来克丽丝蒂的情形，脸上的怒气黑了下来。她也看见这情形叫姑娘瞧在眼里，便打定主意，伍利不回来她就不离开这个家。她张罗着给那位陌生女人烧茶，把儿媳妇撇开让她歇着——这样标致、这样能生养的女人，原该受这份体贴。她自己坐下来陪那位小妇人，叫她不认生。多德放学回来了，他父亲招呼他那份热乎劲儿，她也看在眼里，心里毫不松口。连那个小丫头也给哄着坐到了他膝盖上。亚历克斯八成正巴望伊莎贝尔回家去，好让他一家清静。可她偏要等。

麦克奈尔正讲着渡海过来的种种，伍利推门进来了。见屋里满是人，他顿了一顿。他诧异地望着他们，他们则带着深深浅浅的赞赏望着他。他是刚砍了柴、往家拉冬柴回来的，风把他的两颊吹得跟脖子上围巾的流苏一样红，把他的眼睛衬得跟围巾的绒线一样蓝。旧大衣裹在身上，他堵满了整个门框，好一个魁伟的年轻人，谁也不愿意拿他当对头。他母亲刚要开口告诉那位陌生女人这是她的儿子，亚历克斯已经站起身，伸出了手。

“来吧，伙计！来吧！”他亲热地喊道。这样的会面，伍利可没有料到。可母亲和一屋子女眷都在跟前，他除了回一回那位小妇人的礼，同克丽丝蒂的父亲握一握手，还能怎样呢？他瞧着母亲的样子行事，朝那小妇人笑得那样热络，当场就叫她喜欢起这个继女婿来。他母亲立刻开始给他下指示。他和克丽丝蒂最好这就动手收拾东西。他父亲眼下一会儿就到，他们要一齐回家。麦克奈尔家用不着他了，再说他自己房子上还有那么些活等着干——

===Original===

McNair interrupted her decidedly,

“Huts, Isobel! Ye canna take Chirstie away the night!” One would almost think she was the McLaughlins’ daughter to hear Isobel! That manipulator of events smothered the retort that came to her, upon this. She simply enlarged innocently upon the inconvenience of Wully’s having to ride every day from this place to his own, such a distance. McNair could understand that, but nevertheless they weren’t going one step tonight. Wully winked slyly at his wife. He didn’t know exactly how his mother had worked it all, but it did him good to hear his father-in-law begging for the privilege of his company for a while﻿—that man he had expected to have such a time with! Isobel yielded gracefully at length. They might stay the night with Alex, but they mustn’t stay longer. With her big girls both away at school, she was that lonely for Chirstie!

Then the elder McLaughlin came in and the greetings were all gone over again, with this difference, that John McLaughlin, being less quick at taking hints from his wife than his son had been, showed just enough coldness to McNair to let him see that Isobel’s account of the clan’s opinion of him was not exaggerated. Naturally after the worthy McLaughlins had departed with so little of the old cordiality, Alex was more eager than ever to placate Wully, who, divining that Chirstie dreaded her father’s outburst against her, stood very much upon his dignity, a rather forbidding son-in-law.

===Chinese===

麦克奈尔斩钉截铁地打断了她，

“得了，伊莎贝尔！你今晚不能把克丽丝蒂带走！”听伊莎贝尔这样说话，人家几乎要当克丽丝蒂才是麦克劳克林家的闺女呢！这位摆布世情的老手，把涌到嘴边的回嘴硬生生咽了下去，只装无辜，细说伍利天天从这儿往自己家跑有多么不便，路那么远。麦克奈尔懂这个道理，可不管怎样，今晚他们一步也不挪。伍利朝他媳妇偷偷挤了挤眼。他母亲究竟怎样把事情摆布成这样，他不大清楚；可听着岳父开口求他留下作伴——他原以为要跟这人费多大周折呢！——心里着实受用。伊莎贝尔末后有风度地让了步。他们可以在亚历克斯这儿过一夜，可不许多待。她两个大闺女都在外头念书，她想克丽丝蒂想得那个苦哇！

随后老麦克劳克林也进了门，见面的礼数又全走过一遍；不同的是，约翰·麦克劳克林领会上妻子的眼色，没有他儿子那样快，对麦克奈尔流露出的那点儿冷淡，刚刚够叫他明白，伊莎贝尔说的族里人对他的看法，并不夸张。那可敬的麦克劳克林一家动身走了，拿出来的旧日热乎气竟这样少；这以后，亚历克斯自然越发急于讨好伍利；而伍利猜得到克丽丝蒂怕她父亲冲她发作，便十足端起了架子，一个颇叫人望而生畏的女婿。

===Original===

When the young two were alone in the kitchen that night, Chirstie said, weary with the day’s excitement, and her first taste of shame before strangers;

“Whatever’ll she say in the morning, when you’re not here, Wully?”

He answered;

“What do you care what she says? Anyway, she don’t look like she’d say anything. Just you hold your head high, and she won’t dare!”

“It’s well enough for you to talk of holding your head high! But how can I?”

“I’ll stay about in the morning, and in the afternoon we’ll go home. I’ll say we must go.”

===Chinese===

那天夜里，两个年轻人单独待在厨房里，克丽丝蒂说。白天的紧张折腾，加上头一回在人跟前尝到羞臊的滋味，她累极了；

“明儿早上你不在跟前，她该说什么呀，伍利？”

他答道；

“她说什么碍你什么事？再说了，瞧她那个样儿，也不像敢说什么的。你只管把头抬得高高的，她就不敢！”

“你说把头抬得高高的，说得倒轻巧！可我怎么做得到？”

“早上我在家守着，下午我们就回家。我就说我们非走不可。”

===Original===

So they planned, little knowing how useless it was to fear the wee body. In the next room, she was saying to her husband;

“Ye never telt me you lived in a sty!”

“Huts, woman! ’Tis no sty!”

“And I thinking you like a laird, with so many fine acres!”

“It’s a new country!”

“It’s an old sty!” Had she not from the train seen many a little snug place among comforting hills, livable little places! But that had been, to be sure, far from this, in the east. The further west they came, the more they traveled into desolation. Lonely enough places she had seen, but none so unpromising as this sty. Could it be expected that a man with so disconsolate a bride would add to her woe by rehearsing the fresh scandal of the family into which she had come? She remarked at length that it was a terrible thing for a lassie with the baby coming. Why had he not told her of that before? He hadn’t remembered to. It was a fine place for bairns. Just let her wait till the spring came. She remarked that it was many months till spring. He snored, more or less successfully.

===Chinese===

他们这样商量着，哪晓得怕那位小妇人，怕得多么白搭。隔壁屋里，她正对她丈夫说；

“你从来没跟我讲过，你住的是个猪圈！”

“得了，婆娘！这哪里是猪圈！”

“我还当你是位大老爷（laird）呢，有那么多好田！”

“这是个新开辟的地方嘛！”

“这可是个老猪圈！”坐火车来的一路上，她见过多少处叫人安心的丘岭间的小巧去处，住得下人的小地方！可那自然离这儿远得很，都在东边。越往西走，越是一路走进荒凉里。冷清的地方她也见过，可没有一个像这个猪圈这样叫人提不起半点指望。娶了这样一位愁苦的新娘，难道还能指望她男人再把她进门这户人家新闹出的丑闻数给她听，往她的愁苦上头再添愁苦吗？临了她终于开了口：姑娘家眼见着孩子要落地，这可是件怕人的事。他先前为什么不跟她说明白？他没想起来说。这地方养娃娃顶好不过了。只叫她等着开春就是了。她又说了，离开春还有好几个月呢。他打起鼾来，多少总算打成了。

===Original===

The next morning the new mother unpacked the great kist to get out the presents she had brought for her stepchildren. She unpacked till the poor room lay heaped high and hidden under richness. Wee Jeannie had a fine doll. Dod had fur-lined mittens. Chirstie had a collar of lace more soft and fine than she had ever seen. And the wee body presented these things with that timid, conciliatory air that made her career later so hard to understand. She apologized for having nothing for the baby. If she had known about that, she would have brought it something good. When was it to be born, she asked, point-blank.

Chirstie, blushing to the unruly little curls about her forehead, said in December. This seemed to relieve her stepmother greatly. By that time, she declared, she could make a fine little dress for it, out of stuff she had in another box. Another box! Were there then other boxes? Of course brides bring dowries to their husbands, the girl remembered with a pang. But she had brought hers only disgrace! But the wee body talked on, in a kindly way. Chirstie watched her making friends with little Jeannie. She liked her, very much. That woman could never be anything but kind to the little sister who was to be left in her charge. Oh, Chirstie could have coveted that woman’s love for herself. But, of course, when the truth about herself became known﻿—and when she thought of going to the McLaughlins, to live in that house, full always of children and cousins and visitors, the center, as it were, and rallying place of the neighborhood, her spirits sank lower and lower.

===Chinese===

第二天早上，新来的母亲打开那口大木箱，取出她带给继子继女们的礼物。她一件一件往外拿，直拿到这间寒酸的屋子叫这些华美的东西堆得老高、盖得看不见了。小珍妮得了一个好洋娃娃。多德得了毛皮里子的连指手套。克丽丝蒂得了一圈蕾丝领子，又软又细，她从没见过这样的东西。那位小妇人把礼物递出来的时候，带着那副怯生生的、一味求和的派头——她日后的为人处世所以叫人那样想不明白，毛病就在这副派头上。她为没给宝宝带东西道歉。早知道有这回事，她原会给它捎样好东西来的。孩子什么时候生？她开门见山地问。

克丽丝蒂红了脸，一直红到额前那些不服帖的小鬈发，说是十二月。这话似乎叫继母大大松了口气。到那时候，她宣称，她就能给它做一件漂亮的小衣裳，用另一口箱子里她带的料子。另一口箱子！这么说还有别的箱子？做新娘的原该给丈夫带来嫁妆——姑娘心里一疼，想起来了。可她带来的嫁妆，只有一桩耻辱！但那位小妇人还在和和气气地说下去。克丽丝蒂看着她跟小珍妮交朋友。她喜欢她，喜欢得很。对那个要留下归她照看的小妹妹，这女人是决计只有疼的。唉，克丽丝蒂真恨不得把那女人的疼爱占为己有。可是，自然，等她自己那桩事的底细一传开——再想到要去麦克劳克林家，住进那座总是挤满孩子、表亲、客人的房子，住进那仿佛是左近乡邻的中心和聚合之地，她的心气就一落千丈，越来越低。

===Original===

Wully had learned before now to conquer her depression, and he talked the cold hours cunningly away as they rode towards his father’s. His reward, that evening, was to see his wife sitting there at the table, long after the meal was over, forgetful of herself, telling his ejaculating mother of the dresses, the capes, the mantles, the ribbons and feathers, reds and browns and greens and blues, puffs and ruffles and tucks, all of these out of one box, and besides the one there were three others left at the station to be brought out, full of﻿—whatever did they suppose? They couldn’t imagine! Isobel was trying to fancy how Alex had enticed a woman so obviously rich to the wilderness. She was disappointed in this marriage. She had hoped when Alex married again, he would get a woman who would show him how to treat a wife. But that timid, wee body! Meek like! With faded red hair, and mild light-blue eyes! There would be no hope of her ever separating him from the price of a milk-crock! Anyone could see that. The poor wee thing, married to Alex McNair!

===Chinese===

伍利早就学会了怎么治她的意气消沉，往他父亲家去的路上，他把一路寒冷的钟点，都用话头巧妙地打发掉了。他那晚的报偿，是看见自己的妻子饭后在桌边坐了许久许久，把什么都忘了，正讲给他那连连惊呼的母亲听：那些连衫裙、披肩、斗篷，那些缎带和羽饰，红的、棕的、绿的、蓝的，泡泡的、荷叶边的、细褶的，统共只是一口箱子里的；那一口之外，还有三口撂在车站上没拿出来，装得满满的——装着什么呢？你们猜猜看？她们猜不着！伊莎贝尔竭力想象，亚历克斯是怎样把一个分明阔气的女人哄到这荒天野地里来的。这门婚事叫她失望。她原指望，亚历克斯再娶时能娶到一个女人，教他知道该怎样待妻子。可这么个胆小的小妇人！绵羊似的！红头发褪了色，一双温和的浅蓝眼睛！要指望她有朝一日叫亚历克斯为一只奶缸的钱松一松手，那是没有指望的！谁看了都明白。可怜的小东西，嫁给了亚历克斯·麦克奈尔！

## X / 第十章

===Original===

Barbara McNair had watched Wully and Chirstie driving away towards Wully’s home that afternoon after her arrival at the sty in the slough. It was raining then, and it rained for nearly six weeks. She stood looking after them till they were out of sight. Then she went to the other little window. There she shut her lips tightly—regarded what her eyes discovered, two bony cows, shivering, it seemed to her, in the blown rain, trying to find shelter from the wind by huddling against the haystack that was one side of the barn. The rain was gray and sullen, the prairies sodden and brown; the cows had trampled the ground between the house and the barn into mud, into which they sank knee deep. She stood contemplating. The rain continued blowing about in imprisoning drab veils. Finally she turned away, and sat down weakly. From where she sat, she saw the dripping cows shivering. She sat huddled down. She seemed trying to cuddle up against herself. Her hands, folded in her lap, seemed the only sight not terrifying that her eyes might consider.

Presently the silence of the room was broken with a little sob. She looked up. Chirstie’s little sister, standing near the window, was just turning away from it. She had been trying to see something of Chirstie. She felt deserted. Big tears were running slowly down her face. She looked like a neglected, ragged, little heartbroken waif.

Barbara started from her chair. That moment her face showed she had forgotten the surrounding desolations. She ran and gathered the child into her arms. She sat down with her in her lap. The little Jeannie, finding herself caressed, began crying lustily. The new mother kissed her. She caressed her. She soothed her, coaxing her into quietness. She told her little stories. She sang little songs, examining thoughtfully the poor little garments she wore. Dusk came upon them as they sat consoling one another. Barbara demanded help then of the child. Jeannie must show her where all the things were kept which were needed for the supper. They would make some little cakes together. Jeannie grew important and happy.

===Chinese===

芭芭拉·麦克奈尔（Barbara McNair）那天下午刚到洼地里这座猪圈，就望着伍利（Wully）和克丽丝蒂（Chirstie）赶着车，往伍利家去了。那时候正下着雨，这雨一落就落了差不多六个星期。她站在那儿目送他们，直到望不见了才罢。随后她走到另一扇小窗前。在那儿，她紧紧抿住了嘴唇——端详她眼里发现的东西：两头瘦骨嶙峋的母牛，在她看来正在斜风斜雨里哆嗦，紧紧偎着谷仓一侧的干草堆，想躲一点风。雨是灰的，阴沉得怕人；草原湿透了，一片棕褐；房子和谷仓之间的地，叫牛踩成了一摊烂泥，一脚踏下去就没到膝盖。她站着出神。雨还裹在一幅幅困人的灰褐色帷幕里随风翻卷。她终于转过身，软软地坐了下来。打她坐的地方望出去，看得见那两头滴着水的牛还在哆嗦。她蜷缩着坐下，仿佛要把自己往自己怀里偎。她那双交叠在膝上的手，是她的眼睛所能找到的唯一样看了不叫人害怕的东西。

不大一会儿，屋里的寂静让一声小小的抽泣打破了。她抬起头来。克丽丝蒂的小妹妹正站在窗边，刚从窗口转过身去。她方才一直想望一望克丽丝蒂的影儿。她觉得给撇下了。大颗大颗的泪珠正顺着她的脸慢慢往下淌。她那模样，活像个没人疼、衣衫褴褛、心都碎了的小流浪儿。

芭芭拉从椅子上一下子站起来。那一刻她脸上的神色表明，四外的荒凉她已经忘得一干二净。她跑过去，把孩子搂进怀里，又坐下，让孩子坐在膝头。小珍妮（Jeannie）发觉自己得了疼爱，放声大哭起来。新妈妈吻她，抚爱她，哄她安静下来。她给她讲小故事，唱小曲儿，一面若有所思地细看她身上那几件寒碜的小衣裳。两人坐着彼此安慰，暮色渐渐罩下来。这时芭芭拉才向孩子讨帮手。珍妮得指给她看，做晚饭要用的东西都收在哪儿。她们还要一块儿做几块小糕饼呢。珍妮顿时觉得自己了不得，快活起来。

===Original===

Dod’s eyes fairly bulged with amazement when he saw that supper table. Nothing of the sort had been set before him in that kitchen. His new mother made no apologies. She had been thinking to herself that it had been food of the most primitive sort that had been set before her by Chirstie on the three occasions upon which they had sat down to eat since she had arrived; doubtless Chirstie wasn’t feeling very well, and she was at best but a young housekeeper, whose omissions one could easily overlook. Barbara was pleased with what she had managed to prepare on the strange stove and in the newfangled oven. She saw her husband scowling at the table.

“I dinna like so many cakes!” he remarked severely. One must begin with these women at once, he seemed to be thinking. He had forgotten apparently that his bride came from the very land of cakes, though he wasn’t to be allowed to forget it often in the future.

She said apologetically;

“They’re not so good, I doubt. I couldn’t find any currants in the house. When we get currants you’ll like them fine.”

“There’s too much in them now!” he declared bravely. “We don’t have cake every day.”

“I do,” she said placidly. “I like a wee cake with my tea.”

===Chinese===

多德（Dod）看见那张晚饭桌子时，眼睛简直惊得要突出来。那间厨房里，还从来没有在他面前摆过这样的席面。新妈妈一句表示歉意的话也没有。她心里一直在盘算：她来了以后，同桌坐下来吃过三回饭，克丽丝蒂端到她面前的都是最简陋的吃食；想是克丽丝蒂身子不太爽利，再说她至多也还是个年轻的新手管家娘，有什么疏漏，人家容易担待。芭芭拉对自己在那只生疏的炉子上、新式的烤箱里张罗出来的这一桌，颇为得意。她看见丈夫在桌子那边沉着脸。

“这么些糕饼，我可不喜欢！”他板起脸说。对这些女人，就得一开始就立下规矩——他心里像是在这样想。他显然忘了，他的新娘正是从糕饼之乡来的；虽说往后，人家也不会容他常常忘记这一点。

她道歉似的说；

“怕是不顶好。家里葡萄干没寻着。等置办上了葡萄干，你就爱吃了。”

“如今里头搁的东西就已经太多啦！”他壮着胆子宣称。“我们家又不是天天吃糕饼。”

“我可是天天有，”她心平气和地说。“喝茶我就爱就一小块糕饼。”

===Original===

Alex McNair was not entirely a stingy man—not the most stingy man in the neighborhood. He wasn’t like Andy McFee, for example, who was so careful of expenditure that when his corn got a little high in the summer he always took off his shirt and hoed the weeds in his skin, to save the wear of the cloth; and who persisted in habits of frugality so that, in his old age, when he rode about in his grandson’s Pierce-Arrow, he removed his shoes upon seating himself, to save them from harm, and persisted in this till an able granddaughter-in-law urged him not to misuse shoestrings with such extravagance. Nor was he like the elder John McKnight, who when he went to mill always took with him a hen tied in a little basket, to eat the oats that fell from his horse’s midday feeding. McNair thought such extremes foolish. He even laughed at McKnight’s device. How much easier it was simply to gather the oats up by hand, as he did, dust and all, and to take them home for the hens in his pocket. By this plan the oats were saved, and the hen had a whole day at home to convert useless angleworms into salable eggs. He was not, this proves, an entirely stingy man, yet—the idea of cakes like those for just a common supper! He would have to show that woman his disapproval, his disgust, his sharp pain at such extravagance.

He did his best then, and in the days that followed, to impress her. But she was difficult. She never lifted her voice in perturbation, and she never heeded a word he said. When the howling of the wind woke him up at night, he would hear her sighing, “It’s still raining!” When she looked shrinkingly out of the window in the morning, she murmured, “It’s still at it!” When he came in for dinner, she would ask, “Does it never stop?” At supper she sighed, like a weary child, “ ’Tis a fine land, this!”—for all the world as if he was to blame for the weather. She had been housekeeping for him but two days, when he pointed out the woodpile to her. “Bring the wood into the house,” she said, as if that was a man’s task. “I don’t like going out in the rain.” “The rain’ll not hurt you,” he assured her, going about his work. When he came in at noon, the fire was out, the room was cold, and she and the little girl were asleep and comfortable in bed. “I don’t like going out in the wet,” she repeated simply, as if she had done nothing outrageous in defying him. He had to wait for dinner till the wood was brought in, and dried, and the fire made. The next day she refused, in the same passive, happy way, to bring water from the slough well. She simply remarked she wouldn’t think of going so far in the mud, and waited till he brought the water. He never knew that she had hidden enough water for thirsty hours in a jug under the bed, and was prepared to stand a long siege. And then his boots were to be tallowed and dried near the fire. His wife Jeannie had always tallowed his boots. The new wife looked mildly surprised that he should have expected such a duty from her, and left the boots standing, muddy and soaked, just where they were, till he was driven to caring for them himself. And she kept asking him hour by hour, mildly, when he was going to town for her other boxes. She asked him so often, so kindly, that he was forced in despair to attempt the journey through the rain, thinking that maybe if she had something to sew, she would cease making cakes by the hour. And when he started, she gave him a great list of groceries to bring back, and ordered more sugar than his family ate in years. He growled at this—just growled. There had been enough sugar in the house when she came to last till spring. They could not use sugar as if it were water! Why not? she asked, simply. Wasn’t he a great lord, with acres? She liked sugar.

===Chinese===

亚历克斯·麦克奈尔（Alex McNair）还不算十足的小气——在这一带还算不得顶小气的人。比方说，他就不像安迪·麦克菲（Andy McFee），那人花钱精细到何等地步：夏天玉米长得稍高了些，他总要脱掉衬衫，光着膀子锄野草，好省下布料的磨损；他节俭成癖，积习不改，到老了，坐着孙子的皮尔斯-箭（Pierce-Arrow）汽车四处兜风，一落座就先把鞋脱下来，免得鞋受了伤，一直坚持不改，直到一位能干的孙媳妇劝他，别这样大手大脚地糟蹋鞋带，这才罢休。他也不像老约翰·麦克奈特（John McKnight），那人每回去磨坊，总要随身带一只母鸡，拴在小篮子里，好啄食他的马晌午喂料时撒落的燕麦。麦克奈尔觉得这样的极端做法愚蠢。他甚至笑话过麦克奈特的法子。像他那样，索性用手把燕麦连同尘土一把一把拣起来，装在口袋里带回家去喂母鸡，岂不省事得多？这样，燕麦省下了，母鸡又能在家里守上一整天，把无用的蚯蚓变成卖得出去的蛋。由此可以证明，他还不算十足的小气人。可是——不过一顿寻常晚饭，就摆上那样的糕饼！他得叫那女人看看他的不以为然，他的憎恶，他为这等挥霍感到的切肤之痛。

打那时起，连着后来那些日子，他一劲儿想把自己的心思敲进她脑子里。可她软硬不吃。她从来没有在慌乱里提高过嗓门，他说的话她一个字也不理会。夜里风嚎得他睡不着，就听见她在叹气：“还在下雨！”早晨她怯生生地朝窗外一望，就咕哝一句：“还下着呢！”他进屋吃午饭，她就问：“这雨就没个停的时候？”吃晚饭时她又叹气，像个乏透了的娃娃：“这真是个好地方啊！”——十足十地像这天气全该他担待似的。她给他当家才两天，他就把柴堆指给她看。“把柴抱进屋去吧，”她说，倒像那是男人干的活。“我不爱冒雨出门。”“雨淋不着你，”他一面照旧做他的活，一面给她吃定心丸。晌午他进屋时，火灭了，屋里冷冰冰，她和小姑娘在床上睡得舒舒服服。“我不爱踩着湿出去。”她还是那句话，说得平平常常，倒像她抗了他的命，并没有干出什么大逆不道的事来。他只得等柴抱进屋、烘干、生了火，才等得上午饭。第二天，她又用那副消消停停、乐呵呵的架势，拒绝到洼地的井上去打水。她只说了一句，那么远的泥路她想也不去想，然后就等着他把水拎回来。他从来不知道，她在床底下藏着满满一罐水，够她渴着熬上几个钟头的，早打定了主意要打一场长久的围困。还有他的靴子，也该抹上牛油，搁在火边烘干。他先前的妻子珍妮，一直是给他抹靴子的。新妻子一脸和和气气的诧异，倒像他竟指望她尽这样的本分，是天大的怪事；靴子呢，还是泥糊糊、水漉漉地立在原地，直到他被逼不过，只好自己去拾掇。她还一个钟头一个钟头地、温温和和地追问他，几时进城去取她其余的箱子。她问得那样勤，那样和善，逼得他走投无路，只好冒雨走一程，心想她要是有个针线活儿可做，兴许就不会一个钟头接着一个钟头地烤糕饼了。他动身的时候，她交给他一张长长的杂货单子，吩咐要买的糖，比他一家子几年吃的还要多。为这个他低吼了几声——光低吼而已。她进门的时候，家里的糖原是够吃到开春的。他们总不能把糖当水使！怎么不能？她只简单地问了一句。他不是个有田有地的大老爷么？她喜欢糖。

===Original===

He brought back with him only a little sugar, and most of it the coarse brown kind, and a jug of sorghum which was to last till spring. She fell upon her boxes eagerly, and adorned the sty amazingly with rich-looking things which never really seemed at home there. She made a new dress for her little stepdaughter at once, and set about making Chirstie’s baby a robe. She seemed almost to have resigned herself to the deluge. She spoke with gaiety about her ark to the children, and told them to keep their eyes open for the dove. And then, just when she seemed to be getting settled, the winter set in.

Rains she had seen, and could understand, and snows, too, in moderate fashion. But snow like this, continuing; winds like these, whirling darkening wild clouds of whiteness to burst against windows and doors, rocking the little sty as if it were an insecure cradle—winds with horror howling in them, howling all night through the shaken darkness, triumphant, unconquerable winds against which no life could stand—she had never imagined anything like them. She had never before risen in the morning to find doors drifted tight shut, windows banked with white. She had never seen men burrow out of windows to dig open their doors, and tunnel a way to their barns. The well was as distant as if it had been in Patagonia. The newborn calf froze in the barn with its first breath. The men’s ears froze, their hands froze, their feet froze. Everything in the house froze solid. The bread had to be thawed out in a steamer over a kettle before they could get a bite to eat in the morning. The milk had to be pounded into little bits and melted. The cold—its intensity, its cruelty, staggered her.

===Chinese===

他带回来的只有一点点糖，大半还是那种粗粗的红糖，另有一大罐高粱糖浆，说好要吃到来年开春。她急切地扑向她的箱子，用那些看着华贵、在这里却总归不像自家东西的物件，把猪圈装点得叫人吃惊。她立刻给她的小继女做了一条新裙子，又着手给克丽丝蒂的娃娃赶做一件抱袍。她好像差不多已经认了这场大洪水了。她欢欢喜喜地对孩子们讲起她的方舟，叫他们睁大眼睛守着鸽子。后来，就在她眼看要安顿下来的时候，冬天压境了。

雨她是见过的，也懂得；雪也见过，只要还讲点分寸。可是这样的雪，下起来没完没了；这样的风，卷着一片片越转越暗的狂野的白，劈头盖脑砸向门窗，把小小的猪圈摇得像一只不牢稳的摇篮——风里头嚎着恐怖，在被震得发抖的黑夜里嚎个通宵，那是得意扬扬、不可征服的风，没有一个活物能在它跟前站住——这样的东西，她想都不曾想象过。她从前从来没有早晨起来，发现门叫积雪封得死死的、窗根底下堆着白雪。她从没见过男人们从窗户里爬出去，把自家的门挖开，再挖出一条通到谷仓的地道。那口井远得像是在巴塔哥尼亚（Patagonia）。初生的牛犊在谷仓里，一口气还没喘完就冻死了。男人的耳朵冻了，手冻了，脚冻了。屋里样样东西冻得梆硬。早晨得先把面包搁在壶口的蒸屉里化开，才嚼得上一口。牛奶得砸成小碎块再化。这寒冷——它的凛冽，它的残忍，把她震住了。

===Original===

Her work would be done early in the morning, while the men were yet melting snow at the stove to water their beasts—that is, all the work she chose to do. To conquer those long, dark hours she worked away on the baby dress. When it was all finished—alas, too soon for one having endless time to beguile—she looked at it with satisfaction. She had made every stitch of it by hand. It was a yard and a half long, with seven clusters of seven tiny tucks around the skirt, with hand embroidery between some of the rows, and darned net between others. It was ruffled and shirred, and smocked and featherstitched and hemstitched, eyeleted and piped and gathered. And a tiny darned net bonnet, which went with it, was worthy of it. It had taken many weeks to complete it. And always when her eyes were worn by the fine stitching in the flickering candlelight, she made cakes, for a change, sparing white sugar with noble economy, using only brown sugar, whatever eggs were unfrozen, fresh butter, and thick cream, and raisins and currants while they lasted.

From the day that Wully took Chirstie home, until the first week of January, Barbara McNair had but one visitor in her prison, and that one was her sister-in-law, Libby Keith. She had to turn to Dod to companionship, which no boy could have grudged to so unfailing a source of cakes as his new mother. His Spartan scorn of the cold brought her, many a time, near to tears. He was anointing his frozen ears one morning, and when she cried out in pity of him, he remarked indifferently that this was nothing. She ought to have seen last year, the time his mother died. With what keen sympathy could she appreciate that story now. She asked without hesitation;

===Chinese===

她的活计一早就做完了，趁男人们还在炉边化雪饮牲口的当儿——就是说，凡她高兴做的活，统做完了。为了降服那些又长又黑的时辰，她一针一针赶做那件娃娃袍。全都做好了——唉，对一个有无限光阴要消磨的人，还是快得可惜——她心满意足地端详它。上头的每一针都出自她的手工。袍子一码半长，裙摆一圈缀着七簇细裥，一簇七道，有几行之间是手绣的花，有几行之间是织补的网纱。它有荷叶边，有碎褶，有烟褶绣，有羽毛绣，有抽纱花缝，还开着气眼，滚着边，打着裥。配它的一顶小小的织补网纱软帽，也当得起它。完工足足用了好几个星期。每当摇曳的烛光里细密的针线扎得她眼睛发乏，她就改去烤糕饼，换一换手：白糖省着用，省得一副高贵的节俭气派，只用红糖；鸡蛋有几只没冻的用几只，鲜黄油、稠奶油，葡萄干、小葡萄干，也都是有多少用多少。

从伍利把克丽丝蒂娶回家那天起，直到一月的头一个星期，芭芭拉·麦克奈尔这座监牢里统共只来过一位客人，就是她夫家的姻亲姐妹莉比·基思（Libby Keith）。她只得靠多德作伴——对这样一座糕饼从不断绝的泉源，哪个孩子也小气不了这份陪伴。这孩子对严寒那副斯巴达式的蔑视，多少回叫她眼泪都快下来了。一天早晨，他正往冻伤的耳朵上抹油，她心疼得叫出声来，他却满不在乎地说，这算不了什么。去年他妈妈死的那一回，那才该看看呢。如今她听这故事，该能体味得多么切肤。她毫不犹豫地问；

===Original===

“It was no colder than this, was it?” She couldn’t imagine anything worse. Oh, said Dod, they were alone last winter, and his mother and Chirstie had sometimes to help shovel out. But they had had Chirstie’s husband, hadn’t they, to do that hard work for them? Indeed they hadn’t! Dod himself had been the man of the farm. Wully had come but lately. Not lately, surely, she exclaimed. Yes, only in harvest. They had been married right in harvest. He was sure of it. What month would harvest be in this land? she had asked hurriedly. He informed her, and took up his story. He had had to go alone that morning after his mother’s death to his uncle’s, to get help, and hadn’t it taken them three hours to get the sled over the two miles of drifted snow. He told all the tale, even how the little sister was playing alone, and Chirstie had fainted.

All that afternoon there came little words of pity to Barbara McNair as she fondled her little Jeannie; sometimes, when she was making that great, most magnificent cake which appeared unashamed on the supper table, she had to stop and wipe her eyes. Alex McNair had but begun to disapprove of that delicacy when she ordered him so sharply to hold his tongue that he all but obeyed. And after supper, she made him lift down her kists, which because of the narrowness of the sty had to sit one above another in her bedroom. She opened the third one from the top, and took out a dress, wine-colored and soft, and looked at it carefully a long time, examining the seams. Then she sat down, and by candlelight began to rip it apart, basque and polonaise and all, to make a dress for the erring Chirstie.

===Chinese===

“去年也不比这更冷，是不是？”她再想不出更坏的了。哦，多德说，去年冬天就剩他们自己，有时候他妈妈和克丽丝蒂还得帮着往外铲雪。可他们不是有克丽丝蒂的男人，替他们干那些重活么？才没有呢！多德自己就是农场上顶事的男人。伍利来得晚。不会晚吧，她嚷道。真的晚，就在收麦的时候。他们正是收麦的时候成的亲。这一点他记得千真万确。这地方收麦是几月？她慌忙地问。他告诉了她，又接着讲他的故事。他妈妈死的那天早晨，他只得独自一个到舅舅家去讨帮手，那两英里积雪的道，他们把雪橇弄过去足足花了三个钟头，还了得？他把前前后后讲了个遍，连小妹妹独自个儿在一旁玩耍、克丽丝蒂晕死过去，也都讲了。

那一整个下午，一句句怜惜的话落到芭芭拉·麦克奈尔心上，她搂着小珍妮爱抚着；有时，她正做着那个大得出奇、气派得出奇、往后要毫无愧色摆上晚饭桌的糕饼，也不得不停下来，擦一擦眼睛。亚历克斯·麦克奈尔才刚要对那件精美的吃食露出一点不满，她就厉声叫他住嘴，他差点儿就当真住了嘴。吃过晚饭，她叫他把她那几口大木箱搬下来——猪圈地方窄，箱子只好在她卧房里一口摞一口地码着。她打开从顶上数第三只，取出一条酒红色的、柔软的裙子，仔细端详了半晌，把缝线一条条查过。然后她坐下来，就着烛光动手拆它，紧身上身也好，波兰式罩裙也好，一齐拆散，要给那失了足的克丽丝蒂改做一条裙子。

===Original===

It was the next afternoon that she saw a bobsled drive in. She could see the bundled driver when he was yet some distance from the house, but as he drew near, and stopped, she saw another great beshawled bundle rise from behind the sideboards of the sled. This bundle came at once towards the house, wiped its feet carefully on the doorstep, and, unwrapping layer after layer of covering, revealed itself Isobel McLaughlin. Mrs. McNair could hardly have been more surprised if she had seen an angel descending from heaven. That any woman would be riding around the country in weather like this had not entered her mind. Her concern seemed mildly amusing to her guest, who quickly disclaimed any conduct especially praiseworthy.

It wasn’t really cold now, she explained. It was thawing. This was what is called the January thaw. A body can’t just stay cooped up in the house all the winter, and besides—and this was the great affair—Mistress McNair would be glad to know that she had a fine strong grandson, born a week ago, the mother doing well! Mrs. McLaughlin had wanted to bring the news herself, she was that pleased! She had stopped, too, at a neighbor’s, Maggie Stewart’s, who had a baby exactly the same age, a woman whom always before Mrs. McLaughlin had helped through her confinement. She didn’t add she had made that visit with the hope of lessening the fierceness of Maggie’s slander-loving tongue, though if a good opportunity came she intended explaining to this newcomer the unusual circumstances of the child’s birth, which sooner or later she would be sure to hear some way. But no opportunity came. The new Mrs. McNair was so unfeignedly glad to see her, she brought out that wonderful little robe so timidly, that Mrs. McLaughlin had to admire it even more than it deserved. Chirstie hadn’t many new things for her baby, because there were so many little things of the young McLaughlins saved for future need. Not that any of them had had so fine a garment as this Mrs. McNair had made. Speed, rather than elaborateness, had always been Mrs. McLaughlin’s motto, necessarily. But Chirstie would be that proud of such a little dress! Mrs. McLaughlin could just see her delighted with it. This seemed to comfort Mrs. McNair, who then ventured to show the red dress, all pressed and ready to be put together again, by a method which she hoped would make it large enough for Chirstie—that is, if Chirstie would not be offended by having a made-over dress offered to her. Mrs. McLaughlin again thanked her, and assured her that she need not worry about that. Then Mrs. McNair wondered if Mrs. McLaughlin would take home to the girl her part of her mother’s housekeeping things, which the new mother had wrapped and made ready for her. She had divided the few sheets and spoons and cups into two parts, one for each of the sisters—that is, she hoped Mrs. McLaughlin and Chirstie would be satisfied with such a division. Mrs. McLaughlin, feeling sure that Alex had no knowledge of a plan so bountiful, protested that Chirstie didn’t really need the things, that Wully could get her what she needed in the town. But Mrs. McNair wouldn’t hear of such a plan for a minute. The lassie must have her share of what had been her mother’s. She forebore to mention that she had brought a great deal of household stuff, of a quality much superior to any she found awaiting her. Mrs. McLaughlin, impressed by this spontaneous liberality, began to wonder if, after all, the avenging hand of God might not be seen in this second marriage of Alex McNair.

===Chinese===

到第二天下午，她看见一辆大雪橇赶了进来。离房子还有老远，她就看得见那裹得严严实实的赶车人；等雪橇驶近、停下，她又看见侧板后头直起另一个裹着大披巾的大包袱。那包袱立刻朝房子过来，在门阶上仔仔细细蹭净了脚，然后一层又一层解开裹缠，露出了真身——竟是伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林（Isobel McLaughlin）。麦克奈尔太太就是亲眼看见天使从天而降，惊也不会更惊了。这样的天气，竟会有女人在乡野里跑来跑去，这是她连想也不曾想过的。她那份关切，叫客人觉得有几分好笑，客人连忙声明，她这举动可没有什么特别值得称道的。

她解释说，如今其实不冷了，天在化冻。这就是人家说的“一月化冻”。人总不能一整个冬天都圈在屋里。况且——这才是顶要紧的——麦克奈尔太太听说必定要欢喜：她添了个又结实又漂亮的孙子，落生一个星期了，当娘的很好！麦克劳克林太太这喜信非要自己来报不可，她欢喜成了那样！她路上还在邻居玛吉·斯图尔特（Maggie Stewart）家歇了一脚，那家有个娃娃跟这个岁数一模一样，那女人从前回回坐月子，都是麦克劳克林太太帮着熬过来的。她没有添上说，她去那一趟，原指望磨一磨玛吉那张偏爱造谣的利嘴的锋头；不过，倘若有好机会，她本打算跟这位新来的把那孩子落生的非常情由讲说明白——这桩事，她迟早总要打哪条道上听来的。可机会没有来。新麦克奈尔太太见她来，欢喜得那样真诚；她捧出那件绝妙的小抱袍时，又那样怯生生的，麦克劳克林太太不得不把它夸得比它的本分还盛上几分。克丽丝蒂给她娃娃添的新东西不多，因为麦克劳克林家小的们留下的小物件多着呢，都存着将来用。倒不是说他们哪一个曾有过麦克奈尔太太做的这样一件好衣裳。“快”，而不是“精工”，向来是麦克劳克林太太的座右铭，那是出于无奈。可克丽丝蒂得了这样一件小裙子，该多得意！麦克劳克林太太简直能看见她捧着它欢喜的模样。这话似乎宽了麦克奈尔太太的心，她这才壮起胆子，把那条红裙子也拿出来给她看。裙子已经熨得平平整整，单等重新缝拢，她琢磨出一个法子，兴许能把它放宽到克丽丝蒂穿得下——就是说，倘使人家送她一件改做的旧裙，克丽丝蒂不至于见怪。麦克劳克林太太又谢了她，请她尽管放心，断没有见怪的道理。随后麦克奈尔太太又问，麦克劳克林太太可肯把那姑娘的那一份、她母亲留下的管家什，捎回家去给她——这些东西新妈妈已经包扎妥当，替她备好了。那几条床单、几把汤匙、几只杯子，她都分作两份，姐妹俩一人一份——就是说，她指望麦克劳克林太太和克丽丝蒂对这样的分法能满意。麦克劳克林太太拿得稳，这样一个慷慨的主意，亚历克斯必然毫不知情，便推说克丽丝蒂实在用不着这些东西，伍利在镇上要什么都能给她买。可这样的打算，麦克奈尔太太一分钟也不肯听。姑娘家母亲留下的东西，必有她的一份。她忍住了没说：她自己带来的家常用度何其丰足，又何其高过她初到时迎接她的那一些。麦克劳克林太太被这份出自天性的慷慨打动了，开始纳闷：亚历克斯·麦克奈尔这桩续弦，兴许到头来真能看出上帝那报应的手来。

===Original===

The hostess was overflowing with questions, the burden of them all being just the one unanswerable one that constantly confronted her—namely, how did civilized persons live through winters of this sort? Why did they endure life in small prisons buried under snow? Had there ever before been a winter equal to this one? And did Mrs. McLaughlin look forward with composure to living through such another one?

Mrs. McLaughlin recalled with amusement and sympathy her own horror of her first winter, enlarging upon her experience. Had not she and her husband and their ten, and the Squire and his ten lived through one winter all together in an unfinished cabin, with a row of beds three deep built right around the walls, and a curtain across the middle of it! Often in those terrible nights she had risen from her bed to go about and feel the legs of her wee sleepers, to be sure they were not all freezing solid. Of course there had not really been as much danger as she imagined, but one of the McKnights had frozen to death that winter, being overtaken on his drunken way homeward by a great storm. That had shocked her until she was really foolish about her children. Her twins had been born that year, too, before the cabin was sealed, and the first snow had drifted in upon the bed where she lay. Fine strong bairns they were, too.
