The cold didn’t really hurt anyone.

===Chinese===

女主人满肚子都是问不完的问题，所有的问话归结起来，只是那个时时刻刻横在她面前、没法回答的一个——就是说，文明人到底是怎样熬过这样的冬天的？为什么他们甘受这种埋在雪底下的小监狱里的日子？从前的冬天可有过跟这一个相当的？麦克劳克林太太想到要再熬这样一冬，还能心平气和么？

麦克劳克林太太带着又好笑又体恤的心情，讲起她自己头一个冬天的那份害怕，把她的经历细细铺陈了一番。她和她的丈夫带着他们的十个，还有乡绅（Squire）带着他的十个，一冬天全挤在一座没盖完的小木屋里过，四壁搭着一圈床，一排摞三层，屋子中间拦一道布帘——那不也熬过来了！那些可怕的夜里，她常常从床上爬起来，挨个去摸她那些小睡着的人的腿脚，看是不是全冻实了。自然，实在并没有她想象的那么危险，不过那年冬天，麦克奈特家有一个人是冻死的，喝得醉醺醺往家走，叫一场大雪暴赶上了。这件事把她吓坏了，直到对孩子们弄得真有点儿痴里痴气。她的那对双胞胎也是那年落生的，那时小屋还没堵严，头一场雪就飘进了她躺着的床上。也照旧是结实漂亮的娃娃。寒冷其实没伤着什么人。

===Original===

Moreover, it drove the fever away, so that they welcomed its coming in the fall, when the whole family would be shaking at one time. Fever wasn’t as bad now, either, as it had been at first, though she still fed her family quinine regularly every Saturday during the spring and summer. When the land had all been plowed once or twice, there would be no more of it, ’twas said. And there had been much typhoid at first, before they had realized how much more defilable the new wells were than those in the old places had been. Five of the McLaughlin children had escaped typhoid altogether, which was very lucky indeed, and none of them had died of it, although many of the young ones of the settlement had. These things had all made a good deal of nursing necessary, for thirteen, but undoubtedly the worst days were over. And it was these winters which made the children strong as little lions. Every tree that was planted, moreover, every year’s growth of their cherished windbreaks, took away something of the winter’s severity. And when spring came, besides, in the glory of that season one forgot the cold, and all one’s troubles.

When would spring be coming? asked the longing stranger. Would it be in February, now that January was said to be thawing? No, not February. Nor in March. Sometimes it was a bit springlike by the first of April. But the spring really opened in May. Everyone got out then. Oh, sometimes if the roads were good, the women got out to church in April. Once even there had been a large congregation in March. Mrs. McNair sighed. It was a shame, now, commented her visitor, that she should have had to be alone so much of her first season. If there had been an older daughter, now … if Chirstie had been at home with her. …

===Chinese===

况且，寒冷还把烧热赶跑了，所以秋天它一来，全家正一齐打摆子抖作一团的时候，他们反倒欢迎它来。如今烧热也不像当初那样凶了，虽说春夏两季，她还是雷打不动，每个礼拜六给全家喂一回奎宁。人家说，等地统统犁过一两遍，这病就没有了。起先伤寒也闹得凶，那时他们还不明白，新掘的井比老地方的井容易脏污多少。麦克劳克林家的孩子有五个压根儿没染上伤寒，真是天大的运气，也没有一个死于伤寒，尽管定居点里的娃娃夭折了不少。这一切都少不了许多看护的工夫，照管十三个孩子，不过顶坏的日子无疑已经过去了。也正靠这样的冬天，孩子们才壮得像小狮子。再说，每栽一棵树，他们心爱的防风林每长一年，就把冬天的严酷削去一分。何况一到春天，那季节的荣光一起，人就把寒冷连同种种苦处都忘了。

春天什么时候才来呢？那满心渴望的客人问道。一月既然听说都在化冻，那春天总该二月就来吧？不，二月不来。三月也不来。有时到四月初，倒有几分春意。可春天真正开张是在五月。那时人人都出得门了。哦，有时路要是好，四月里女人们也上得了教堂。有一回，甚至三月里就有过一大堂会众。麦克奈尔太太叹了口气。她的客人说，头一个季度就这样大半孤单单地过，实在说不过去。要是跟前有个大女儿呢……要是克丽丝蒂在家陪着她……

===Original===

Mrs. McNair wondered timidly if Chirstie couldn’t come home for a visit, when it got a little less freezing. Mrs. McLaughlin, thinking quickly that Chirstie would surely be happy with this simple gift-giving woman, thought it possible that Wully might bring her over for a few days in March. At least in April. And when she saw the poor, wee body seize upon this hope of companionship, she felt more sure than ever that Chirstie would enjoy the visit.

If only she would come, that dress should be made for her, Mrs. McNair ventured to promise. And she went on to get more information. What sort of a little house would it be, now, that Wully was building for his wife? What could houses be like in these parts? How many rooms would it have? Isobel explained that there were to be three rooms on the first floor, a parlor, a kitchen and a bedroom, and two bedrooms above. Certainly it would be plastered, all white and clean. Doubtless it would be painted in time, not just at first, of course, but as soon as Wully could manage it. Of course it would have a fence around it, like those Mrs. McNair had seen from the train, and trees, most certainly. They had been planted last fall. Trees were one thing essential on the prairies. Well, likely flowers, too, in time, although women as yet had so much to do that there weren’t many flowers about. Mrs. McLaughlin had herself often sighed for a few wee rosebushes. And she had a fine young orchard set out and flourishing. Had not Alex McNair been in these parts as long as the McLaughlins, the new wife asked. And Mrs. McLaughlin, hiding her malice sweetly, didn’t doubt but what he would be setting out an orchard soon. “The poor wee body!” she said to herself. “Her wanting flowers, and a man like Alex!”

The pitied one set out such a tea, she sent her guest home with such an abundance of sweeties for her bairns, that Mrs. McLaughlin talked hopefully about her all the way home to her husband. She solemnly affirmed that that new wife would give away Alex McNair’s last sock, if she could find anyone to take it; and for her part, she hoped fervently that she could.

===Chinese===

麦克奈尔太太怯怯地寻思，等天不那么冻了，克丽丝蒂能不能回家来小住几日。麦克劳克林太太心思转得快：克丽丝蒂跟这样一个单纯、一心给东西的女人在一起，一定快活，便说兴许三月里伍利能送她过来住上几天。至少四月里。她见这可怜的小人儿抓住这个有人作伴的指望不放，越发有把握，克丽丝蒂这一趟一定过得痛快。

只要她肯来，那条裙子就给她做，麦克奈尔太太壮着胆子许了愿。她又往下打听。伍利给他媳妇盖的那座小房子，如今会是个什么样儿？这地方的房子，还能盖出个什么样？能有几间屋？伊莎贝尔说，楼下有三间屋：一间客厅、一间厨房、一间卧室，楼上还有两间卧室。那自然是要抹灰的，里外雪白干净。油漆想必迟早要上，自然不是一上来就上，是要等伍利腾得出手。四周围自然要有一圈栅栏，就像麦克奈尔太太在火车上看见过的那些一样；树更是准有的，去年秋天就栽下了。在草原上，树是少不得的一样东西。花嘛，将来说不定也会有，只是眼下女人们的活计太多，四下里花还不算多。麦克劳克林太太自己也常为几丛小蔷薇叹气巴望呢。她自己就有一片好果园，新栽下去，长得正旺。新媳妇问，亚历克斯·麦克奈尔来这地方，不是跟麦克劳克林家一样久了么？麦克劳克林太太甜甜地藏起自己的促狭，说她毫不怀疑，他不久也要栽果园了。“这可怜的小人儿！”她心里说。“她盼着花，偏配了亚历克斯这么个男人！”

那位被怜惜的摆了那样一桌茶点，送她的客人回家时，又给她娃娃们包了那样一大包糖果，麦克劳克林太太一路上满怀希望地跟她丈夫念叨她。她一本正经地断言，那个新媳妇只消找得着肯要的人，连亚历克斯·麦克奈尔最后一只袜子也要送了人；她自己呢，倒是满心热诚地巴望她能找着。

===Original===

That evening as Alex sat smoking his pipe, with his stocking feet well into the oven, his wife asked him artlessly:

“Will Chirstie’s man have much, now?”

“What would he have but his land?”

“But he’s building a fine house!”

“He would. The McLaughlins were ever spenders and poor. Not that the house would cost much,” he added.

“Now what would such a house as his be costing?” It seemed a natural question.

“Four hundred dollars. Or maybe five.”

===Chinese===

那天晚上，亚历克斯坐着抽烟斗，穿着袜子的双脚深深伸在烤箱里，他的妻子天真地问他：

“克丽丝蒂的男人，如今家当多么？”

“除了地，他还能有什么？”

“可他正盖着一座好房子呢！”

“他会盖的。麦克劳克林家从来是又肯花钱又穷。倒不是说那房子要花多少钱，”他又添上一句。

“那么，像他那样的房子，得花多少钱呢？”听上去是个再自然不过的问题。

“四百块钱。兴许五百。”

===Original===

She was surprised, for once, almost excited.

“You could build a castle with your money from Scotland!”

“Likely!” he commented, knocking his pipe’s ashes into the stove.

“But a little house like the new one would do me fine!”

“Don’t say new house to me, woman!” he roared.

===Chinese===

这一回她吃惊了，几乎兴奋起来。

“拿你从苏格兰带来的钱，你都能盖一座城堡了！”

“可不是嘛！”他应了一声，把烟斗的灰磕进炉膛。

“可像那新房子那样的一座小房子，叫我住就很好了！”

“别跟我说新房子，婆娘！”他吼道。

===Original===

A great deal of good his roaring did him! It was as if she never heard him protesting. “I canna live in a sty,” she explained, for the thousandth time, and she said new house to him without ceasing, without haste or rest, by night and by day, apropos of everything he mentioned, till he began to wonder if he were indeed a God-fearing Presbyterian, with such murder in his heart. He couldn’t quite beat a woman—a small woman—no matter how utterly she might deserve punishment. He could scarcely do that. But he sometimes wondered if there was any other measure of relief for him. He thought longingly of the silences of Chirstie’s mother. He remembered story after story of men who had beat their wives. He experienced a sharp sympathy for them. Doubtless when men do such desperate things, they have adequate reason, he reflected often. He was at his wits’ end. He was in despair. That he might have made himself comfortable by granting her request never occurred to him. He was already deliberating upon certain pieces of land he intended buying.

And that woman didn’t seem able to believe that he would really buy more land. She simply looked out of the window when he mentioned it, looked out of the window at the winter, and then turned puzzled to look at him, as if trying to fathom why anyone should desire more of such a country.

So February passed, tantalized by new houses, and March got away, maddened by little white fences. Chirstie came over for her visit at home, the first of April, and that first week was frenzied by plans his wife insisted on drawing of her grounds and garden. Alex was no special lover of babies, but he was driven to feigning a prodigious interest in his grandson to escape even temporarily from the meek, eternal din of her ambitions.

===Chinese===

他这一吼，又顶了什么用！她好像根本没听见他抗议似的。“我没法住在猪圈里，”她解释道，第一千遍地说，新房子新房子，说个不停，不慌不忙，也不歇气，白天说，夜里说，他提起什么，她都能就着什么说到新房子，直说得他起疑：自己这个敬畏上帝的长老会教徒，心里怎么窝着这样的杀机。他到底下不去手打一个女人——一个小小的女人——不管她多么该受责罚。这个他几乎做不来。可他有时候不免琢磨，除了这个，还有没有旁的解脱的法子。他苦苦地想念克丽丝蒂母亲的那种沉默。他记起一桩又一桩男人打老婆的故事，对他们生出一种切肤的同情。男人干出这样绝望的事来，想必总有充分的缘由——他常常这样寻思。他一筹莫展。他陷于绝望。只要依了她的请求，他自己就能安生自在——这层道理他从来想不到。他正盘算着几块他打算买下的地呢。

而且那女人好像没法相信他当真要买更多的地。他一提起，她只朝窗外望，望着窗外的冬天，然后满腹疑团地转过脸来看他，像是要探明白，怎么会有人还想多要这样的地方。

二月就这样过去了，叫新房子撩拨得心痒难熬；三月也溜走了，叫白色的小栅栏折磨得发疯。四月一号，克丽丝蒂过来家里做客了；头一个星期，他妻子非要画她那片宅地园子的图样不可，闹得人发疯。亚历克斯本不是个特别喜欢娃娃的人，可他不得不装出对孙子有一种了不得的兴趣，好哪怕暂时躲开她那志向的柔顺而没完没了的聒噪。

===Original===

Chirstie had come with misgivings, somewhat doubtful of her welcome. But she perceived the first hour in the house that her stepmother was lonely enough to have welcomed the most disgraceful, the most evil of women. She wondered sometimes if she was not dreaming. After all that had passed, how strange it was to be sitting honored in her father’s house, coddled, waited on, made much of, by this harmless stranger, who cooked surprising rich things for her delectation, and was making her the most beautiful dress she had ever seen.

She was so happy that she almost regretted that Wully came for her so soon. Mrs. McNair was determined that she must try on the new dress to show it to him. She had forbidden him at first to look in their direction, so he sat with his back to them, holding his little sister-in-law in his lap by the fire. After pinnings and bastings and warnings and ejaculations they had bidden him to turn and look. Chirstie was standing by that window, in the sunshine, where he had first seen her. And now, turning towards her, he gave a little involuntary gasp of delight, more flattering than anything he could have said. He had never seen her before in a soft, rich thing like that. She had worn, of necessity, gray or brown calico garments. And the glowing crimson fabric brought out the whiteness of her neck, the darkness of her hair, the softness of her coloring cheeks, as he cried sincerely;

“Why, Chirstie! You *queen*! Turn around!”

===Chinese===

克丽丝蒂是揣着忐忑来的，多少疑心自己不受欢迎。可进屋头一个钟头她就看出，她的继母孤单到了那份上，就是顶不名誉、顶邪恶的女人上门，也会受到欢迎。她有时疑心自己是在做梦。经过那许多事之后，竟能坐在父亲家里受着敬重，让这个无害的陌生女人娇宠着、伺候着、宝贝着——她做出叫人吃惊的好菜来给她受用，又正给她缝她这辈子见过的最漂亮的裙子——这多么奇怪啊。

她快活到那份上，伍利这么快来接她，她倒几乎有些舍不得了。麦克奈尔太太拿定主意，她非得把新裙子穿上试给伍利看不可。起初她不许他朝她们那边看，他便背对着她们坐在炉火边，让他的小姨子坐在膝上。别针别了又拔，疏缝缝了又拆，警告了一声又一声，惊呼了一阵又一阵，这才准他转过身来看。克丽丝蒂正站在那扇窗前，站在阳光里——他头一回看见她的地方。此刻他朝她转过身去，不由得轻轻欢喜得倒抽一口气——这比他说得出的任何话都更叫她得意。他从前从没见她穿过这样柔软华贵的衣裳。不得已，她从前只能穿灰的或棕的印花布衣裳。如今那灼灼的绯红料子，衬得她的脖颈更白，头发更黑，双颊的颜色更柔和，他真心诚意地喊道；

“哎呀，克丽丝蒂！你这*王后*！转个身！”

===Original===

She turned around for his inspection.

“Goodness!” he exclaimed. “I wouldn’t have known you! What’ll I do now? I won’t can walk beside you in my old rags! I’ll have to get some store clothes!”

They laughed for delight.

“What’ll I get to match it?” he went on, looking at his mother-in-law. “I ought to have—a purple coat—or something magnifical! Chirstie, do you remember that window! She was standing there the first time I ever saw her!” he explained to Mrs. McNair.

===Chinese===

她转过身去，由他细细地看。

“老天爷！”他嚷道。“我差点儿认不出你来！这可叫我怎么办？穿着我这身旧破烂，我可没法再跟你并肩走了！我得去置办几件铺子里卖的现成衣裳！”

他们欢喜得笑作一团。

“我该配点什么才相称呢？”他接着说，眼睛望着他的岳母。“我该有一件——紫色的外衣——或者什么顶气派顶堂皇的物什！克丽丝蒂，你还记得那扇窗户不！我头一回看见她，她就站在那儿！”他向麦克奈尔太太解释道。

===Original===

And then at length, in their high, young spirits, they went away, and left her alone there. She was a puzzled woman. A man like that, and a scandal like that! It was incomprehensible. A man building so happily a new house for his wife, with a little fence around it!

That evening Alex McNair gave vent to a great, wicked, bloodcurdling oath, most surprising, most improper—all for no reason at all—apropos of nothing. His innocent wife had simply remarked that she couldn’t live in a sty.

===Chinese===

到末了，这一对兴致高高的年轻人走了，把她一个人留在那里。她是个满心疑惑的女人。这样一个男人，配着那样一桩丑闻！叫人没法明白。一个欢欢喜喜给妻子盖新房子的男人，四周围还要扎一道小栅栏！

那天晚上，亚历克斯·麦克奈尔爆出一句大大的、恶毒的、叫人毛骨悚然的咒骂，出人意外，不成体统——无缘无故——跟什么都不沾边。他那位无辜的妻子，不过说了一句她没法住在猪圈里罢了。

## XI / 第十一章

===Original===

The infamy of Chirstie’s condition, becoming known, had been scarcely less interesting than the scandal of Isobel McLaughlin’s attitude toward it. She herself had told her sister and her sisters-in-law what was soon to be expected from the girl, and all her cousins and friends. She had informed them of it casually, without the flutter of an eyelid, as if, to be sure, a little less haste might have been from some points of view desirable, but, after all, Wully’s marriage was the one she would have chosen for him if she had had her choice, and the young pair would be happier with a baby. The neighbors had certainly never expected Isobel McLaughlin to “take on” in such a fashion. Some of them had been annoyed at times by her self-reliance, her full trust in her own powers, and were not exactly sorry to hear of this affair which must “set her down a notch.” But not a notch down would she go! Her pride, it appeared, was too strong for even this blow. The way she talked about her expectations scandalized the righteous. Maggie Stewart said one would have supposed Wully had waited ten years for that baby.

It had been bad enough in the beginning, but after the child was born it grew out of all bounds. Her husband’s younger sister, Janet, a woman still of childbearing age, came to remonstrate with her. For the sake of the other young people in the community, to say nothing of her own family of half-grown boys and girls, she really ought to moderate her raptures somewhat. She was just encouraging them in wrongdoing! But Isobel replied simply that since she had always had to be painfully modest in praising her own children, she was going to say exactly what she thought about this grandchild. She philosophized shamelessly about the privileges of grandmothers. And, after all, if she was his own grandmother who was saying it, Janet would have to acknowledge that the baby was an unusually fine child.

Janet did have to grant that. She was the first one, too, to notice the remarkable resemblance the child bore to his father. Isobel was grateful to her for that hint, and after that day no visitor departed without agreeing that wee Johnnie was a living picture of great Wully. Isobel would recall her son’s infant features. Wully’s nose had been just like that. And his eyes. She minded it well, now. This child brought it all back to her. She had occasion to repeat these reminiscences, for baby-judging, giving a decision about his family traits, was nothing less than a ritual among these Scots. A woman could hardly acquit herself with distinction in it with less than six or eight of her own. And men, even fathers of thirteen, knowing how far short of the occasion they would come, generally avoided it as best they might.

===Chinese===

克丽丝蒂那桩处境的丑名声传扬开来以后，人们津津乐道的程度，几乎不亚于伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林对此事的态度所掀起的轩然大波。她自己就把姑娘家眼看要有的那桩事，告诉了她的姐妹和妯娌们，还有她所有的表亲和朋友。她说得随随便便，眼皮都不眨一下，仿佛——自然喽——这事要是从容些，从某些角度看未始不可取；可说到底，伍利这桩婚事，就算由着她自己挑，她挑中的也是这一桩，何况小两口有了娃娃会更快活。邻居们着实没料到，伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林竟会这般“忘乎所以”。她那份自立自主、那份对自己本事的十足自信，有时本就叫一些人来气；如今听说出了这么一桩事，想着准能“杀杀她的傲气”，他们心里倒也不怎么替她惋惜。可她半分傲气也不肯跌！她的骄傲，看来连这一记重击也压不垮。她谈论自家那份“盼头”的口气，叫正经人听着觉得有伤体统。玛吉·斯图尔特（Maggie Stewart）说，听那话音，人家还当伍利等这个娃娃等了十年呢。

起头已经够糟的了，等娃娃落了地，更是漫没了边儿。她丈夫的妹妹珍妮特（Janet），一个还在生育年纪的女人，特地上门来劝她。看在社区里其他年轻人的份上——更不必提她自己家里那一群半大不小的儿女——她也真该把那份欣喜若狂收敛几分。她这简直是在纵容他们做错事！可伊莎贝尔只淡淡回了一句：她夸自家的孩子，向来憋着性子、谦让得苦；对这个孙儿，她打算把心里想的痛痛快快说出来。她不知羞地大谈起做祖母的种种特权。再说，说这话的又是娃娃的亲祖母，珍妮特总得承认，这孩子出奇地好。

这一点珍妮特也只得认了。而且她还是头一个看出这孩子跟他父亲像得出奇的人。伊莎贝尔为这句提点感激她；打那天起，没有哪个客人临走不连声称是：小约翰尼（Johnnie）活脱脱是大伍利的翻版。伊莎贝尔会细数儿子襁褓里的模样。伍利的鼻子当年正是这样。眼睛也是。这会儿她记得清清楚楚。这孩子把一切全给她带回来了。这套追忆她少不得一遍遍重演，因为品评娃娃、断定他身上家族的印记，在这些苏格兰人中间简直是一场仪典。一个女人自家没有六八个孩子，在这上头就难得出手不凡。男人们呢，哪怕是十三个孩子的父亲，明知自己在这场合要差得多远，大都尽量躲着走。

===Original===

Squire McLaughlin, of course, was just brazen enough to enjoy such a ceremony. He may have had some secret sympathy for Wully’s predicament, for he came over to inspect the child only a few days after it was born. The Squire was the playboy of the community. None of them ever took him seriously, and none failed to welcome him heartily in for a “crack.” It appears that even his absurd pretensions endeared him to his friends. He fancied himself a great lord, before an acre of his “estate” was subdued, and sang a silly song about gravel walks and peacocks. He never hauled a load of gravel to fill the mudhole before his cabin door. But he did the easier thing. He managed to have some gullible soul send him a pair of peacocks. They died promptly upon arrival. He said, laughing with the neighbors at himself, that it was the shock of seeing their laird barefooted that killed them. He was a farmer who rode forth to preside at theorizing agricultural meetings, while the forests of weeds on his land grew unchecked up to the heavens. (Even two years ago, the wild sunflowers near a culvert on that farm reached the telephone wires.) He was later on one of the first men west of the Mississippi to have purebred bulls, and east or west, no man confused pedigrees more convivially. From the first he considered it his duty to see that no Scottish folly was forgotten in the new world, or even Hogmanay allowed to pass unobserved. He was the man who all but popularized curling in the west. Three times he had been left an undaunted widower with a family of small, half-clothed children, his esteemed heirs and heiresses of only his gay fancies. Just now he was looking for a fourth helper to relieve him of the responsibilities of his family, and such a man he was that, in spite of his follies, all wished him success in the venture. He consulted Isobel about various possibilities and she gave him her opinion, with the frank statement that she pitied any woman who married him. However, he still liked her. He had always liked her since that time in Ayrshire, soon after she had married his older brother, when she had saved him from a long and well-earned term in prison for poaching. His successful pursuers were almost upon him when they turned suddenly in the wrong direction, from which they had just heard firing. She had seen his plight, and fired cunningly into the air, and when the men had rushed into her cottage they found only a young woman demurely sewing on baby clothes. Now since, of course, it was impossible to poach in a land where not even God preserved game, he was a reformed man, and an eminent huntsman. But sometimes he still said jovially that he might as well have gone to prison as to have to listen to all she said to him on that occasion. Even yet he was not averse to giving her occasions of finding fault with him.

So when she lifted the baby up for his inspection, he rose, and squinted down thoughtfully upon the little bundle. He turned his head appraisingly from one side to the other. Then, knowing very well what she thought, he said recklessly;

“He’s a perfect little McNair, Isobel. He’s like Alex. That nose of his—”

She enlightened him stoutly. He persisted in his error, and only asked:

“What’s he called?”

===Chinese===

乡绅麦克劳克林自然脸皮够厚，偏偏就受用这样的仪典。他对伍利的窘境，心里或许还暗暗存着几分同情，因为娃娃落地才几天，他就过来看过了。这位乡绅是社区里的活宝。没有谁拿他的话当真，也没有谁不热热络络把他迎进屋去“闲扯”（crack）一场。看来连他那些荒唐的做派，也替他在朋友中间挣下了人缘。他那片“庄园”还没开出一英亩来，他就自命为大老爷了，还唱一首傻里傻气的歌，什么砾石小径啦，孔雀啦。他自家木屋门前的泥塘，他可从没拉过一车砾石去填。他干了件省力的事：他想法子哄得哪个好骗的人给他送来一对孔雀。孔雀一到就死了。他跟邻居们一起笑自己，说那是孔雀看见它们的大老爷（laird）打着赤脚，惊吓死的。他是个农夫，却骑着马四处赴会，在坐而论道的农会上做主席，他地里的荒草却无人过问，一蓬蓬直长到天上去。（就在两年前，那座农场一处涵洞旁的野向日葵，还长得够着了电话线。）后来，他是密西西比河（Mississippi）以西最早养纯种公牛的人之一；而无论东西，谁也不如他那样兴高采烈地把血统谱系搅得一塌糊涂。打一开始，他就认定自己有责任不让任何一项苏格兰的荒唐玩意儿在新世界失传，连霍格马奈（Hogmanay，苏格兰人的迎新大年夜）也不许它悄没声地溜过去。把冰上掷石戏（curling）在西部推广开的，差不多就是此人。他三度做了鳏夫，却回回斗志不减，身边留下一群衣不蔽体的小儿女——全是他那些欢天喜地的空想的尊贵继承人和女继承人。眼下他正物色第四个帮手，好替他卸下养家的担子；他就是这样一个人，尽管荒唐，大家伙儿却全盼他这回成功。他找伊莎贝尔商量过好几个人选，伊莎贝尔给了他她的意见，还直截了当补了一句：谁嫁了他，她都替谁叫屈。不过他照旧喜欢她。打从艾尔郡那会儿起他就一直喜欢她——那时她刚嫁给他哥哥不久，救了他一命，免了他一桩罪有应得、要蹲上很久的偷猎牢狱。追他的人眼看就要得手，他们却忽然拐错了方向，朝刚听见枪响的那一头追去。她看见了他那副狼狈相，机灵地朝天放了一枪；那些人冲进她的小屋，只见一个年轻女人端端正正坐着，缝娃娃衣裳。话说回来，在一块连上帝都不保全猎物的土地上，偷猎自然无从谈起；他从此洗心革面，做了个赫赫有名的猎手。不过有时他还乐呵呵地说，与其听她那天数落他的那一大篇话，还真不如蹲监狱去。直到如今，他也还不惮于给她递由头，让她好挑他的错。

因此，等她把娃娃举起来给他验看时，他站起身，眯起眼睛，若有所思地打量那个小包袱。他把头评鉴似的从这边摆到那边。随后，明知她心里怎么想，他却冒冒失失地说了：

“他是个十足的小麦克奈尔，伊莎贝尔。他像亚历克斯。瞧他那个鼻子——”

她义正词严地开导他。他执迷不悟，只问了一句：

“他叫什么名字？”

===Original===

Now what to name the child was a question not altogether easy for Wully, who had been standing near his mother, looking with proper paternal pride upon the child. Each McLaughlin named his firstborn son, not boastingly, for himself, but gratefully, for his father; so that Johns and Williams came alternatingly down through the generations. That was the rub. Perhaps John McLaughlin might not relish having this irregular child bear his name. So Wully was too proud to seem to desire it.

“He’s such a husky little fighter for what he wants, we thought we’d call him Grant. There’s no better name than that, is there?”

His father was sitting by the stove, smoking, seeming as usual absorbed in a dream and only half-conscious of what was going on about him. At this he took his pipe from his mouth and said, without a sign of emotion;

“I wonder at you, Wully. The laddie’s name is John.”

Wully was greatly relieved.

“Oh, well,” he said lightly. “Maybe that would be better. There won’t be more than fourteen or fifteen John McLaughlins about in twenty years. Grant’ll keep. We’ll save it for the next one.”

===Chinese===

给这孩子取什么名字，对伍利来说原是个不十分好办的问题；他一直站在母亲身旁，带着恰到好处的为父的骄傲望着娃娃。麦克劳克林家的男人给长子取名，不是夸耀自己，而是感念父亲，用父亲的名字；于是一代传一代，约翰和威廉轮流往下叫。难就难在这里。这位约翰·麦克劳克林，兴许不见得乐意让这么一个不合体统的孩子顶他的名字。所以伍利出于自尊，绝不肯露出巴望的样子。

“这小家伙想要什么就拼死拼活地去争，壮实得很，我们想就叫他格兰特。再没有比这更好的名字了，是不是？”

他父亲坐在炉边抽烟，照例像沉在一场梦里，对身边的事只有一半在意。听到这里，他从嘴里拿下烟斗，不露一丝声色地说了：

“你倒叫我奇怪了，伍利。这小子的名字是约翰。”

伍利大大地松了一口气。

“哦，那也好，”他若无其事地说。“也许那样更好。过二十年，左近顶多也就再添十四五个叫约翰·麦克劳克林的了。格兰特留着不迟。留给下一个用。”

===Original===

Wully had rejoiced beyond measure at the child’s birth, not for the reason some supposed, but solely because Chirstie was safely through her ordeal. So gay he had become, so lighthearted, after that burden of anxiety for her had been taken from him, that he seemed quite like a rejoicing young father. It had been terrible for him to see her time unescapably approaching. Those days seemed to him now like a nightmare. He had planned what he would say to his wife when he adopted her baby for his own. He would go blithely in, and cry to her gaily, “Where’s my son, Chirstie?” And the child would be his. He had planned that. But it had been different. That one irrepressible moan he had heard from her before his mother had sent him for the doctor had driven him through the night cursing. Cursing that man, whose very name he hated to recall, cursing any man who lightly forced such hours upon any woman—to say nothing of a dear woman like Chirstie. He wanted to kill such men, to pound them to bits. And yet, lightly or not lightly, what would his love of her bring her to, eventually, if not to such hours as these! It was a hellish night. Afterwards he had gone in to see her, not blithely, but otherwise. He had found her lying there, hollow-eyed, exhausted, all her strength taken from her, and her roundness, leaving her reduced, it seemed, to her essential womanhood. And then suddenly he had not been able to see her for the tears that burned his eyes. He had knelt down beside her, to put his face near hers, so unseeing that she had cried sharply, “Don’t! Be careful!” He had hurt her! But her hand was seeking for his. When she had shown him the child—well he remembered that she had never asked him for pity for herself. But now her eyes were praying, “My baby! Love my baby, Wully!” With her lying there, even her familiar hands looking frail, her hair lying wearily against her pillow, if she had asked him to love a puppy, would he not have bent down to kiss it! Later he had marveled to see her with the child. A farmer, a man judging his very female animals by the sureness of their instincts for their young, he wouldn’t have wanted a wife not greatly maternal, he told himself. It came to be soon that in loving the child he was playing no role; he liked all his wife’s adornments.

So the terrible days passed away. His wife became altogether his. And wee Johnnie slept and thrived, his tiny hands doubled against his little red face, in the cradle that had served the five younger McLaughlins. When he opened his bonnie blue eyes, he saw only adoration bending over him. He felt only delighted and reverent hands lifting him. His grandmother, who “just couldn’t abide a house without a baby in it,” would sometimes allow one of her children, sitting carefully in just a certain chair, to hold him a little while as a mark of her favor. If Johnnie was a shame to the household, he was certainly an entertaining and a well-fed shame; if he was a disgrace, he was surely an amusing and a hungry one.

===Chinese===

娃娃落地，伍利喜得不知怎么才好，倒不是像有些人猜想的那个缘故，只因克丽丝蒂平平安安熬过了她那一关。压在心上的那副为她悬着的重担一卸，他变得那样快活，那样轻松，看上去十足就是个欢欢喜喜的年轻父亲。眼看着她的产期无可逃避地一天天逼近，那滋味对他真是熬人。那些日子如今回想起来，像一场噩梦。他盘算好了，等他把她的娃娃认作自己的孩子，要跟妻子说什么话。他要兴冲冲走进去，快快活活朝她喊：“我的儿子呢，克丽丝蒂？”这孩子就是他的了。他是这么盘算的。可事情全不是那样。母亲打发他去请大夫之前，他听见她那一声抑不住的呻吟，搅得他一整夜在外头咒骂。咒骂那个男人——他的名字伍利连回想都嫌；咒骂一切随随便便就把这样的时辰强加给任何一个女人的男人——更不必说强加给克丽丝蒂这样可疼的女人。他想杀了这样的男人，把他们捶成齑粉。可是话说回来，不管轻率不轻率，他对她的这份爱，到头来除了把她带进这样的时辰，还能把她带到哪里去！那是个地狱般的夜晚。后来他进去看她，没有兴冲冲，而是全然另一副样子。他见她躺在那儿，眼窝深陷，筋疲力尽，力气全叫抽干了，那份圆润也褪尽了，整个人仿佛精简到只剩最本色的女儿之躯。跟着，眼泪烧着他的眼睛，他忽然什么也看不见了。他在她身边跪下来，把脸凑近她的脸，眼睛朦朦胧胧，以致她尖声叫起来：“别！小心！”他弄疼了她！可她的手在寻他的手。她把娃娃抱给他看——他清清楚楚记得，她从没为自己的可怜讨过他一句怜惜。可这会儿她的眼睛在祷告：“我的娃娃！爱我的娃娃吧，伍利！”她躺在那儿，连他熟惯的那双手都显得单薄，头发倦倦地贴在枕上；这光景里，她就是要他去爱一只小狗崽，他难道不会俯下身去亲它！后来他看她带着孩子，心里暗暗称奇。他是个农夫，连自家的雌畜也要看它们护犊的本能有多可靠来评定；他对自己说，他总不至于想要一个缺少母性的妻子。没多久他就明白，他疼这孩子并非做戏；凡是妻子的妆饰，他无一不爱。

可怕的日月就这样过去了。他的妻子整个儿成了他的。小约翰尼睡得香甜，长得壮实，两只小手攥成拳头抵在红扑扑的小脸旁，睡的是那口先后伺候过麦克劳克林家五个小的的摇篮。他睁开那双好看的蓝眼睛，看见的只有一片崇拜俯向自己；感到的只有欣喜而虔敬的手把自己捧起来。他的祖母——就是那位“屋里没个娃娃就受不了”的祖母——偶尔会开恩，让她的一个孩子抱他一小会儿，算是一份殊荣；那孩子得规规矩矩坐在指定的一把椅子上。倘若约翰尼是这一家的耻辱，他起码是个逗人喜爱、喂得饱饱的耻辱；倘若他是桩丢脸的事，他准是桩有趣却挨饿的丢脸事。

===Original===

It was wonderful how completely Chirstie was sheltered from reproach. Though her humiliation was gossiped about by the hour, after all, the gossipers had to remember her mother, and, sighing, grant the daughter some little toleration. And then, however proud that Isobel McLaughlin might be, there was hardly a family in the community which had not, upon arriving from the old country, made “Uncle John McLaughlin’s” their convenient home till another could be built. Moreover, Wully had always been particularly indulgent to those who were his aunts and uncles. Greatest of all, he was a soldier. Not so far down the creek, a Quaker soldier had come home from war without a leg, and his congregation had said if only he would say, even privately, that he was sorry he had fought, he would again be received into their communion. But he refused to say he was sorry. And they refused to take him again to their approval. That didn’t seem to trouble the soldier very much. But it had troubled the Scotch, where he had come to work, extremely. They loved to belittle the Quakers for what they considered a meanness to a man who had fought. So it behooved them to treat their own veterans with more consideration. On the whole, there might have been much more gloating than there was. There might have been battles. Great, quiet, simple men like Wully, however, people seem instinctively to avoid exciting to fury.

So Chirstie had scarcely had occasion to feel the awkwardness of her position till the afternoon early in April when her stepmother came over with the finished dress to try on her. Chirstie had donned the beautiful, rich, wine-colored thing, to be sure it hung right, and set right, and standing forth so that Isobel McLaughlin might view the effect, she turned round and round while Barbara McNair smoothed out even imaginary wrinkles. It was pronounced perfect. Mrs. McNair admired it as if it were not her skill but the girl’s beauty that made the gown remarkable. Then, beaming, as much as her little pale weak face could beam, she unwrapped a hat—a hat all wine-colored and black, and set it jauntily on Chirstie’s head, so that the long feather swept down over the brown coil of hair low on her neck. Chirstie was radiant. She had never seen so lovely a hat in her life, she said. And she stood looking at herself in the little glass, in surprise, a very happy surprise, to see how she looked in such soft, rich things. Then, with a command, Barbara McNair took all the joy out of her face.

===Chinese===

说来奇怪，克丽丝蒂竟被遮护得滴水不漏，听不见半句责难。她的丢脸事固然叫人一个钟头一个钟头地嚼舌头，可嚼舌头的人毕竟得顾念她的母亲，叹口气，给做女儿的留几分容让。再说，伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林再傲，社区里也难得有哪一户人家，当年从故国过来，不曾把“约翰·麦克劳克林大叔家”当现成的落脚处，一直住到自家的房子盖起来。何况伍利素来待他的叔伯姑姨们格外宽厚。最要紧的是，他是个当过兵的人。沿溪下去不远，有个贵格派（Quaker）士兵缺了一条腿从战场回了家，他那个会的人说，只要他肯说一句——哪怕私下里说——他后悔打了仗，就重新接他回到会里。可他不肯说他后悔。他们也决意不再接纳他、认可他。那士兵自己似乎并不很在意。可他来做工的地方，苏格兰人却为此极其不平。他们最爱拿这件事贬损贵格派，说他们对一个打过仗的人行这样卑吝的事。所以他们待自家从战场上回来的人，理当多几分体恤。总的说来，幸灾乐祸本可以比实际的多得多；明枪暗仗也本可以打起来。不过，像伍利这样高大、沉静、质朴的人，人们出于本能，似乎都避免把他们惹到暴怒。

因此，直到四月头上那个下午——继母拿着缝好的衣裳过来给她试穿——克丽丝蒂几乎没有机会觉出自己处境的难堪。克丽丝蒂穿上那件漂亮、华贵、酒红色的衣裳，好试试它垂得正不正、着体不着体；她站在那里，让伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林端详效果，一边转了一圈又一圈，芭芭拉·麦克奈尔连想出来的褶子都要一一抚平。众口一词：十全十美。麦克奈尔太太赞不绝口，倒好像叫这件衣裳出众的不是她的手艺，而是姑娘本人的美丽。随后，她把她那张苍白羸弱的小脸所能绽出的全部光彩都绽了出来，解开一顶帽子——一顶酒红与黑色相间的帽子——俏皮地扣在克丽丝蒂头上，长长的羽毛顺势垂下，罩住她低低挽在颈后的棕色发髻。克丽丝蒂容光四射。她说，她这辈子从没见过这样可心的帽子。她站在那面小镜子前看自己，心里惊讶——一种欢喜得不得了的惊讶——看她穿上这样柔软华贵的东西竟是这样好看。随后，芭芭拉·麦克奈尔一道吩咐，把她脸上的欢喜一扫而空。

===Original===

She simply demanded that Chirstie wear that conspicuously beautiful outfit the second Sabbath to come, when the winter’s crop of babies was to be formally dedicated to the Lord. Chirstie went suddenly crimson, standing there, blankly, fingering the feather on her neck.

Mrs. McNair insisted on an answer.

“Oh!” cried Chirstie meekly, her eyes appealing to her mother-in-law. “Our baby—” she began to say it wasn’t to be baptized, but she had to turn away. She started for her room, to take the dress off.

The girl was so sensitive, Isobel started to say—But Barbara called after her to come back, breaking forth into the broadest Glasgow accent. They weren’t to suppose she didn’t understand! She had known it all the time. That innocent laddie had told her, unconsciously. (More innocent then than now, she might have added, if she had known.) And she thought, indeed, that Chirstie had great reason for shame, and not of her bonnie wee Johnnie, either, but of her own heathen ingratitude. Chirstie lifted her face upon hearing that, from the towel upon which she was wiping it, and Mrs. McNair demanded that moment if she expected the Lord to sit studying the almanac all the year for her convenience. She was sure that if she had been in Chirstie’s place, and the Lord had given her a son, she wouldn’t have gone sulking, no matter what the month might have been. Was it not better to have one any time than none at all? she demanded, with such a passion of regret for her own childlessness that Chirstie was left speechless. She had never imagined anyone speaking in such a strain. She looked at her mother-in-law, who seemed mildly amused. The idea that she had been deriding the Lord’s chronological calculations was in itself sobering to one of so tender a conscience. The giver of all her good clothes went scolding away at her, till she promised at least to wear the new things the week after the baptisms.

===Chinese===

她干脆下了道指令：隔周那个安息日，那一冬的娃娃们要正式献给上帝，克丽丝蒂得穿上这身招眼的漂亮衣裳到场。克丽丝蒂霎时涨得通红，怔怔地站在那里，手指捻着颈边的羽毛。

麦克奈尔太太非要个答复不可。

“哎呀！”克丽丝蒂怯怯地喊了一声，眼睛向婆婆求助。“我们的娃娃——”她刚要说不给他施洗，却不得不扭过头去。她朝自己屋里走去，要把衣裳脱下来。

“这姑娘脸皮太嫩——”伊莎贝尔才要开口——芭芭拉却喊她回来，满口迸出最浓重的格拉斯哥（Glasgow）腔。她们可别当她不明白！她早就一清二楚。那个天真的小伙子自己不知不觉告诉她的。（她要是知道后来的事，兴许会添上一句：那时候的他比如今还要天真些。）她还当真认为，克丽丝蒂大有理由羞愧，不过羞愧的不是她那可疼的小约翰尼，而是她自己那异教徒似的忘恩负义。克丽丝蒂听见这话，从擦脸的毛巾上抬起脸来，麦克奈尔太太当场就逼问：她莫非要上帝一年到头坐着翻历书，专拣她方便的日子不成？她拿准，换了她在克丽丝蒂的位置，上帝赐给她一个儿子，她才不会赌气呢，管它是哪个月份。什么时候有一个，总比一个没有强，是不是？她逼问道，那份痛惜自己无儿的激切，叫克丽丝蒂哑口无言。她从没想过有人会这样口气说话。她望望婆婆，婆婆倒像有点儿觉得有趣。一想到自己竟敢取笑上帝安排的时辰，她那样柔嫩的良心，单这一点就够她清醒过来了。那位给她所有好衣裳的人一路数落下去，直到她答应，至少在洗礼过后的那个礼拜天把新衣裳穿出去。

===Original===

Chirstie kept thinking of the scolding as she drove in the wagon of that harassed man, Alex McNair, with her stepmother and her mother-in-law, to see the new house that was getting about ready for her occupancy. Wully had to lay a plank for a walk hurriedly from the wagon to the house, for the new Mrs. McNair still wore such boots that one step in the thawing black mire would have ruined them. It was always that way. That little insignificant-looking person refused to adjust herself to the new country. She just sat tight, and let the great significant country adjust itself to her as best it might. The house towards which she neatly walked was not perhaps, to disinterested eyes, a very inviting place. But to Wully and Chirstie it was their very palace of love. It stood a story and a half high on a slight rise of ground, a decent way back from the path that has since become one of the nation’s highways, built of shining new lumber, the tall grass around it trampled into the black ground littered with bits of boards and yellow curling shavings. From the front door, just hung that day, the women looked down over fifteen miles of prairie, an occasional plowed square humanizing the distances, which sloped with so gentle an incline that one standing on any one of the acres could scarcely have told it was not level. From the windows of the parlor the women saw the plot that Wully’s father had insisted on breaking the year before, along one side of which the maple seeds he had planted were presently to appear as slight as spears of sprouting grass. From the kitchen window they saw a row of elms as thick as broomsticks, which Wully had brought the fall before from the creek. In a long furrow there, the walnut trees that were to make gunstocks for the World War were still waiting in their shells for a warmer sun to bring them forth, and to the north the trench was ready for the red and white pines that are nowadays a pride to the family. Chirstie pointed to the piece of ground that was to be fenced for a garden. Whereupon Mrs. McNair asked anxiously if the fence was to be painted white.

Wully heard his father-in-law move impatiently behind him, and, though he hadn’t before thought of such a thing, he answered that it would be painted white as soon as he had the money for the paint. The stepmother-in-law sighed with relief, and began inspecting the kitchen closet. Wully pointed out with malicious glee the finish of the cupboards, making light of the expense and difficulty of building, while his father-in-law poked about glooming, refusing to admire the conveniences which the little woman coveted with so gentle a simplicity. He still had a grudge against that man, and aired it whenever he could without Chirstie seeing him. He knew McNair disapproved of the size of the windows. But what business of that man’s was it what his windows cost?

The Sabbath of the Communion Wully unabashed, and shame-filled Chirstie wearing the appealing old coat of her mother, and the bedecked wee Johnnie went to church for the first time since the baby’s birth. But let no one suppose that they attracted much attention. What chance for consideration could even the most unholy child have had that morning, sitting in front of the Glasgow fashions in the person, or on the person, of his stepgrandmother? Wasn’t she wearing a most stunning little hat with a dark-green feather curling down over a chignon of red hair, sitting there in the pew just behind Mrs. McLaughlin, who wore with grace and satisfaction the bonnet a lamenting friend in Ayrshire had made for her in fifty-four, and just in front of Mrs. Whannel, whose headpiece was conceived in the spring of fifty-eight, and across from Mrs. McTaggert, who had bought somewhat more expensively than was necessary in sixty-one, but who, considering the well-preserved condition of her purchase, had really nothing to regret. One skilled in millinery might have reckoned from the mother’s bonnets more or less accurately, the year of each family’s immigration, although the array of such young girls as were not away at school would have slightly vitiated his calculations. And now, this Sabbath morning, there sits down in this world, so remote from others, a Metternich jacket, a cape-like affair trimmed with fur, and a skirt spreading gracefully, but without hoops, a floating veil, and gloves embroidered in faint gray! If wee Johnnie had been baseborn twins, he could never have attracted more than a stray thought to himselves on that occasion.

===Chinese===

克丽丝蒂坐着那忙得团团转的亚历克斯·麦克奈尔的大车，同继母和婆婆一道，去看那座眼看要拾掇好给她住的新房子，一路上脑子里还转着那顿数落。伍利得赶紧从大车到房门口铺一块木板当路，因为新麦克奈尔太太脚上那双靴子金贵，化冻的黑泥里踏一步就毁了。事情向来是这样。那个看着毫不起眼的小个子，偏偏不肯迁就这个新国家；她只是稳坐不动，任凭这个了不得的大国家尽力来迁就她。她端端正正走过去的那座房子，在无关的人眼里或许算不得一处多么可心的去处；可在伍利和克丽丝蒂眼里，那就是他们的爱之宫殿。房子立在一小块高地上，一层半高，离那条日后成了全国公路之一的小径端端正正退后一截，通体是新木料，亮闪闪的；四周的高草踩进黑色的土地，地上撒着碎木板和打着卷儿的黄色刨花。从前门望出去——那门是当天才挂上的——女人们俯瞰十五英里草原，间或一块犁过的方田给苍茫添上一点人气；草原的坡那样缓，站在任何一英亩地上的人几乎说不出它不是平的。从客厅的窗户，女人们看见那块地——去年伍利的父亲坚持要翻开的那块——地的一边，他种下的枫树籽眼看就要冒芽了，细得像刚出土的草茎。从厨房的窗户，她们看见一排扫帚把粗的榆树，是头年秋天伍利从溪边移来的。那里一条长长的犁沟里，日后要为世界大战造枪托的胡桃树，还裹在壳里等一个更暖和的太阳唤它们出土；朝北，沟也开好了，等着红松和白松——如今这已是这一家的体面。克丽丝蒂指着那块要围起来做园子的地。麦克奈尔太太立刻焦急地问，栅栏打不打算刷白。

伍利听见岳父在身后不耐烦地挪动，虽然先前想都没想过这码事，还是答道，一有钱买漆就刷白。他的继岳母放心地舒了一口气，开始查看厨房的碗柜。伍利带着促狭的得意，指点碗柜的漆工，把盖房的破费和难处说得轻描淡写；他岳父却闷闷地这里戳戳、那里捅捅，硬不肯夸一夸那小个子女人仰慕得那样天真温顺的种种便利。他对那个人心里的芥蒂还没消，只要能背着克丽丝蒂，他就要发作一回。他知道麦克奈尔嫌他窗户开得大。可他的窗户花多少钱，关那个人什么事？

领圣餐的安息日，伍利坦然自若，满心羞惭的克丽丝蒂穿着她母亲那件惹人怜惜的旧外套，打扮得花枝招展的小约翰尼，一家三口上教堂去——娃娃落地以来头一回。可谁也别以为他们招了多少眼。那天早上，格拉斯哥的时新装束就在他继祖母这个人身上，或者更确切说，就戴在她头上——连最不圣洁的娃娃，坐在这样的行头跟前，又能指望分到几眼关照呢？她头上不正顶着一顶叫人看直了眼的小帽子，深绿的羽毛弯弯地垂在红头发挽成的髻上，坐在长椅上，恰好在麦克劳克林太太身后——麦克劳克林太太端端正正、心满意足地戴着艾尔郡一位哀伤的友人五四年替她做的女帽；又在惠南尔太太跟前——惠南尔太太的头饰是五八年春天构思出来的；还同麦克塔格特太太斜对着——麦克塔格特太太六一年买帽子时花销略略超出了必要，可瞧那顶帽子保存得那么完好，实在也没什么可惋惜的。一个精于女帽行当的人，从各家太太的女帽上，多半能把各家移居来美的年份估个八九不离十——只是那些没出门念书的年轻姑娘们也排成一列，怕要让他的估算打点折扣。而如今，这个安息日的早上，在这片同别处远远隔开的世界上，端坐着一袭梅特涅式短外套（Metternich jacket）——斗篷似的一件东西，镶着毛皮——还有一条铺展得优雅大方却不带裙撑的裙子，一方飘飘忽忽的薄纱，一双绣着淡灰纹样的手套！就算小约翰尼是私生的一对双胞胎，在那个场合，落到他们身上的闲思也不会再多一星半点。

## XII / 第十二章

===Original===

Soon after the garments of Barbara McNair dawned upon the congregation, her husband bought three hundred acres of land at three dollars an acre. There are those who say a man owning eight hundred and forty acres of land should be happy. Alex McNair was not. There was in his flesh one great thorn﻿—that Glasgow wife.

She had lived through the autumn and the terrible winter, waiting for spring. And now that spring was here, what was it? Only an oozy wet waste, with patches of green in the lower places, and winds shrieking always across flat desolations. Near the sty, a sagging haystack of a barn, and a couple of bony cows trampling dead grasses deeper into the mire of the dooryard. If only there had been even a little white house, and a fence, and a few flowers sending up their endearing shoots! But this! And her from Glasgow!

Words failed her.

===Chinese===

芭芭拉·麦克奈尔（Barbara McNair）的衣裳在会众眼前一亮，没有多久，她丈夫便按每英亩三块钱的价钱买进了三百英亩地。有人说，一个人有了八百四十英亩地，就该心满意足了。亚历克斯·麦克奈尔（Alex McNair）却不然。他肉里扎着一根大刺——就是那位格拉斯哥（Glasgow）来的老婆。

她熬过了秋天和那可怕的冬天，一心等着春天。如今春天到了，春天又是什么？只是一片湿漉漉、水汪汪的荒滩，低洼处斑斑点点透出些绿色，风没日没夜地在平坦的荒凉上尖叫。猪圈近旁，一座塌着腰、活像大草垛的谷仓，两头瘦骨嶙峋的牛把枯草往门前的烂泥里越踩越深。哪怕有一座小白房子也好啊，有一道篱笆，有几丛花探出惹人怜爱的嫩芽也好啊！可偏偏是这个！她可是打格拉斯哥来的人！

她没话可说了。

===Original===

Had she not set forth day by day and hour by hour conscientiously, the necessity of a new house? Yet in the face of her demands, her man had gone to town to buy more wilderness. If she had known that spring that Dod was to sell part of that land for six hundred dollars an acre, her contempt for her husband’s folly would scarcely have been less hot. There he was, driving into the yard now! She went to the door and greeted him.

“You didn’t buy it?” she asked.

“Did I not say I would buy it?” he answered doggedly.

===Chinese===

盖新房子是多么要紧，她哪一天、哪个时辰不认认真真地摆给他们听？可面对她这些要求，她男人偏偏进城又买下一片荒野。倘若那年春天她就知道，多德（Dod）日后要把那块地卖出一英亩六百块的价钱，她对丈夫那份愚蠢的鄙薄，也未必就会冷几分。瞧，他这会儿正赶车进院子呢！她走到门口去迎他。

“地你没买吧？”她问。

“我不是说过要买吗？”他执拗地答道。

===Original===

Not a change of expression passed over her face. She stood watching him unhitch his team. She had never before been so much interested in that process, having always avoided the barn.

The next day, when he was in the field, and Dod was hitching up, she went out and watched him. Would he show her how he did that? she asked. She thought she ought to know, she said. Which were the gentlest horses? And which harness did they take? She learned where it all hung in the barn. Dod liked teaching an old person. It wasn’t any trick to hitch a horse to the wagon, he said. You put this under the belly, so. And the lines through here, taking them from here, thus. She practiced. She grew proficient. She waited.

One day in early May her husband rode away horseback to the Keiths’, to pay back one of the many days of labor he owed that family. He left home at daylight, and Dod went to school. Then Barbara began.

===Chinese===

她脸上一点神色也没有动。她站在那里，看他卸马。这一道工序，她从前从没有这样留意过——谷仓她向来是躲着不去的。

第二天，他下地去了，多德在套车，她走出去看多德套。能不能做给她看看这是怎么弄的？她问。她说，她想着自己也该懂一点儿。哪几匹马最温顺？套车使哪一副挽具？这一套都在谷仓哪里挂着，她也学了个明白。多德喜欢指点一个上了年纪的人。把马套上车，他说，一点儿巧劲都不用。把这个打肚皮底下兜过去，这么着。缰绳打这儿穿，从这儿接过来，这样。她练习。她练熟了。她等着。

五月初的一天，她丈夫骑马去了基思（Keith）家，去偿还他欠着那家人家的许多天工当中的一天。他天一亮就出了门，多德上学去了。芭芭拉便动了手。

===Original===

When McNair came home that evening, Dod asked, lonesomely,

“Where’s mother?”

“Is she not here?”

“She is not.”

“She’ll be gone to Chirstie’s. Or McCreaths’. Who came for her?”

===Chinese===

傍晚麦克奈尔回到家，多德孤零零地问，

“妈呢？”

“她不在这儿？”

“不在。”

“准是上克丽丝蒂（Chirstie）家去了。要不就是麦克雷思（McCreath）家。谁来接她的？”

===Original===

“She took the team and went herself.”

“You’re daft! Her take a team!”

But the team was gone. The barn was as empty as the house. Dod made a fire in the fireplace, and put the kettle on. Then the father made a discovery that the son had made some time ago. The cupboard was bare. Not a bite in it. Not a crumb of cake.

McNair didn’t like that. She might have told them where she was going. She ought to have come back in time to have the supper ready. He hated a cold house. He went to his tobacco box. At least that was always ready for a hungry man. He opened it, and found a strange white paper in it. A note from his wife. A fine note! “I can’t live in a sty,” it said. “I have gone back to Scotland. Jeannie is with Chirstie. Barbara Ferguson.”

===Chinese===

“她自己赶了那挂马走的。”

“你疯了！她还自己赶一挂马车！”

可那挂马确实不在了。谷仓空得跟屋子一个样。多德在壁炉里生了火，把水壶坐上。随后，父亲也发现了一桩儿子早些时候已经发现过的事。碗柜里空空如也。一口吃的都没有。连一星蛋糕渣都没有。

这可不是麦克奈尔乐意的。她要去哪儿，总该说一声。她也该赶回来做晚饭。他见不得家里冷冷清清。他去拿他的烟盒。那东西至少对一个饿汉子是随时现成的。他打开来，里头躺着一张陌生的白纸。是他老婆的字条。好一张字条！“我没法在猪圈里过日子，”上面写着，“我回苏格兰去了。珍妮（Jeannie）在克丽丝蒂那儿。芭芭拉·弗格森（Ferguson）。”

===Original===

Back to Scotland!

A woman alone!

Starting away with his team! She was daft! He rushed into the bedroom, as soon as he began to realize her meaning. Were her hat and cloak there? They were not! What was this? The kists not one on top of the other, as usual! Spreading all over the room! And empty! Nothing left in them! He rushed to the kitchen. The kist that set there was empty, too, more empty if possible than the others! He sat down.

He was outraged. He was speechless. That woman hadn’t been able to lift those boxes alone into the wagon, so she had taken all their contents and left them. Such cunning! Such deceit! And had he not paid all her passage from Scotland! She had left him! Left *him*, Alex McNair! Without saying a word! Her so quiet, and all! The whole clan would know all about it! They would all have seen her passing! A woman alone! Had anyone ever before heard of such a thing? Certainly not in those parts! Everybody wondering where his wife was off to! Oh, Jeannie would never have played him so base a trick!

===Chinese===

回苏格兰去！

一个女人家独自上路！

赶着他那挂马说走就走！她是疯了！他刚一回过味儿来，就冲进了卧室。她的帽子和斗篷还在不在？不在了！这是怎么一回事？那几口大木箱（kist）没有照往常那样一只摞在一只上头！摊了满屋都是！还是空的！里头什么都没剩！他冲进厨房。搁在那儿的那口大木箱也空了，如果还有可能，比那几口空得更彻底！他坐下了。

他气坏了。他说不出话来。那个女人独自一人根本抬不动那些箱子上车，所以她把里头的东西全数带走，箱子撂下了。好一条心机！好一场哄骗！她从苏格兰来的全部路费，难道不是他掏的！她撇下了他！撇下了他，亚历克斯·麦克奈尔！一个字也没有留下！她平日那么安静，居然这样！全族人都会知道得一清二楚！她打那儿路过，他们必定都看见了！一个女人家独自上路！这样的事，从前谁听说过？这一带自然更没有！人人都得纳闷他的老婆上哪儿去了！哦，珍妮是决不会对他使这么下作的手腕的！

===Original===

Dod came into the room. McNair stuffed the note hastily into the box.

“Your mother has gone to town,” he murmured, meekly.

Dod heard that with surprise. Presently he volunteered that he saw now why she had wanted to learn how to hitch up the horses. Had she indeed learned all that from him? his father gasped. Oh, the depth of deceit in her! And he had paid her way from Glasgow! Dod made disconsolate cornmeal for their supper, forgetting to put salt in it. To think of that woman ridding the cupboard of its last crumb! McNair went to the barn and pretended to work, after the meal, being too excited to sit still. Back to Scotland! Had ever anyone heard the like! Everyone would be laughing at him. A rich wife, indeed! Oh, he understood now why the canny widowers of Scotland had meekly let him take this jewel of a woman away to America. They must have known her!

===Chinese===

多德走进屋来。麦克奈尔慌忙把字条塞回盒子里。

“你妈进城去了。”他低声下气地咕哝道。

多德听了吃了一惊。过了一会儿，他自告奋勇地说，他如今明白她干吗要学套马了。她那些本事，果真是从他这儿学去的？他父亲倒抽一口气。哦，她心机藏得这样深！他可是付清了她从格拉斯哥来的路费的！多德闷闷不乐地做了玉米糊给他们当晚饭，忘了搁盐。想想吧，那个女人把碗柜里最后一粒渣都扫荡一空！吃过饭，麦克奈尔到谷仓去装模作样地干活，他心里翻腾得坐也坐不住。回苏格兰去！这样的事谁听说过！人人都得笑话他。真是好一位有钱的太太！哦，他如今明白了，苏格兰那些精明的鳏夫，干吗服服帖帖地让他把这个宝贝女人带走、弄到美洲来。他们必定是知道她的底细！

===Original===

There was but one thing to be done. He would rise early, long before dawn, and pursue her, getting out of the neighborhood before anyone would be awake to see him pass. Her with his good horses in the town, not knowing enough, maybe, to give them a drink at the end of the journey! If she ever imagined he would give her a cent to get back with, how greatly mistaken she was. He would surely show her who was master here.

He found her the next afternoon, in the hall of one of those long, shanty-like hotels which comprised the town, found her in the very act of making a bargain with a man to make her new boxes to take the place of those she had so extravagantly abandoned. They faced each other in her room, he, tall, gaunt, black-eyed, ragged, she, small, dainty, red-haired, bedecked. Her placidness, as usual, disarmed him. He began;

“You can’t go back to Scotland! Are you daft?”

“I canna’ live in a sty.”

===Chinese===

只有一样事情可做。他要早早起身，天不亮就走，去追她，赶在什么人醒来瞧见他路过之前出了这一带。她赶着他那几匹好马在镇上，兴许连走完这一程要给马饮水都不懂呢！她要是想过他会掏一个子儿给她做回去的盘缠，那她可是大错特错了。他定要叫她看个明白，这儿究竟谁是一家之主。

第二天下午他找到了她，在镇上那几座细长的、棚屋似的旅馆中一座的大堂里，正撞见她在跟一个男人讲价钱，要打几口新箱子，好顶替她那么大手大脚扔下的那几口。两人在她那间客房里面对面站定：他，高挑，瘦削，黑眼睛，衣衫褴褛；她，小巧，秀气，红头发，浑身穿戴。她那副平静劲儿照例先缴了他的械。他开了口；

“你不能回苏格兰去！你疯了吗？”

“我没法在猪圈里过日子。”

===Original===

They were off, then. He urged decency, morality, economy, honesty, pride, race, the waning reputation of Glasgow. After each argument she simply said, like one born foolish;

“I canna’ live in a sty.”

It was a deadlock, till he demanded angrily where she expected to get money for the journey. At her answer he surrendered. It fairly took the life out of him. She certainly had not expected to get it from him, thank you! She knew him too well. She had money enough with her to take her comfortably to her home in Glasgow. Did he suppose that she was one to come to the wilds without knowing how she might get back? She had kept it all﻿—all that *gold*, mind you!﻿—in the lining of her muff.

That woman had come thinking she might not stay! He, Alex McNair, had been, as it were, married on probation. And him a Presbyterian!

===Chinese===

于是两人就辩开了。他搬出体统，道德，节俭，诚实，志气，宗族，格拉斯哥日渐暗淡的名声。每听完一条道理，她只简简单单回一句，像个天生愚笨的人；

“我没法在猪圈里过日子。”

就这样僵住了，直到他气冲冲地逼问，她这趟路费打算从哪儿来。听了她的回答，他投降了。这一下当真把他浑身的气力都抽走了。她自然没指望从他这儿拿到，多谢！她把他知根知底。她随身带的钱，尽够舒舒服服回到格拉斯哥的娘家。他当她是不摸清退路就肯到荒野里来的人吗？她把那笔钱——是金子，记着！——全收在手筒（muff）的夹里。

那个女人来的时候，心里就想着兴许待不长！他，亚历克斯·麦克奈尔，等于是带着试用期结的婚。而他还是个长老会信徒呢！

===Original===

He asked hopelessly what kind of a house she wanted.

She replied promptly that she wanted three good big rooms downstairs, and two upstairs, a wee porch, all painted white, except the green shutters, with closets and windows like Chirstie’s and besides a wee white house for the fowls. All this was to be bought today, at once.

The Lord preserve us! Why, there wasn’t a painted fowl house in the state!

The train left for Glasgow at seven the night.

===Chinese===

他无可奈何地问，她要一座什么样的房子。

她立刻答道，楼下的好大房间要三间，楼上的要两间，一个小门廊，统统漆成白的，绿百叶窗除外，壁橱和窗子都照克丽丝蒂家的样式，另外还要一座小白鸡舍。这一切都要今天置办，立刻。

上帝保佑我们！嗐，全州也找不出一座上了漆的鸡舍来！

去格拉斯哥的火车今晚七点开。

===Original===

He couldn’t buy all that in a day, could he? He had no money!

He could sell the last great plot he had bought.

Was she daft? Did she suppose he could sell it in a day?

Why could he not sell it in one day? Hadn’t he bought it in one? She would call to the man to bring in those boxes.

He would buy the lumber as soon as he got around to it. Couldn’t she trust him to do it?

He hadn’t told her in the first place that he lived in a sty, had he? She felt the inside of her muff carefully.

===Chinese===

这些他总不能一天就置办齐，对不对？他没有钱！

他可以把他最后买下的那块大地卖掉。

她疯了不成？她当他能在一天里卖掉一块地？

他为什么不能一天把它卖掉？他不是一天就买下的吗？她会把那个人叫进来，把那些箱子搬进来。

木料他一腾出手来就去买。这一点她信不过他吗？

当初他可没告诉她自己住的是猪圈，是不是？她仔细摸了摸手筒的夹里。

===Original===

The next day in the dusk they drove into Wully’s together, having a wagon whose strange shape would have excited the curiosity of the most philosophical, with that same long, uneven thing all covered with blankets and tucked in, such a load as no man ever hauled, and plainly the same thing that she had taken with her the day before. McNair was apparently in a bad humor. How could the two who came out to welcome them in, know that the nearer he had got to his home, the more he dreaded the explanation he would have to give of his wife’s desertion. But he had not yet learned all the depth of that lintie! Was she embarrassed? Not she! She began immediately telling the news, in that hesitating, ingratiating way of hers. They were to have a new house! The lumber was to be hauled at once. She was that glad she hadn’t been able to wait for Alex, but had gone in ahead, to see about it. It was all settled. Just about like Wully’s, it was to be. But a little larger. With a white fence. And a wee white fowl house. They had bought even the paint. And, having had some time on her hands, she had found this wee pair of shoes for the baby. No, they couldn’t come in. Let Wully just hold wee Johnnie up till she would see if they were the right size. Out of that confounded muff came the shoes. They fitted. Well, the McNairs would just take their wee Jeannie and be going on. She had so wanted them to hear her good news. She hoped Jeannie hadn’t troubled Chirstie much. And wasn’t Johnnie just growing bonnier day by day!

What could a man do in the face of that? Where in the name of the shorter catechism had the woman got those shoes, and when﻿—after all the money she had wasted that day on houses? McNair simply gave up. Like the Queen of Sheba before Solomon, he had no spirit left in him. But he had acquired an uncomfortable amount of fear of women.

===Chinese===

第二天黄昏，两人一道赶车进了伍利（Wully）家的院子，拉着一辆形状古怪的大车，那副怪样子，就是顶沉稳的哲学家见了也免不了纳闷：车上又载着那条长长的、坑坑洼洼的东西，用毯子盖得严严实实、掖得整整齐齐——这样一副载，从来没有哪个男人拉过，分明也正是她前一天带走的那件东西。麦克奈尔显而易见情绪恶劣。出来迎接他们进屋的夫妻俩，哪里知道，他离家越近，越发害怕到时候要怎么解释他老婆的出走。可他还不知道那只小雀儿（lintie）的心机有多深呢！她难为情吗？她才不！她立刻就报起新闻来，还是那副吞吞吐吐、曲意讨好的腔调。他们要盖新房子了！木料马上就运。她欢喜得了不得，没能等亚历克斯，先自进城张罗去了。一切都定妥了。房子就照伍利家的盖。只是稍大一点。带一道白篱笆。还有一座小白鸡舍。连油漆都买好了。还有，趁着得空的工夫，她给小娃娃相中了这双小鞋。不行，他们不进屋坐了。就请伍利把小约翰尼（Johnnie）竖着抱起来，她好比一比尺寸合不合适。那双鞋就从那只恼人的手筒里掏了出来。正合脚。好了，麦克奈尔家这就带上他们的小珍妮，接着赶路了。她一心想叫他们听听她的好消息。她希望珍妮没有怎么麻烦克丽丝蒂。还有，小约翰尼不是一天长得比一天俊吗！

面对着这个，一个大男人还能怎样？那女人凭《小教义问答》（the shorter catechism）起誓，到底是打哪儿、又是什么时候弄来那双鞋的——她那天为房子糟蹋了那么多钱之后？麦克奈尔干脆认了输。就像示巴女王（Queen of Sheba）站在所罗门跟前，他一点脾气也没有了。只是打这以后，他对女人的惧怕，积攒下叫人很不自在的一大份。

===Original===

Chirstie and Wully took it for granted that the rich wife had paid for the house, until the next Sabbath. Therefore, when Wully heard as he came out of church that his revered father-in-law had sold part of his newly bought land to Geordie Sproul, in a panic so to speak, in a hurry, without much bargaining, to get the required funds for the lumber, he grinned to himself, and waited to hear his mother’s comment on the tale. He took his family as usual home to his mother’s, after the service, and when dinner was over, he had a chance to speak with her alone. She heard his pleasant suspicions. Doubtless the new wife had made him sell that land. And she chuckled with deep, deep mirth.

“Yon’s a fine woman, Wully!” she exclaimed, relishing her thoughts. “She’s a grand wee captain!” She heaved sighs of contentment from time to time all the afternoon, whose import was not lost on her son. Surely, late as it was, Jeannie was being avenged.

===Chinese===

克丽丝蒂和伍利本想当然地以为，那位有钱的太太自己掏了盖房子的钱，一直以为到下一个安息日。所以，当伍利从教堂里出来，听说他可敬的岳父把新买的地卖了一块给乔迪·斯普劳尔（Geordie Sproul）——可以说是慌了神，急急匆匆，没怎么讲价，只为凑齐买木料的款子——他暗自咧开嘴笑，等着听他母亲对这段新闻的议论。礼拜完了，他照例带着一家老小回他母亲家，饭吃完了，他才得着一个机会同她单独说话。她听了他那番叫人痛快的猜疑。不用说，是新媳妇逼着他卖了那块地。她呵呵地笑了，笑得那样深，那样畅快。

“那个可是个了不得的女人，伍利！”她感叹道，有滋有味地咂摸着自己的心思。“她是位顶呱呱的小统帅！”整个下午，她时不时舒舒服服地叹一口气，那叹气里的意思，她儿子没有不了然的。纵然迟了这些年，珍妮总算是申了冤、报了仇。

===Original===

Quite unconscious of the envious comment and the snickers of admiration which her house was causing among her neighbors, Barbara McNair went again with her husband to town, a month later, after the bluebells had faded in the creek woods, just when the wild roses were beginning to bloom, when the prairie was blue with spider lilies. She rode along arrayed like the lilies﻿—not to say like the twenty-eight colors of wild phlox which a Dartsmouth botanist records he found there that year. When at length she came within sight of the town which stirred Isobel McLaughlin so greatly to speculation, she speculated upon it not at all. There was nothing significant to her in a town of eleven real estate offices and nineteen hotels, wherein every other inhabitant was a land speculator. She left the main street without paying it the compliment of a thought, and turned toward the first street of dwellings, a muddy lane not worthy to be called a street. The further down it she went, the more homesick she grew, so bare and naked it was, shack after shack uncared-for﻿—wherever she turned, no gardens, no flowers, no trees, even in the year’s height of leaf and blossom. On she went, down one path after another. Then, away at the end of one﻿—Oh, there she found a little, unpainted vine-covered shanty, with color, with fragrance, iris blooming, borders of clove pinks, pansies, a yellow rosebush, a red one, grapevines in blossom, a honeysuckle, budding peonies!

It came over her with such delight that it never occurred to her to hesitate. She pushed open the gate, and followed the path of clove pinks around the house. There in the shade a woman was bending over her washtub, a large, fat uncorseted woman, who raised a red face from her steaming work.

===Chinese===

她那座房子正在邻里间惹起又眼红又佩服的议论和哧哧的笑声，芭芭拉·麦克奈尔对此一概不知，一个月后，她又同丈夫进了城。那时溪边林子里的蓝铃花已经谢了，野蔷薇正待开放，草原上蜘蛛百合开得一片蓝。她一路坐车前去，装扮得像那些百合——更不必说像那一年一位达特茅斯（Dartsmouth）的植物学家记下他在那里找到的二十八种颜色的野生福禄考。她终于望见了那座曾叫伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林（Isobel McLaughlin）大动投机心思的镇子，她对它却不曾动过半点心思。一座开着十一家地产行、十九家旅馆，居民里隔一个就是一个土地投机商的镇子，在她眼里没有一点了不得。她离开大街，没赏它一个念头，转向头一条住人的街——一条烂泥巷子，配不上‘街’这个称呼。越往里走，她越想家：那条巷子那样光秃，那样赤裸，一间棚屋挨着一间没人经管的棚屋——她放眼望去，没有园圃，没有花，没有树，哪怕正当一年里枝叶最茂、花开最闹的时节。她走了一巷又一巷。后来，在远远一条巷子的尽头——哦，在那里她看见一座小小的、没上漆、爬满藤蔓的棚屋，有颜色，有香气：鸢尾开着花，一畦畦的香石竹，三色堇，一丛黄玫瑰，一丛红的，开花正闹的葡萄藤，一架忍冬，含苞的芍药！

一阵大欢喜向她涌来，她压根儿没想到要踌躇。她推开院门，顺着香石竹夹道的小径绕到房子跟前。荫凉里，一个女人正俯在洗衣盆上，好大一个胖身量，不束胸衣，从热气腾腾的活计里抬起一张红脸。

===Original===

Barbara said to her positively and politely, moved to her broadest accent,

“I have come to see your flowers!”

The woman wiped her well-soaked hands on a limp apron, and replied in perfect Pennsylvania Dutch;

“I don’t understand you.” But she smiled a smile of extraordinary width.

===Chinese===

芭芭拉一字一板、客客气气地对她说，激动得带出了她顶浓重的乡音，

“我来看您的花来了！”

那女人在一条软塌塌的围裙上擦了擦湿透的手，用一口地道的宾夕法尼亚德语（Pennsylvania Dutch）答道；

“我不懂你的话。”可她咧开一张出奇大的嘴笑了。

===Original===

They faced each other, Scotland and Germany, curiously for one moment. Then Barbara pointed dramatically at the pansies. There was that look on her face that was understood by frontierswomen of many tongues. The German began babbling sympathetically about her display, pointing out one beauty after another, breaking off little sprays to hold near her visitor’s longing nose. So much there was that Barbara wanted to ask, and her hostess wanted to explain, and they understood each other after so many repetitions and efforts! Barbara examined each plant, and felt the soil it grew in. She bowed her face down to them again and again, hungrily. Not one did she omit to sigh over enviously. Presently the German led her into the shanty, and set before her in a red-carpeted, closely-guarded parlor, coffee and coffeecake, which Barbara esteemed but lightly, surprised out of politeness by the fact that on the kitchen table a pair of pigeons sat cooing. Then, the refreshments being finished, the woman took her by the hand, and led her out of the house, down a barren street, just as she was, in her wet dress, unhatted, red-faced. Barbara surmised she was being taken to a place where plants were sold.

===Chinese===

两人面对面，一个苏格兰，一个德意志，好奇地对望了一瞬。随后芭芭拉朝三色堇指了指，像演戏一样郑重。她脸上带着那种神气，操着各种语言的边疆妇人都认得它。那德国女人絮絮叨叨、满心热络地夸起自己这一园花来，一桩一桩美色指给客人看，还折下小小的几枝，送到客人那副馋羡的鼻子跟前。芭芭拉想问的有那么多，主人家想讲的有那么多，反复了多少遍、费了多少劲，她们到底彼此听懂了！芭芭拉一株一株细细看过去，连根下的土都摸了摸。她一次次把脸俯到花上，饿也似的。没有一株她漏掉不为它眼馋地叹息。过了一会儿，德国女人领她进屋，在一间铺红地毯、护得严严实实的客厅里，给她端上咖啡和咖啡糕。芭芭拉没把这些点心看得多重——厨房桌上一对鸽子咕咕叫着蹲在那里，把她惊得连客气都顾不上了。茶点用完，那女人拉起她的手，领她出了屋，顺着一条光秃秃的街走去，就那么一身湿衣裳，没戴帽子，红着脸。芭芭拉猜想，她这是要被领到一个卖花草的地方去。

===Original===

They came to a large square house, built on a high foundation, in a yard planted with trees which were not just small sticks, approached by a walk which had wide blossoming borders which Barbara would fain have examined. But her guide waddled up determinedly and knocked on the door. A lady opened it, a lady perhaps fifty, whose gray calico was fastened at the throat most primly by an oval brooch. She was sad-faced, and gray-haired, and as the German woman babbled to her, she turned and smiled upon Barbara gravely and kindly, and asked them to come in. But the German was not for sitting in a house on such a morning. The lady put on a wide hat, and gloves, and came out to the border. In her foreign language, which was merely New England English, she discussed her loves, pointing out one blossom and another. Her pansies never equaled the German’s. But look at the number of buds on her peonies! She could hardly wait till they opened. And Mrs. McNair followed her about with the great question on her tongue, namely, where does one get these things in this country?

She was standing by a yellow rosebush when she asked that, first, and its owner, bending down, said;

“Here’s a good little new one now. You may have that. Have you a place for it? Where do you live?”

===Chinese===

她们来到一座方方正正的大房子前，房子起在高高的地基上，院里栽着树——可不是些细枝条，门前一条小径，两边宽宽的花境开着花，芭芭拉恨不得停下细看一番。可她的向导一摇一摆，执意走上前去敲门。开门的是一位太太，五十上下，灰印花布衣裳，领口端端正正用一枚椭圆胸针别住。她面容愁苦，头发灰白，德国妇人同她絮叨的时候，她转过脸，朝芭芭拉庄重而和善地笑了笑，请她们进屋。可这样的早晨，德国人不肯坐在屋里。那位太太戴上宽檐帽和手套，出来到了花境边。她用她的‘外语’——其实不过是新英格兰英语（New England English）——谈起她心爱的花草，一朵一朵指给人看。她的三色堇从来比不过那德国女人的。可是瞧她芍药上的花苞！数一数都叫人等不及了。麦克奈尔太太跟在她身边转，舌尖上顶着一个大问题，就是：在这个地方，这些东西上哪儿弄去？

她头一回问这话的时候，正站在一丛黄玫瑰旁边，花的主人俯下身去，说；

“这儿正好有一棵新栽的好苗。这棵你可以拿去。你有地方栽它吗？你住在哪儿？”

===Original===

“Twenty-five miles west.”

The lady sighed.

“We have come for wood to build our house today,” Barbara informed her.

“Have you been here long?”

“Long enough,” said Barbara, simply. “I came in November.”

===Chinese===

“往西二十五英里。”

那位太太叹了口气。

“我们今天是来买木料盖房子的。”芭芭拉告诉她。

“你来这儿久了吗？”

“够久了。”芭芭拉淡淡地说。“我是十一月来的。”

===Original===

The lady sighed again, and went to get her spade. She asked again if Barbara had a place for the rose. Barbara was offended at the suggestion she might not cherish that plant until death. Where can you buy them here? she asked again.

That rose, the lady explained, she had brought with her from Davenport, in a little box with grape cuttings and the peony, which she had carried in her lap in a covered wagon long before there were railroads to the town. She had brought it to Davenport coming down the Ohio and up the Mississippi soon after she was married. A woman had given it to her when she left Ohio for the West. The peony her mother had brought from eastern to western Ohio many years ago, and when she had died, her daughter had chosen the peony for her share of the estate. Her mother had got it from her mother, who came a bride to Ohio from western New York, clasping it against her noisy heart, out of the way of the high waters her husband had led her horse through, across unbridged streams, cherishing it more resolutely than the household stuffs which had to be abandoned in pathless woods. Her great-grandfather had brought it west in New York in his saddle bag, soon after Washington’s inauguration as he returned from New York City. She supposed before that the Dutch had maybe brought it from Holland to Long Island. There had been tulips, too, but the pigs had eaten them in Ohio. She had wondered sometimes if it was the fate of the peony to be carried clear to the Pacific by lonely women. At least, if she gave a bit of it to Mrs. McNair, it would be that much farther west on its way to its destination, which she, for one, hoped it might soon reach, so that there would be some rest for women. Let Mrs. McNair remember to come for a root of it in the fall, when her fence would be finished. Without fences it is useless to try to protect flowers. Her mother in Ohio had had a sort of high stockade made of thorny brush around a little garden, so that one had to come near, and look down over the top to get a glimpse of the blossoms. But the pigs had been very hungry in those days. Their destruction of that garden and the rescue of the peony she had heard her mother tell about with tears in her eyes twenty years afterwards. It was one of the sorrows of her life.

===Chinese===

那位太太又叹了一口气，去拿她的铁锹。她又问了一遍，芭芭拉有没有地方栽那棵玫瑰。那话的意思仿佛她不会把这棵花疼爱到死，芭芭拉觉得受了冒犯。这儿上哪儿买得着这些花？她又问了一遍。

那棵玫瑰，那位太太解释道，是她从达文波特（Davenport）带来的，装在一个小匣子里，同葡萄插条和那棵芍药作伴，那时候通镇上的铁路还早着呢，她是坐在帆布篷大车里把它们抱在膝上运来的。她刚结婚不久，顺俄亥俄河（Ohio）而下、溯密西西比河（Mississippi）而上，把它带到了达文波特。花是一位妇人送给她的，那时她正离开俄亥俄往西部去。那棵芍药，是她母亲许多年前从俄亥俄东部带到俄亥俄西部的；母亲去世的时候，女儿把芍药挑去，作自己名下分得的那份家产。她母亲又是从外祖母手里得来的；外祖母当年做新娘从纽约西部来到俄亥俄，一路把它紧紧搂在扑扑跳动的心口上，躲着大水——她丈夫牵着马蹚水驮她过的那些没有桥的河——它比那些不得不丢在无路荒林里的家什，受着更坚决的呵护。再往前，是她的曾祖父在纽约州把它往西带，装在鞍囊里，那时华盛顿就任总统不久，他正从纽约城回来。她想，再往前，兴许是荷兰人把它从荷兰带到了长岛。郁金香也是带了的，可在俄亥俄让猪给吃掉了。她有时纳闷，芍药的命，是不是就该让孤单的妇人们一路抱到大平洋边去。至少，她分一枝给麦克奈尔太太，它离目的地就又往西近了一程；至于那目的地，就拿她自己来说，是盼着它早日到达的，好叫妇人们也有个安息的日子。请麦克奈尔太太记着，秋天篱笆扎好了来分一段根。没有篱笆，想护住花是白搭。她母亲在俄亥俄的时候，曾叫人用带刺的枝条在小花园四围扎起一种高高的栅栏，人得走近，从顶上探头张望，才看得到里头的花。可那些年猪饿得很。猪毁掉那座园子、芍药又如何被抢救出来，她听母亲讲过；事隔二十年讲起来，母亲眼里还含着泪。那是她一生的隐痛之一。

===Original===

When Mrs. McNair went home that day, she had with her the roots of all transplantable things, lilacs, white and purple, roses pink and red and yellow, pinks and young hollyhocks, grape cuttings and snowballs. She had a pile of old *Horticultural Advisers* from the lady’s library, full of advice about planting windbreaks, and letters from frontier gardeners who had morning glories growing over their young pines, and walls of hollyhocks twelve feet high. She had been urged to stay at the lady’s for dinner, and the German had made her promise always to come back to her for coffee when she came to town. The road was full of ruts and swamps, and her bones ached long before the springless wagon got home. But her plants had felt no joltings, for she had held them carefully in her lap. That was the first day she sang in the United States of America. It was her “Americanization.” Her husband never even noticed her song, however. He was suffering acutely from the price of glass windows.

===Chinese===

麦克奈尔太太那天回家的时候，带走了各样能移栽的花木的根：丁香，白的紫的；玫瑰，粉的红的黄的；石竹，还有小蜀葵；葡萄插条，还有雪球花。她还带着一摞从那位太太书房里借来的旧《园艺顾问》（Horticultural Advisers），满纸是栽防风林的门道，边疆园艺师们的信里说，他们叫牵牛花爬满自家的小松树，蜀葵长得像十二英尺高的花墙。那位太太再三留她吃了饭，德国女人又叫她许下愿，往后进城一定回她那儿喝咖啡。一路上道上车辙纵横、泥沼处处，没有弹簧的大车还没到家，她的骨头早颠疼了。可她的花草没受一下颠簸，因为她一直小心地把它们抱在膝上。那是她来到美利坚合众国以后头一天开口唱歌。这就是她的‘美国化’。不过，她的歌声她丈夫压根儿没有留意。他正为玻璃窗的价钱痛心疾首呢。

## XIII / 第十三章

===Original===

Wully and Chirstie and their bonny wee Johnnie moved into their new house towards the first of May, and at the end of that month, Wully’s brother John, having finished his second year in the snug little New England college, came to work for him. That institution was only fifty miles away, a distance that a lame McLaughlin, unfit for the army, walked to vote for Lincoln in sixty-four, not being able to give one great big valuable dollar for the hire of a horse. John himself walked when his sister Mary’s company didn’t necessitate a wagon. Having John at Wully’s suited the whole family. His mother liked it because Wully was such an excellent example of patience and goodness for John, who needed just that. Chirstie liked it not only because she was spared the unpleasantness of having a strange hired man at the table, but because she saw in John the first of a succession of younger brothers, to whom, as they worked for Wully, she might in some degree repay their mother’s kindness to her. Wully heartily admired John, and never neglected to point out the signs of his brilliancy to those who were interested, especially his mother. There was no one like John in the family, and therefore, of course, in the community, in Wully’s estimation. The books which the other children in the little school studied ragged, John glanced at, and mastered. He never had anything to read, because the few books that Wully went slowly through, he read in an hour or two, getting more out of them in that fashion than Wully could in his. He had read every printed thing in the neighborhood: the books Wully had sent home from St. Louis, most of Scott, and some of Dickens, and Macaulay’s histories. (“You understand that no stolen book comes into my house, Wully!” his mother had written him, enraged by the boys’ stories of war plunder.) He had read those three hundred pious volumes that the governor of an eastern state had sent to the library of a Sunday school near by, in which he had become, in so romantic a manner, interested. He had read the college library from start to finish, and the more precious books his interested teachers would lend him. His teachers thought sometimes that John was to have a great career. But they were all amateurs in expectations, compared to his mother.

===Chinese===

五月初前后，伍利、克丽丝蒂和他们漂亮的小约翰尼搬进了新居；到那个月底，伍利的弟弟约翰在那所安适小小的新英格兰学院念完第二年，来给他干活了。那所学堂离这儿只有五十英里——六四年，一个不中用去当兵的瘸腿麦克劳克林，舍不得拿出一大枚值钱的银元去雇马，就是靠两条腿走完这五十英里，去投了林肯的票的。约翰自己也是走着去，除非姐姐玛丽同行，才用得上马车。让约翰住在伍利家，合乎全家的心意。母亲喜欢这样，因为在伍利身上，约翰能得着一个耐心与善良的绝佳榜样，而约翰恰恰缺这个。克丽丝蒂喜欢这样，不只是省下了饭桌旁坐着生雇工的那份不自在，还因为她在约翰身上看见了接踵而来的小叔子们中的头一个——趁他们一个个来给伍利干活，她好多少报偿些他们母亲待自己的恩情。伍利打心眼儿里钦佩约翰，从不放过向任何感兴趣的人——尤其是他母亲——指出他天资出众的种种迹象。依伍利看，家里没有第二个约翰，因此，不用说，邻里间也没有。小学堂里别的孩子们翻得卷了边的那些课本，约翰瞟上一眼，便都弄通了。他手里从来没有多余的书可读，因为伍利慢慢啃了许久的那么几本书，他一两个钟头便读完了，这样读法，收获反倒比伍利多得多。左近但凡印成字的东西，他全都读遍了：伍利从圣路易斯（St. Louis）寄回家的书，司各特（Scott）的大半，狄更斯（Dickens）的一部分，还有麦考利（Macaulay）的几部史书。（“你记住，伍利，我家里不许进一本偷来的书！”他母亲写信给他这样说，孩子们讲回家的战利品的故事把她气坏了。）他还读完了那三百册虔敬的藏书——那是东部某州的州长，以一段风流佳话式的机缘，对近旁一所主日学的图书馆上了心，才送来的。学院图书馆他从第一架读到最后一架，那些关心他的教师们也肯把更珍贵的书借给他。教师们有时觉得，约翰日后必有一番大造化。可比起他母亲来，他们这些人在指望这门功夫上全都是外行。

===Original===

John had two very good reasons for wanting to work for Wully. The first was that at Wully’s he could study all the Sabbath day in peace, which he was not allowed to do at his father’s. To be sure, he was still expected to appear at church, which he did but seldom, and then only with great groans and complainings. Wully told him it wouldn’t hurt him to rest his mind an hour or two once a week, and he retorted that after a week in the field, rest was the thing his mind needed least. He scolded about his father’s intolerance. Wully only grinned at him, and remarked that he couldn’t see that the father was much more intolerant than the son. However, if John was seized with a pain on the morning of the Sabbath, Wully wouldn’t minimize his agony when his father inquired about it.

The other reason that John liked being with his brother was that there he could be sure of being paid. The summer before he had hired out to a Yankee at Fisher’s Grove, for twelve dollars a month, payable in gold. He had endured food inexcusably bad, even for those circumstances, and when he had asked for his wages the man had given him, shamefacedly enough to be sure, instead of gold, one hundred and twenty acres of land! John had been barely seventeen at the time and it was years before he acknowledged that in his disappointment he had gone to the woods and cried bitterly. He could afford to tell that story with amusement when there was a town of forty thousand on that land, and he still owned most of it. That year his father had with much difficulty got a deed to the land, and mortgaged it for a little to help with the boy’s schooling. He and his sister, living together on cornmeal carried from home, and working for their room rent for the kindly New Englanders with whom they lived, needed, fortunately, only a little cash. But this next year John was going to Chicago to study law. That was what the teachers advised and that would take real money.

===Chinese===

约翰愿意到伍利手下干活，有两条十足正当的理由。头一条是，在伍利家，他可以安安静静读一整个安息日的书，这在父亲家里可不容许。当然，做礼拜还是得去，他却难得去，去也必是哀声叹气、满腹抱怨。伍利跟他说，一星期里让脑子歇上一两个钟头，碍不了他什么；他回嘴说，在地里干了一个礼拜，最不必歇的恰恰就是脑子。他数落父亲不容人。伍利只朝他咧嘴一笑，说他看不出做父亲的比做儿子的不容人到哪里去。不过话说回来，安息日早上约翰若是叫什么疼痛缠住了，父亲问起来，伍利是决不肯把他的苦处往小里说的。

约翰喜欢跟着哥哥的另一条理由是，在这里他的工钱是有准儿的。头年夏天，他在费舍尔格罗夫（Fisher’s Grove）给一个北方佬打短工，讲定月钱十二块，付金币。那家的伙食坏得说不过去，就算按当时的境况也是坏得离谱；等他讨工钱，那人给他的是一百二十英亩地——不是金币，羞倒真羞得可以！约翰那时才刚满十七岁，过了好些年他才肯承认，自己失望之余跑到树林里痛哭过一场。等那片地上立起了一座四万人的镇子，而那镇子底下的大半地产还握在他手里，这段故事他才讲得笑呵呵了。那一年，父亲费了好大的周折才把那块地的地契弄到手，押出去小小一笔，帮衬孩子的学业。他和姐姐住在一处，吃着从家里背去的玉米面，给同住的好心新英格兰人干活抵房钱，幸而花不了几个现钱。可转过年，约翰要去芝加哥（Chicago）读法律了。这是教师们都劝的路子，而那是要花实打实的大钱的。

===Original===

It was one of those interested teachers who unknowingly changed the order of worship at Wully’s that season. One morning, when breakfast was over at dawn, John’s first week there, as Wully reached for The Book, he said in a voice which seemed, as usual, a little impatient, somewhat too eager;

“Let me do the reading, Wully, and you do the praying!”

Wully was rather surprised by such devotion on John’s part.

“All right,” he said, handing him the book.

===Chinese===

这一季，在伍利家不知不觉改了礼拜规矩的，正是这样一位关心他的教师。一天早上，天刚亮早饭就吃罢了，约翰来这儿才头一个星期，伍利伸手去拿那本《圣经》，他用那听来照例带点不耐烦、有些过于心急的嗓音说：

“读经我来，伍利，祷告你来！”

约翰这等虔敬，倒叫伍利吃了一惊。

“行啊。”他说，把书递给了他。

===Original===

John began abruptly at the first of Isaiah, which was not the place according to the custom of their fathers, and he read stumblingly, with pauses, so that his brother, turning toward him, saw that he was looking at the text only for occasional phrases, trying to read from memory. And when they sat around the table again, in the evening, almost stupid from weariness, John went over the same chapter, but with scarcely any hesitation. Wully asked him, after prayers, why he had repeated it. John had just picked up the lamp to go up to bed—he had the one lamp, because he studied—and he turned at the bottom of the stairs to answer, the light flickering across his neck, where his hickory shirt collar was open. He was six feet, even then, and he had huge broad shoulders strangely awkward. His head was long and narrow, and though he was blistered red just then from the sun, his untanned forehead was a clear yellow, unlike any other complexion in the family. He had the long upper lip that spoiled the symmetry of so many McLaughlin faces, and a long determined chin, and from his deep-set blue eyes he stood gazing at his brother with that speculating keenness with which he examined even the most familiar things.

===Chinese===

约翰开门见山，从《以赛亚书》（Isaiah）的开头读起——这地方可不合祖辈的老规矩——读得磕磕绊绊，时有停顿；哥哥扭头看他，只见他只是偶尔才瞧一眼经文，是在凭记忆往出背。到晚上，他们又围着桌子坐下，累得几乎发懵的时候，约翰又把同一章过了一遍，这回却几乎毫无迟疑。祷告完了，伍利问他为什么把这一章重复一遍。约翰刚拿起灯准备上楼去睡——灯归他拿，因为他要用功——他在楼梯口转过身来答话，灯光在他脖子上晃动，粗布衬衫的领口敞着。那时他就有六英尺高，肩膀又宽又大，笨拙得出奇。脑袋窄而长，虽然那阵子叫太阳晒得通红，没晒着的前额却是一种清亮的黄，一家人里再没有第二副这样的皮色。他长着那片叫许多麦克劳克林家的脸失了匀称的长上唇，下巴颏长而坚决；那双深陷的蓝眼睛，正带着一种掂量的锐气盯着哥哥——他打量哪怕最熟悉的东西，用的也是这种眼光。

===Original===

“Professor Jamison advised me to learn Isaiah this summer. He said it would be a good thing to get the swing of the sentences. We might as well get *some* good out of worship, I suppose.”

“Commit Isaiah to memory!” gasped Wully.

“Well, why not? We know most of it now, don’t we? We’ve heard it all our lives. I told him we knew the Psalms. We’ll read a chapter twice a day, and we’ll know it.”

“I won’t,” said Wully.

“You’ll know enough of it,” said John, starting up to his reading.

Wully gave Chirstie a significant look.

===Chinese===

“詹米森（Jamison）教授劝我这个夏天把《以赛亚书》学下来。他说，把那些句子的文气摸顺了，大有好处。做礼拜嘛，总不能白做，总得从里头得着点儿好处才是，我想。”

“把《以赛亚书》整个背下来！”伍利倒抽一口气。

“怎么不行？咱们如今大半都记得，不是吗？打小听到大嘛。我告诉他，诗篇咱们都会。每天把一章读两遍，慢慢就都会了。”

“我可不。”伍利说。

“你总会记得够用的。”约翰说，上楼念他的书去了。

伍利朝克丽丝蒂递了个意味深长的眼色。

===Original===

“Did ever you hear the equal of that?” he asked her. “I wouldn’t know that chapter if I read it every day for a month.” He considered John. It would not have been his father’s way to use the few minutes of the day set apart for the worship of the Most High God, to learn the swing of sentences, whatever that might be. It certainly would not have been Wully’s own way. But it was John’s way, and doubtless a good way, and since John was living with them, he might as well have his way. Chirstie didn’t mind. She only wanted John to be happy.

===Chinese===

“那样的高论，你可听人说过第二回？”他问她。“那一章，我就是一天读一遍，读上一个月，也记不住。”他端详着约翰。拿一天里拨给至高上帝敬拜的那么几分钟，去学什么句子的文气——管它文气是个什么——这绝不是父亲的做派，自然更不是伍利自己的做派。可这是约翰的做派，想必也是个好做派；约翰既然跟他们住在一起，不妨就依他。克丽丝蒂倒不在乎。她只盼约翰快活。

===Original===

They *were* happy as the summer wore on, the three of them working from the first streak of dawn to the frog-croaking darkness. The stars in their courses and the clouds in their flights seemed to be working with them that season. Week after week, just as the ground grew ready for it, they watched the desired clouds roll up in great hills against the sky, and pour down long, slow, soaking rains. They watched the sun grow more and more stimulatingly warm, and then, just when their corn needed it, grow fiercely hot in its coaxing. They worked like slaves, of course. But then, they had always worked like slaves. Wully was at the height of his strength that year, apparently, and he tried to save John, who was, after all, still a growing boy. But John sharply refused to be considered less than any man. Chirstie was cruelly tired every night, with far too much fever. She had her new house to keep as clean as her mother’s linen-hung cabin had been. She had more than a hundred little chickens to feed and water, and to guard from the slow-rising storms, and the low-hovering hawks. She had an orphan lamb to feed. She had washing to do, and ironing, and scrubbing and sewing and cooking, bread making and butter making, with pans and pails and churns to be scalded and kept sweet; she had yarn making, and knitting, vegetable drying and wild fruit canning. She had wee Johnnie to care for, and whenever she sat down to nurse him, she fell asleep worn out. More than one pie got itself scorched that way that summer.

===Chinese===

夏天一天天过去，他们三个当真是快活的：从天边第一线晨光，一直干到蛙声聒噪的夜色。那一季，连运行的星辰、飞渡的云朵，都像是与他们一同做工。一个星期接一个星期，地里的墒情刚到要的时候，他们就眼望着盼的云在天边堆成一座座大山，落下又绵长、又缓慢、浇得透透的雨。他们眼看着太阳一天比一天暖得提神，待到玉米正需要，又在它那番哄劝里狠热起来。他们干活自然像牛马。可话说回来，他们一向都像牛马一样干活。那一年伍利的力气正当盛年，样子上看得出来；他想法子照应约翰——说到底，约翰还是个长身体的半大小子。可约翰斩钉截铁，决不肯让人把他当作不如哪个大人。克丽丝蒂每夜都累得狠了，烧热也发得太凶。她那座新房子，得收拾得同她母亲那间挂满亚麻布的小木屋一样干净。一百多只小鸡要喂、要饮水，还要提防慢慢压过来的风暴和低低盘旋的老鹰。一只没娘的羊羔要喂。要洗衣裳，要熨，要擦洗，要做针线，要做饭，要和面烤面包、打黄油；锅盆桶罐、搅乳器都要烫过，存得清甜不坏；还要纺线，要织毛活，要晒干菜，要罐装野果。小约翰尼要照看，她一坐下给他喂奶，人就已经乏得睡着了。那年夏天，这样烤糊的馅饼就不是一张两张。

===Original===

And with it all, they were so happy that sometimes she had to say to Wully, although he didn’t want her to mention it, “Oh, think of last summer, and of this!” And he would answer, “*I* certainly had a time without you, Chirstie!” Everything seemed to swell the sum of their well-being. Every noon, if the dinner was not entirely ready when Wully was washed for it, he seized his spade and transplanted two or three little trees from their seedbed to their place in the windbreak. Every evening, tired to death, with the baby in his arms, he went with his wife to see if by chance any seedlings had halted, and needed water. Every leaf on the little trees called for comment. There they would stand, looking over their domain, brushing mosquitoes from their faces. Wheat and corn had surely never grown better than theirs did that year. To John, now, a field of wheat was a field of wheat, capable of being sold for so many dollars. To Wully, as to his father, there was first always, to be sure, the promise of money in growing grain, and he needed money. But besides that, there was more in it than perhaps anyone can say—certainly more than he ever said—all that keeps farm-minded men farming. It was the perfect symbol of rewarded, lavished labor, of requited love and care, of creating power, of wifely faithfulness, of the flower and fruit of life, its beauty, its ecstasy. Wully was too essentially a farmer ever to try to express his deep satisfaction in words. But when he saw his own wheat strong and green, swaying in the breezes, flushed with just the first signs of ripening, the sight made him begin whistling. And when, working to exhaustion, he saw row after row of corn, hoed by his own hands, standing forth unchoked by weeds, free to eat and grow like happy children, even though he was too tired to walk erectly, something within him—maybe his heart—danced with joy. Therefore he was then, as almost always, to be reckoned among the fortunate of the earth, one of those who know ungrudged contented exhaustion.

===Chinese===

尽管如此，他们快活得那样，有时她竟忍不住要对伍利说——虽然他不许她提——“哦，想想去年夏天，再看看今年！”他就会答道：“没你在身边，我那日子可真叫够呛，克丽丝蒂！”似乎样样事情都在给他们的福气添码。每天晌午，伍利洗好了手等饭，饭菜若还没全齐，他就操起铁锹，把两三棵小树从苗床移到防风林带的位置上去。每天傍晚，累得半死，抱着娃娃，他同妻子一道去看看，是不是有哪棵苗恰好停住了不长，需要浇水。小树上的每一片叶子都值得议论几句。两人就站在那里，望着自己这片领地，赶着脸上的蚊子。那一年，他们的小麦和玉米，敢说从来没有人种得比这更好。对如今的约翰来说，一片麦田就是一片麦田，能卖上多少多少块钱。对伍利呢，如同对父亲一样，头一条自然还是长着的庄稼里有钱的指望，他也确实缺钱。可除此以外，那里面还有更多的东西——也许谁都说不出，至少他从没说出过——正是那些叫庄稼人心心念念守着庄稼的东西。那是挥洒了的劳作得着报偿的完满表征，是爱与操心得着回报的表征，是创造之力的表征，是妻子忠贞的表征，是生命的花与果、生命的美与狂喜的表征。伍利骨子里是个农夫，从不曾试着用言语去表达那份深切的满足。可当他看见自家的小麦青壮，在微风里摇曳，刚泛起将熟的头一层颜色，那景象就叫他吹起口哨来。而当他干到筋疲力尽，看见一垄又一垄亲手锄过的玉米挺立着，没有被杂草憋住，像快活的娃娃一样放开来吃、放开来长，哪怕他已经累得直不起腰，他身体里的什么东西——也许是心——也在欢欢喜喜地跳舞。所以他那时，正如几乎一贯的那样，算得世上有福之人里的一个，算得那些深知毫无怨尤、餍足而疲惫的人里的一个。

## XIV / 第十四章

===Original===

John came out for a three months’ vacation the next year and worked again for Wully. They had acres of sod corn that summer, and wheat to make a miser chuckle. Both men, and whatever neighborly passerby they might be able to hire, worked day after day till they staggered. To have stopped while yet there was sufficient daylight to distinguish another hill of corn would have been shirking; to go to supper while yet one could straighten up without a sharp pain in his back would have been laziness. Yet John was never too tired to choose an idiom as far removed as possible from the one he heard about him. Now that he had been in Chicago he had a growing contempt, which never failed to amuse Wully, for the speech of his own people. What was it they spoke, he demanded scornfully, swinging a violent hoe among the weeds. It was Scotch no longer. It wasn’t English. It wasn’t American, certainly. It was just a kind of﻿—he tried all summer to describe it satisfactorily in a word. Once he called it “the gruntings of the inarticulate forthright.” Mrs. Alex McNair was the only one that spoke pure anything, he declared. John seemed to like that woman, strange to say. Wully suspected he listened to her because her pronunciation fascinated him, but at Wully’s he was intolerant of any tendency towards Scotticisms. Wully’s and Chirstie’s articulation he supervised continually, their grammar and their diction.
