搜寻就这么组织起来了。人们分成几队。一队人要冒险探进几处最可能出事的洼地深水里去。一队往奥布赖恩那面的树林里搜——彼得见了酒总想再来一口，醉眼昏花之中，说不定就拐向了那个方向。还有一队去搜溪边的矮树丛。伍利挑了往溪去的那一队，一半因为那条道会打他父亲家门前过，他急于叮嘱克丽丝蒂，无论受什么撩拨，眼下都不许说出她知道的事；一半因为这样走，他能离他姑妈最远。他觉得，脚下那片坚实忠厚的土地整个儿塌了下去，他想攀住的——只是个空。那个女人，他姑妈，抢在他前头，把他本该得的同情收割了个干净。如今他杀了彼得，乡邻心里就只装得下她的悲伤了。谁也不会想到，那个被逼到杀人这一步的人，自有他站得住的道理。这一切，不知怎么，透着一股叫人咽不下的不公。乡绅对搜寻这样不上心，倒叫伍利闷声得了些安慰。他一路走，一路咕哝抱怨非来不可。伍利对他是心里发热的，却不知道乡绅的声色之欲，同天下男人的欲念一样，总得同母性交战不休——而那母性，便是莉比·基思。伍利得着空私下盘问过约翰，可没问出更多的东西。连克丽丝蒂也说不出个所以然。“他看上去病了吗？”伍利焦急地逼问她。“他醉了，不是吗？”她避开了这个问题。“哦，别问我！”她喃喃道。“他就是看着我——看着我！”

===Original===

The men spent all day in the more unfathomable menaces. The women searched back and forth about the Keiths’ house. The two miles between that house and the corner, back and forth, up and down that road, they beat persistently and prayerfully, until the little path of the day before was a great riverbed of trodden muddy grass hiding nothing. They searched all impossible places; through the Keiths’ and McCreaths’ and McTaggerts’ barns they went again and again. Peter hadn’t disappeared out of existence. He was somewhere. Likely somewhere between the house and the corner. They went over that path continually till their children began to cry for supper.

The men stopped not even to eat. Let the women and the children do the chores. Let them go undone. Steaming and weary and excited, they went on with their hunt till the sun set, till the last glimmer of twilight was gone. Now none was as persevering as the Squire. The hunt had become for him the greatest game of his maturity. One by one in the darkness the men had at length to ride home to their waiting families, with no news. Strange things they had to think on, places in the swamps where they had not been able to touch bottom, places where the rushes grew rank and thick with scarcely space enough for nest of the crying waterbirds—stretches with no sign of a lost man, and no hope for one losing himself. …

===Chinese===

男人们整天整日泡在更深不可测的凶险里。女人们绕着基思家的房子来回搜寻。从那房子到拐角的两英里地，来来回回，沿路走上走下，他们执着地、近乎祷告一般地拍打搜寻，直到头一天的那条小径成了一条踩得稀烂的泥草大河床，什么也再藏不住。一切不可能的地方他们都搜遍了；基思家的、麦克雷思家的、麦克塔格特家的谷仓，他们一遍又一遍进去搜过。彼得并没有从这世上消失。他总在个地方。多半就在房子和拐角之间的什么地方。那条小径他们不停地过了一遍又一遍，直到自家的孩子哭喊着要晚饭。

男人们连停下来吃口饭都不肯。杂活让女人孩子做去。做不完就随它去。他们热气腾腾、疲惫不堪、心火灼灼，搜寻个不停，直到太阳落山，直到暮色的最后一丝微光熄灭。这时候，谁也不如乡绅那样锲而不舍。这场搜寻于他成了壮年岁月里顶大的一场游戏。黑暗里，男人们终于一个接一个不得不骑马回家，回到等消息的家小身边，什么消息也没有。他们不得不去想的，净是些叫人心头发凉的事：沼泽里竿子探不到底的处所；芦苇长得又旺又密、啼哭的水鸟几乎寻不出一块筑巢之地的所在——一片一片，不见失踪人的半点踪迹，也不给迷路人半点生望。……

===Original===

At the Keiths’ Isobel McLaughlin in Peter’s bed in the kitchen was lying praying. Except his mother, no one prayed as fervently for Peter’s safe return as Isobel. All that she asked of the Almighty was that Peter might be found alive and well enough to take the shame away from her good innocent Wully. If Peter was brought home dead—how then ever, in the face of Libby’s grief, could she say that the beloved was a scoundrel! How could she ever endure not saying it? That would be too bitter a dose for her. Let God not give her that cup to drink! If fervency could have brought an answer to prayer, how quickly would Peter have appeared!

Her passionate hope had been some consolation to Libby, who so little understood the reason for it. Libby was lying down in her room, not because Isobel had besought her to, but because she was no longer able to stand up. Isobel wanted to get some rest, but she couldn’t leave off her praying to God, the good Father. She hoped Libby might sleep till morning.

===Chinese===

基思家，伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林躺在厨房里彼得的床上祷告。除了他母亲，再没有人像伊莎贝尔这样热切地祷告彼得平安归来。她向全能的上帝所求的只有一件事：叫彼得活生生地给找着，好好儿的，好把那层羞耻从她那无辜的好伍利身上揭走。要是抬回家来的是彼得的尸首——当着莉比的哀恸，她怎么还能开口，说那宝贝是个恶棍！可不说，她又怎么受得住？那一剂药对她来说太苦了。求上帝别把那一杯递给她喝！倘若热切换得来祷告的应允，彼得早就现身了！

她那满怀炽烈的指望，倒给了莉比几分安慰——莉比实在太不明白这指望从何而来。莉比躺在自己房里，不是因为伊莎贝尔苦苦哀求，是因为她再也站不住了。伊莎贝尔想歇一歇，可她撇不下她对上帝、对仁慈天父的祷告。她盼着莉比能一觉睡到天亮。

===Original===

But the moon rose after midnight, and with the first flicker of its light, Libby came out of the bedroom, tying a skirt about her. Isobel sat up in bed.

“There’s moonlight now,” said Libby. Even from the doorway, where she stood in the darkness, Isobel could hear her breathing.

“Lie down, Libby!” she implored.

===Chinese===

可午夜过后，月亮升起来了。月光头一闪动，莉比就从卧房里出来了，一面往身上系裙子。伊莎贝尔在床上坐起身。

“这会儿有月光了。”莉比说。她站在门口的黑地里，伊莎贝尔连她的呼吸声都听得见。

“躺下吧，莉比！”她央告道。

===Original===

“I mind wee Jennie Price,” said Libby.

“Ah, Libby!” protested Isobel, shrinking from the mention of such poignancy. Jennie Price was the six-year-old who had been lost in the grasses, wandering from her home some twenty miles down the creek, a year or two ago. What but that had all the women been thinking of all the day and shrinking from mentioning.

Libby was groping about for her shoes which she had left in the kitchen.

===Chinese===

“我还记得小珍妮·普莱斯（Jennie Price）。”莉比说。

“哎，莉比！”伊莎贝尔抗声道，这样锥心的话，她连听都不敢听。珍妮·普莱斯就是那个六岁的小姑娘，一两年前在深草里走失，从家里出来，顺着溪往下游去了约莫二十英里。这一整天，女人们心里翻来覆去想着的，嘴上躲着不敢提的，不正是这件事么。

莉比正伸手四下里摸她的鞋——鞋落在厨房里了。

===Original===

“Just near home, Isobel! Forty yards from her mother’s door.”

“You can’t go out by night, Libby. You can’t stand up!”

“Crawling towards home, it may be.”

===Chinese===

“就在离家不远的地方，伊莎贝尔！离她母亲的门口才四十码。”

“你不能夜里出去，莉比。你站都站不住！”

“说不定是爬着朝家去呢。”

===Original===

“Libby! Libby!” cried Isobel, getting up. Forty yards from home they had found the girlish skeleton the next spring, in a place a hundred men would swear in court they had sought through dozens of times. The mother herself had come upon it. Had the child been stolen away for some evil purpose, and flung back later to die? No one would ever know.

“The wee bones were all white, Isobel!”

“Spare us, Libby! Peter’s a man grown!”

The women went out calling down the road together. At dawn, when John McCreath came out to milk, while yet the stars were shining, he heard Libby calling hoarsely, “Lammie! Lammie! Your mother’s coming!”

===Chinese===

“莉比！莉比！”伊莎贝尔喊着，从床上起来了。翌年开春，人们找着那具小姑娘的骸骨，离家四十码，在一处一百个男人上法庭起誓也说自己搜过几十遍的地方。是那做母亲的自己撞见的。是不是有人怀着什么歹念把孩子拐了去，后来又抛回来等死？谁也永远不会知道。

“那些小骨头全都是白的，伊莎贝尔！”

“饶了我们吧，莉比！彼得是大人了！”

女人们一齐出了门，顺着大路呼唤而去。天快亮的时候，约翰·麦克雷思（John McCreath）出来挤奶，星星还在天上闪，他听见莉比哑着嗓子一声声唤：“小羊儿！小羊儿！你妈妈来了！”

## XVII / 第十七章

===Original===

By that time men were beginning to gather again—middle-aged men on horseback, stiff from years of toil, bearded great young men with dogs at their heels, large-boned, ruddy, gaunt, rugged of face like Lincoln, overgrown boys, and boys of the very smallest size which fearful mothers could be persuaded to let go into possible danger—they came walking or riding towards the Keiths’ for thirty miles away. The younger ones were sent on horseback to spread the news along all the roads towards town, even along obscure untraveled paths that led to the cross-state coach road to the north. In the morning council Wully had again ventured to suggest that Peter had of his own accord gone back to the place from which he had so mysteriously come. Again they all refused to consider his suggestion. Was it likely a man should return without a glimpse of those he had come so far to see? The whole thing was baffling. It seemed beyond belief that no one had seen him come. That could have happened only on such a day as the Fourth, when all the settlers were away from home. Wully wondered to himself, grimly, however, why, if Peter had managed to come once, unperceived, he would not be able to come again as slyly. He didn’t see that to tell what he knew would ease the situation. And he had no intention of telling it if he had proof that it would have ended the search. He would tell that tale only to justify his making Chirstie safe from violence. He felt strangely distant from those whose eagerness to help increased with each glimpse they got of Libby Keith. At his father’s bidding he went again with a party to search the creek underbrush.

===Chinese===

到那时候，男人们又开始聚拢来了——骑在马上的中年汉子，叫多年的苦活压得腰背发僵；络腮胡须的高大后生，脚边跟着狗，骨架粗大，面色红润，瘦骨嶙峋，一张脸同林肯一样粗粝；长得人高马大的半大小子；还有顶顶小的那档子男孩——是提心吊胆的娘们儿好歹被劝得松了口，才容他们去冒一点可能有的危险。方圆三十英里，他们或步行或骑马，朝基思家聚过来。年轻些的被打发骑上马，沿条条通往镇上的大路传信，连那些通往北边横贯全州的驿车大道的荒僻无人走的小径也都跑到了。早上商量的时候，伍利又大着胆子提出：彼得是自己甘愿回他神秘来处去了。大家又都不肯把他的话当回事。一个人大老远奔来，连要见的人一面都不照就回去，有这种可能吗？整件事叫人摸不着头脑。没有人看见他来，这简直叫人难以相信。这种事只可能出在独立日那样的一天，那时移民们都离家在外。不过伍利心里冷冷地纳闷：既然彼得能神不知鬼不觉地来上一回，为什么就不能一样鬼祟地再来一回。他不觉得把他知道的事说出来能让局面松快些。就算有凭有据，证明说出来能让搜寻收场，他也无意说。只有为了证明他让克丽丝蒂免遭暴行是应当的，他才会把那件事讲出来。那些人每见莉比·基思一面，帮着找的热心就添一分；他同这些人之间，倒生出一种说不出的隔膜。他父亲一声吩咐，他又跟着一伙人去搜溪边的矮树丛。

===Original===

From morning till noon they went on fighting their way through the impenetrable briary wall of green, stopping only for breath at the water’s edge, scratched, mosquito-bitten, baffled, exhausted. Once John and Wully happened to get to the bank at the same moment, and John, stooping down to wash his face, said to his brother, carefully lowering his voice;

“I wouldn’t be at all surprised if you are right, Wully. It would be just like Peter to have to leave some place suddenly, in some scrape. I think it probable, after all, that he had started on short notice for the west, and passing O’Brien’s, was unable to resist the smell. He wouldn’t even have had the decency to go to see his mother if he had been within half a mile of the house!”

Wully said nothing to this, but it comforted him to know how low John’s opinion of Peter was. He could work with new energy after that. At noon the ten of them stopped at the nearest house for dinner.

There was not a woman in the neighborhood who would not have been glad to set dinner before a party of searchers. Not a woman who had not been frightening her little ones more carefully about wandering into the tall grass, such helpless slight persons, with that tall menace always waiting at hand for them. Marget McDowell had all the morning been looking from time to time down the road, hoping to see a horseman coming with good news. But no news came. She served the men. They ate in silence, hungrily. Having finished, they went out and lay down in the shade of the house. Most of them slept. Davie McDowell sat next to Wully, smoking vile homegrown tobacco in a stern old pipe. Beyond him Geordie Sproul went on theorizing in a lullabying voice. Wully was half asleep himself when he heard him saying;

===Chinese===

从清早到晌午，他们一直在那堵密不透风的绿色荆棘墙里奋力开路，只在到了水边才停下喘口气，一个个被抓得一道道口子，咬得满是蚊子包，泄气，精疲力竭。有一回，约翰和伍利恰巧同一刻到了岸边，约翰俯下身去洗脸，小心地压低了嗓音对兄弟说；

“要说你是对的，我一点儿也不会意外，伍利。彼得这个人，本来就有可能忽然从什么地方拔腿开溜，闹出什么乱子来。我倒觉得，说到底很可能是他匆匆忙忙就动身往西去了，打奥布赖恩酒馆门前过，那个味儿他顶不住。就是离家不到半英里，他也不见得有那份脸面进去看看他娘！”

对这话伍利什么也没说，可知道了约翰把彼得看得这样低，他心里踏实了些。那之后他干活又有了新的劲头。晌午，他们十个人在最近的一户人家停下吃午饭。

邻里没有哪个女人，是不乐意把一顿饭端到一队搜寻人面前的。也没有哪个女人不曾把自家小的吓唬得更严实些，叫他们别往深草里乱跑——那样毫无招架之力的小人儿，那高高的凶险就守在近旁等着他们。玛格特·麦克道威尔（Marget McDowell）一上午不时朝大路那头望，盼着看见一个骑马的人带来好消息。可是没有消息来。她伺候男人们吃了饭。他们闷声不响，吃得又急。吃完了，他们出去躺在房子的荫凉里。多数人睡着了。戴维·麦克道威尔（Davie McDowell）挨着伍利坐，用一只老派严肃的旧烟斗抽着劣质的自种烟草。再过去，乔迪·斯普劳尔（Geordie Sproul）用一副催眠曲似的嗓音继续讲他的道理。伍利自己也是睡意朦胧了，听见他说；

===Original===

“If we knew the girl to ask, we might learn something.” “Girl” when he pronounced it, rhymed with peril. He was a canny man, Geordie, and Wully was instantly awake.

“Hoots!” replied Davie. “He was never one to run after girils!”

“Was he not!” answered Geordie. His voice was so suggestive, so leering, that Wully sat up.

“It’s one o’clock!” he hastened to announce. “We ought to be going on!” He woke all the lads up. They started by twos and threes back towards the creek.

===Chinese===

“要是知道该去问哪个姑娘，兴许能打听出点什么来。”“姑娘（girl）”这个词打他嘴里念出来，音竟同“危险（peril）”押上了韵。乔迪是个精明人，伍利霎时全醒了。

“呸！”戴维答道。“他从来就不是跟在姑娘儿们后头跑的人！”

“他不是么！”乔迪答道。他这口吻里的暗示太多了，斜睨的意味太浓了，伍利霍地坐了起来。

“一点钟了！”他赶忙宣布。“咱们该接着往前去啦！”他把小伙子们全都叫醒。他们三三两两动身，回溪那边去。

===Original===

Wully might easily have asked Geordie privately what he meant by that comment of his. But he didn’t dare. Was it possible that Geordie, that unconsidered man, knew anything about Chirstie? Or about Wully McLaughlin’s private affairs? He must have meant something, and Wully wanted intensely to know what it was. Doubtless Davie McDowell would presently be inquiring, for gossip’s sake. But Wully assured himself that if Geordie really knew anything about the truth of the matter, he would never dare to tell it. Nor would he have dared to hint before Wully that he knew it! Only—would he not dare? Men dared strange things, nowadays, it seemed! Even cowards like Peter Keith! They seemed to think Wully McLaughlin a soft, easygoing man. They would speedily find out their mistake! They would get rid of the idea that he was a man with whom one might safely take unspeakable liberties. If only he might have the fortune, the one chance in a thousand, or ten thousand, to come upon that damned snake, lying somewhere hidden. … Exhausted, sore in muscles and mind, he went on through the breathless thicket.

===Chinese===

伍利本可以轻而易举私下问问乔迪，他那句怪话究竟是什么意思。可他不敢。难道乔迪这么一个从来没人放在眼里的家伙，会知道克丽丝蒂的什么事？还是会知道伍利·麦克劳克林家的私事？他那话一定有所指，伍利急切地想知道到底是什么。毫无疑问，过一会儿戴维·麦克道威尔就会去打听，图个嚼舌头的材料。可伍利让自己相信，乔迪要是真知道事情的内情，他绝不敢说出去。他也绝不敢当着伍利的面暗示自己知道！只是——他果真不敢吗？如今这世道，男人什么出格的事都敢做，看样子！连彼得·基思那样的孬种都敢！他们好像把伍利·麦克劳克林当成一个软和、好说话的人。他们会很快发现自己看走了眼！他们会趁早丢掉那个念头：别当他是个可以任人放心大胆施以难言放肆的人。只求老天让他撞上那份运气，千遭难逢、万遭难逢的一遭，撞见那条天杀的毒蛇，正藏在什么地方躺着呢……他筋疲力尽，肌肉酸痛，心口也痛，继续在那叫人透不过气来的密丛里往前走。

===Original===

At four he came again to the water’s edge, and saw Chirstie’s brother Dod just coming out from a swim. He threw himself down under a great linden tree for a rest, and under his hand he saw Dod’s hat full of choice blackberries. Dod was undoubtedly preparing to make himself as comfortable as possible. He was weary enough to defy the world, and relinquish his pretenses of being a man. He made his decision known flatly.

“I’m not going back into that!” he announced. “I’m through!” It was plain that his swim hadn’t cooled his temper much.

Wully repressed a smile. Dod was extremely thin. The ridges of his ribs showed under his skin, which gleamed white and wet in places, in vivid contrast to his tanned arms and neck, and he was stepping along gingerly to avoid thorns, lifting his bony legs high. One of his eyelids had been scratched so that his eye was swollen shut.

“You’ve done enough,” said Wully. “You’ve got a bad eye there!”

===Chinese===

四点钟，他又到了水边，看见克丽丝蒂的弟弟多德刚游完泳上来。他一头倒在一棵大椴树底下歇息，手边看见多德的帽子，满满装着拣好的黑莓。多德分明在打算把自己安置得怎么舒服怎么来。他累得可以不把世人放在眼里，撂下“自己是个男子汉”的那副撑劲了。他把自己的决定直通通地宣布出来。

“我不回那鬼地方去了！”他宣布。“我不干啦！”显而易见，游这一泳并没有把他的火气消掉多少。

伍利忍住了笑。多德瘦得厉害。一根根肋骨在皮下显出棱来，那皮有几处泛着白亮的湿光，同他晒黑的胳膊和脖颈成了扎眼的对照；他小心翼翼地择着路走，好躲开荆棘，细骨头腿抬得高高的。他一只眼皮叫抓破了，眼睛肿得睁不开。

“你干得够多了，”伍利说。“你那只眼伤得不轻！”

===Original===

The boy struggled wet into his shirt and overalls and stretching out near Wully, began dividing the berries. Wully had to notice, how men’s zeal to help Libby Keith vanished as she grew distant. In her presence, in the presence of Motherhood itself, so to speak, they were shamefaced and eager, deploring their helplessness, as men are while their wives labor in childbirth. But away from her agony, they forgot … as men do after labor is over … and turned again to their own comfort. Dod broke the silence surprisingly.

“Chirstie’d be glad if he *was* dead!” he said, resentfully.

“Why, Dod!” exclaimed Wully.

“She would that! She hates him!”

===Chinese===

男孩湿淋淋地费劲套上衬衫和背带裤，在伍利身旁伸直了躺下，开始分那些黑莓。伍利不能不注意到，男人们帮莉比·基思的热心，随着她离得远了，也就消散了。当着她的面——可以说，当着母性本身的面——他们又羞又急，哀叹自己无能为力，同女人们分娩阵痛时男人们的光景一样。可一离开她的苦痛，他们就忘了……同苦痛过去以后的男人一样……又转身顾自己的安逸去了。多德出人意料地打破了沉默。

“他要真是死了，克丽丝蒂才高兴呢！”他恨恨地说。

“哎呀，多德！”伍利嚷道。

“她就是会！她恨他！”

===Original===

“He’s your cousin, lad!”

“He’s as much your cousin as he is mine! She can’t endure the sight of him!”

Wully sat up. He looked at Dod. He had thought of him always as a child. He was a big, tall boy now. Fourteen years old he was, and doubtless able to put two and two together. How much did he know? He must have heard people talking. Wully suddenly wondered why he had not always been afraid of Dod. To be sure, he had always been careful to keep on the good side of his little brother-in-law.

“He never done us any good!” Dod spoke vindictively.

===Chinese===

“他是你表哥，小伙子！”

“他是你表哥，同是我的也一样！她见了他一眼都受不了！”

伍利坐了起来。他看着多德。他一向拿他当个孩子。如今他是个高高大大的男孩了。他十四岁了，分明能把二二添作四，看出些门道来了。他知道多少？人们说话，他准听见过。伍利忽然纳闷，自己怎么从来不怕多德。诚然，他向来小心，总顺着这位小舅子的心思走，不叫他挑出错来。

“他就没给咱们带来过一星半点好处！”多德恶狠狠地说。

===Original===

Now what could he mean by that? Wully was getting excited. Why had the boy so great a resentment against Peter, instead of against him, Wully, under the circumstances? Dod’s sudden and apparent preference for Wully at once grew odious to him. Dod had chosen that morning to work with Wully. He was always choosing to work with him. Why? It seemed unaccountable to him that he had never been suspicious of the lad before. Wully dared not say to him;

“Well, he never did you any special harm, did he?” Suppose Dod would blurt out what he knew! He said, confusedly;

“Look here, Dod. You oughtn’t to talk that way! Not at this time, I mean—you can’t speak ill of the dead, you know.”

“I ain’t said half the truth!”

===Chinese===

这话他究竟是什么意思？伍利激动起来。在这样的情形下，这男孩对彼得的怨气怎么这样大，倒不冲着他伍利来？多德突然明白地表露出的对伍利的偏袒，顿时叫他厌恶起来。那天早上是多德自己拣着同伍利一伙干活的。他一向总拣着同他一伙。为什么？他从前从来没有提防过这小子，如今想来简直说不通。伍利不敢对他说；

“这么说，他没特别害过你，是不是？”万一多德把知道的事脱口说了出来呢！他慌乱地说；

“听着，多德。你不该这样说话！我是说，在这个当口——你知道，人死了，不能说死人的坏话。”

“我连一半的实话都没说呢！”

===Original===

“You know how Aunt Libby feels!” Wully urged stupidly. “And Chirstie wouldn’t like you to say that—not now, you know—”

“Old fool!” commented Dod. Undoubtedly he was meaning his aunt. Wully couldn’t approve of such sentiments in one so young.

“You ought to go home and get something put on your eye!” he began, hastily. “And if you feel like working in the morning, you come back with me again!”

Dod went away, unsolved and uncomforting. Hour by hour the seekers, conquered by fatigue and the growing assurance of futility, stopped more often for breath. They had time to gather more and more berries, from bushes which obviously hid no dying man. They refreshed themselves more and more frequently in waters wherein no drowned man was floating. Most of them went home in time for their neglected chores that night, discouraged, hopeless.

===Chinese===

“你知道莉比姑姑（Aunt Libby）心里是什么滋味！”伍利笨嘴拙舌地劝。“克丽丝蒂也不愿意你这样说话——不是在这个时候，你知道——”

“老糊涂！”多德评了一句。无疑他说的是他姑姑。这样年纪的孩子存这样的心思，伍利不能赞成。

“你该回家去，眼睛上弄点药敷敷！”他赶忙开了口。“明儿早上要是还想干活，就再跟我一块儿来！”

多德走了，疑团没解开，也没得着安慰。一个钟头又一个钟头，搜寻的人叫疲乏和越来越拿得准的徒劳征服了，停下来喘气的次数越来越多。他们有的是工夫去采越来越多的黑莓——那些灌木丛分明藏着没有哪个垂死的人。他们越来越勤地跳进水里去凉快——那水里也分明没有哪个淹死的人浮着。当晚他们大多数人赶回家去做那些耽误下的杂活，一个个灰心，绝望。

===Original===

Isobel McLaughlin was still at the Keiths’, detained by Libby’s need of her. Libby, though she used men easily for her purpose, was not a woman to depend on them. Her mild old husband could give her no sufficient support in her affliction. He had never been a mother. He was just a man whom life and marriage had left blinking, swallowing as best he might his realization of his own unimportance in the universe. Libby would have Isobel with her. So Chirstie in her mother-in-law’s house put the younger McLaughlins and Bonnie Wee Johnnie to bed, and came out to sit on the doorstep with her weary and outraged husband. Presently she asked him wistfully;

===Chinese===

伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林还留在基思家，莉比离不得她，把她绊住了。莉比虽说使唤男人为她的事出力很是顺手，却不是个肯倚靠男人的女人。她那位温和的老丈夫，在她遭难的当口给不了她足够的撑持。他从来没当过母亲。他只是个男人，叫生活和婚姻撂在那里直眨巴眼，好歹咽下他对自己在天地间无足轻重的自知。莉比要伊莎贝尔陪着她。于是克丽丝蒂在婆母的家里，把麦克劳克林家的小的们和小宝贝约翰尼（Bonnie Wee Johnnie）一个个安顿上床，出来坐在门槛上，挨着她那又累又愤懑的丈夫。过了一会儿，她怅然问道；

===Original===

“Do you really think he’s dead, Wully?”

“It’s getting to look like it.”

She gave a great sigh. If only she could be *sure* he was dead!

“You don’t think he’s just gone away now?” she continued.

“Nobody thinks that now.”

===Chinese===

“你当真觉得他死了，伍利？”

“看着是越来越像了。”

她长长叹了口气。她要是能千真万确地知道他死了才好呢！

“你现在不觉得他只是走开了吧？”她接着问。

“如今没人这么想了。”

===Original===

“Why don’t they?”

“It don’t look reasonable to them.”

“It looks reasonable enough to me.”

He longed to reassure her.

“If he had gone back to town, he would have had to stop in some place to get something to eat. He didn’t stop anywhere.”

===Chinese===

“为什么不想了？”

“他们看着不合情理。”

“我看着够合情理的。”

他巴望能叫她安下心来。

“他要是回镇上去了，总得在什么地方停下来弄点吃的。他哪儿也没停。”

===Original===

She slapped away a mosquito.

“But if he didn’t stop as he came, why should he stop going back?”

“He may have stopped at a dozen places coming, and found no one at home. He may have gone to his mother’s when she was at the picnic. That’s what she keeps wailing about—because she wasn’t there when he came!”

In the silence of the starlight, she gave a great sigh.

===Chinese===

她拍开一只蚊子。

“可他来的时候既然没停过，回去为什么要停呢？”

“他来的时候兴许在十来个地方停过，都碰上家里没人。他兴许上他娘家去过，正赶上她去野餐了。她哭来哭去就是为这个——他来的时候她偏不在家！”

星光下的静默里，她长叹了一声。

===Original===

“It’s all my fault!” she declared.

He was too tired to listen to that.

“Our fault, indeed!” he answered sharply. “We never told him to come sneaking back and get lost, did we! We didn’t tell him never to write to his mother.”

“I didn’t say it was your fault. I said mine! Really, all auntie’s trouble seems to come from me. Sometimes I just seem to make everybody miserable.” She had been wondering what she was to do if Peter’s death made Wully’s lie permanent.

===Chinese===

“都怪我！”她说。

他累得听不进这个。

“倒成了咱们俩的错！”他尖利地答。“咱们可没叫他偷偷摸摸溜回来再走丢，是不是！也没叫他不给他娘写信。”

“我没说是你的错。我说的是我的！真的，姑姑的苦头好像桩桩件件都打我这里来。有时候我好像就是叫人人都受苦。”她一直在琢磨：彼得的死要是叫伍利那句谎成了永久，她可怎么办。

===Original===

“Havers, Chirstie!” he remonstrated, “her trouble comes through her own foolishness. She was never less than a fool about that—that—”

“She was always good to me, Wully, whatever you say. I mind how she stayed with me after mother’s death. If she’s been foolish about Peter, she’s paid well for it.”

“So’ve you!” said Wully. “He’s *dead*, I tell you!” And there was another thing to be said. Wully might be bewildered, uncomfortable, frustrated, cheated of any assurance of safety for Chirstie. But there was one triumph, and not a small one. “He’s dead. And we never speak ill of the dead, Chirstie!”

She understood his triumph. She would have been glad to have him dead, and not putting Wully into danger. She would be relieved, too, of that sense of terror, if she saw him dead. Then she thought of that great sinful lie, and of Isobel McLaughlin.

===Chinese===

“胡扯，克丽丝蒂！”他劝道，“她的苦头是她自己的糊涂招来的。在那桩——那桩——事上，她从来没有不糊涂的时候。”

“不管你怎么说，她待我一直是好的，伍利。我记得母亲去世后她怎么守着我。她要是在彼得的事上糊涂过，她也付足了代价。”

“你也付得不轻！”伍利说。“他真死了，我告诉你！”还有一层话可说。伍利也许惶惑，也许难受，也许处处碰壁，被夺去了克丽丝蒂平安无事的任何一点把握。可他有一桩胜利，还不是小小的胜利。“他死了。而且咱们从来不兴说死人的坏话，克丽丝蒂！”

她懂得他这份胜利。她原也巴望他死了，伍利就不用再往危险里去。若是亲眼看见他死了，她也能卸下那份恐惧。随后她想起那桩天大的、有罪的谎话，想起伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林。

===Original===

“I can’t tell what to wish!” she sighed miserably. “It can’t end well. I wish they’d find him dead. But if he’s dead, how can I ever. …” Her voice gave way to despair.

“Yes,” repeated Wully. “How can you ever. …” They sat silent.

“You never can!” he said securely, at length.

===Chinese===

“我都不知道该盼什么好了！”她凄然叹道。“这事不会有好收场。我盼他们找见他死了。可他要是死了，我往后怎么还能……”她的声音沉进了绝望里。

“是啊，”伍利重复道。“你怎么还能……”他们闷坐着。

“你永远也不能！”末了他笃定地说。

## XVIII / 第十八章

===Original===

The night after the second day’s search Libby Keith had gone to bed for a while, because she was unable longer to stand up. Again she had risen when the moon rose, and Isobel McLaughlin, hearing her in the kitchen, had risen to find her washing out a shallow tin milk pan. Libby had managed to make her purpose known. Her voice was altogether gone now, after so much calling to her Lammie, and she was starting out with the pan and the poker, so that when her Peter heard the noise she was making, he would know that help was near. With Isobel following her as best she might, she beat back and forth up and down the roads again till morning, when she fell exhausted near the McCreaths’ at dawn, so that they had to hitch up and take her home. And lying in the wagon, she muttered and moaned. Isobel understood that sometimes she was simply saying her son’s name. Sometimes she was trying to tell what a good lad he had always been. And sometimes she said, “Only forty yards from home”; sometimes, “A wee’an’s bones!” But some of the neighbors gathering had heard her pan’s din and praying, and the hunt was on again, before the sun was well up.

Later that morning Isobel McLaughlin sat telling Wully about that night, in the Keiths’ kitchen, whispering, looking carefully towards the door of the room where Libby was supposed to be resting. She was sitting by the breakfast table. On the red cloth three cold half-drunk cups of tea told how negligible a thing food was in that household. Suddenly she said passionately:

“Wully, you’ve got to bring him home alive today!” and with that, to her son’s consternation, she burst into great weeping.

===Chinese===

第二天搜寻过后的那个夜里，莉比·基思上床躺了一会儿，因为她实在站不住了。月亮升起来的时候，她又起来了；伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林听见厨房里有响动，也起身出来，见她正涮着一只浅浅的白铁奶锅。莉比总算把自己的打算说明白了。呼唤她的小羊儿唤了这许多，她的嗓音如今已经完全没有了；她要带上锅和拨火棍出门，这样，她的彼得听见她敲出的声响，就会知道搭救的人近了。伊莎贝尔竭力跟在她身后；她敲敲打打，沿着大路小路来回奔走，又一直敲到天亮；黎明时分，她精疲力竭，倒在麦克雷思家近旁，那家人只得套上车把她送回家去。躺在车斗里，她喃喃呓语，声声呻吟。伊莎贝尔听得出，有时她不过在一遍遍念着儿子的名字；有时她想诉说这孩子从来是个多么好的小伙子；有时她说：“离家只有四十码”；有时说：“小不点儿的一把骨头！”不过，聚拢来的一些邻居听见了她那只锅的敲打声和她做的祷告，日头还没升老高，搜寻就又开始了。

那天上午晚些时候，伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林坐在基思家的厨房里，跟伍利讲那个夜里的事，压低了嗓子，眼睛不时小心地瞟着那间屋子的门——莉比按说正在里面歇着。她坐在早饭桌旁。红桌布上摆着三只凉了的、喝剩一半的茶杯，说明吃食在这一家人眼里算不得什么要紧东西。她忽然满心激动地说：

“伍利，你今天非得把他活着带回家不可！”话一出口，她就放声大哭起来，把她儿子惊得手足无措。

===Original===

Wully, fearing the sight of his aunt’s grief, hadn’t wanted to come that morning to the accursed house. But his father had asked him to, looking at him, Wully thought, with an unusual sharpness, so that hurriedly, to avoid suspicion, he had said he would come. He had dreaded the errand. But he had never foreseen this. He never remembered seeing his mother cry before, not even at the time of his brother’s death, though she must have wept then. And now—well, it was no wonder she was undone, after forty-eight hours of such nightmare. But he was beside himself at the sight. He got up and strode around the room, at his wits’ end. Life was upside down. Chirstie at his mother’s broken and nervous from her shock; his aunt raving mad; his mother crying noisily. …

“You think he’s alive, don’t you, Wully?” she was asking him, between sobs and sniffles. “You don’t think he’s dead, do you?” He marveled to see how utterly she shared his aunt’s grief. She could scarcely have wanted more Peter’s return, if he had been her own son. He answered staunchly;

“No! Of course he’s not *dead*, mother! A man don’t die from sleeping outdoors a couple of nights in July!”

===Chinese===

伍利怕见姑姑伤心的样子，那天早上本不愿意到这遭了咒的屋里来。可他父亲要他来；看他时的那副眼神，伍利觉得，锐利得异乎寻常，他只好慌忙应承下来，免得惹人起疑。这趟差事他心里直发怵。可眼前这一幕，他万万没有料到。他记不得从前见过母亲哭，连他兄弟阵亡的时候也没有，虽说那时候她必定也哭过。而如今——唉，熬过了这样四十八小时的噩梦，她垮了下来，原也怪不得她。可这情景让他心神俱裂。他站起身，在屋里大步踱来踱去，一筹莫展。日子整个翻了个个儿。克丽丝蒂待在他母亲那里，惊魂未定，神经脆弱；他姑姑疯了似的说胡话；他母亲哭得呜呜咽咽。……

“你觉得他还活着，是不是，伍利？”她抽噎着、吸着鼻子问他。“你不觉得他死了，对吧？”他吃惊地看到，她把姑姑的伤心分受得这样彻底。哪怕彼得是她亲生的儿子，她盼他回来，也不会盼得更切了。他斩钉截铁地答道；

“没有！他当然没有死，妈妈！七月的天气，在外头睡上两夜，哪能睡死人！”

===Original===

“You don’t think—he’s fallen into some slough—and drowned, do you?”

“No, mother! Of course not! He’s around some place, drunk, likely! Don’t cry, mother!”

“How could he be alive—some place—and let us all go on hunting him?”

===Chinese===

“你不觉得——他掉进哪片沼泽里——淹死了，是吧？”

“没有，妈妈！当然没有！他在哪个地方待着呢，多半是喝醉了！别哭，妈妈！”

“他要是活着——在哪个地方——怎么能眼看咱们大家一直找他？”

===Original===

Suddenly she added, with a greater sob, lifting her head;

“Wully, if Peter’s alive, and just letting his mother think he’s lost, we ought to whip him when he’s found! Every man that’s spent a day hunting him ought to give him a—beating! Wully, he’d never do that! I think he’s—he’s dead!”

“Mother, mother! Don’t you cry so! It’ll be all right. They’ll find him soon!”

“If you don’t find him soon, Auntie will go mad!”

===Chinese===

忽然她抬起头来，抽噎得更加厉害，又添了一句；

“伍利，彼得要是活着，存心叫他母亲当他丢了，那找着他以后就该抽他！每一个为他搜寻过一天的男人，都该揍他一顿——狠狠地揍！伍利，他绝不会那样干！我想他是——他是死了！”

“妈妈，妈妈！别哭成这样！会好起来的。他们很快就找着他了！”

“你们要是不能很快找着他，姑姑会疯的！”

===Original===

Wully could have cried aloud the conviction that came flooding over him that minute: “If we do find him alive, and I get my hands on him, you will go mad!” He began, like a child begging;

“Mother, don’t you stay here! You come home with me! It’s enough to kill you, staying here with Auntie! Let someone else stay a while. Why can’t Aunt Flora stay with her today? You come on home with me!”

“I can stay. She wants me. I can stand anything, if only he’s found. Wully!” she cried, raising a face toward him distorted with tears, “don’t you know where he is?”

If Chirstie had been there to see that face, she would have thought that now, at last, Isobel McLaughlin was betraying her secret, so visibly did forbidden questions tremble on her tongue. Wully only said, soothingly, indulgently;

===Chinese===

那一刻涌上伍利心头的确信，他简直想喊出声来：“咱们要是真把他活着找着了，又叫我逮住了他，你才会疯呢！”他开了口，倒像小孩子央告似的；

“妈妈，你别待在这儿了！跟我回家去！跟姑姑在这儿守着，能把人熬死！叫别人来守一阵吧。今天怎么不能叫弗洛拉姑姑（Aunt Flora）来陪她？你跟我回家去吧！”

“我守得住。她要我在。只要能找着他，什么我都受得住。伍利！”她哭喊着，朝他仰起一张让泪水扭歪了的脸，“你不知道他在哪儿吗？”

克丽丝蒂要是当时在场，看见那张脸，准会以为伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林这一下终于泄露了自己的秘密——那几个不敢出口的问题在她舌尖上颤动，看得再清楚不过。伍利只是温言细语、百般包容地说；

===Original===

“If I knew where he was, don’t you think I would go there and find him? Mother, you need a rest. You haven’t had enough sleep!”

His mother sat bending towards him, beseeching him with all her soul to tell her the truth. But not one of her passionate unspoken entreaties reached him. It never occurred to him that she might know. He sat looking at her sympathetically, troubled that she spoke words of such unusual foolishness, being overwrought by all that had befallen her.

“Won’t you come home with me?” he said again.

“No, I won’t!” she said, with some asperity, and put her head down on her arms on the table, and went on crying.

===Chinese===

“我要是知道他在哪儿，你以为我不会上那儿把他找回来？妈妈，你缺个歇息。你没睡够！”

他母亲朝他俯过身来，用整个灵魂恳求他说出实话。可她那些火热的、无声的哀告，一句也没有传到他心里。他压根儿没想到她可能知道。他坐在那里，满怀体恤地望着她，只发愁她竟说出这样反常的傻话，准是这些天临头的祸事把她压得过了头。

“你跟我回家去，好不好？”他又说了一遍。

“不，我不回！”她说，话里带了几分生硬，随把头埋进桌上交叉的两臂里，接着哭。

===Original===

He rode away to his place in the hunt, and underneath all his greetings, his short and dry comments on the day’s possibilities, there stayed with him a troubled sense of pity for his mother. She was getting old. And he had treated her badly. Sometimes he even thought that he had treated her very badly in that affair, even though it was over now. All those hours, those murderous hours of the last days, he had never given her a thought. He hadn’t stopped in his hating long enough to imagine how deeply, how terribly, he was about to wound her. If he came upon Peter, and killed him—as he must—what would his mother do? How brokenly even now she grieved for Aunt Libby! What would her grief be like then? The thought sickened him. He said to himself bitterly that he was so tired, so confused, that if he came upon that damned snake alone, he’d likely shake hands with him and let him go! He scarcely knew what he was doing.

All the parties had changed places that day. It seemed impossible for men to hunt repeatedly through the same place with any heart. It was a fifteen-hour nightmare. Added to the growing sense of futility, of frustration, of physical exhaustion, and the burden of the heat, Wully had that uneasiness about his mother to harrow him. He had gone with the men who were searching through his own lands, that day, through the low land where he had so prayerfully hoped to bury his enemy. And he seldom was allowed even to hunt about alone. Someone or other was always near him, so that if he came upon that—that—he would have no chance to work his quick will upon him safely.

The fourth day they gathered again, going over routes that seemed hopeless. Peter, alive or dead, was simply in no place within miles. Not a little pebble, even, remained unturned now. The older men were sustaining themselves on strong drink more or less soberly, and the younger ones considerably less soberly. The first day of the alarm had been something of a picnic to thoughtless youngsters used to solitary hoeing, something of a diversion to men accustomed to plowing alone from dawn to darkness. But the excitement was dying away. Paths were beaten roads, and roads great wide highways. Miles of untrodden sloughs had become familiar ground, and acres of cryptic underbrush had become overworked monotony. What the slough had swallowed up, it would keep. If the tall grasses had treasures hidden, only the winter could bring low the tall grasses. The crowd dwindled.

First those from the farther and less concerned settlements went back to their work, protesting they would all be watching, that they would keep a wide and long lookout always, for any signs of news. They regretted that their harvests were urgent. They departed. Then day by day members of the clan returned to neglected fields. John McLaughlin kept his children hunting, and as for the Squire he vowed he would never stop. His sporting blood was up. For nine days more Wully and his father went again and again from impossible clue to foolish conjecture. Wully’s belief grew constantly stronger that Peter had simply gone back to wherever he had come from. But how he had done it on a road where one passerby made a day memorable, he couldn’t imagine. It suggested a devilish cunning, a subtleity not to be lightly reckoned with, a persistence that made an honest man’s blood boil. To his praying mother he affirmed that Peter was alive. To his dreading wife, he proclaimed that certainly he was dead. The whole desire of his life was to know which statement was true.

===Chinese===

他骑马追赶搜寻的队伍去了。可在他一声声寒暄底下，在他对当天搜寻前景那些短促干巴的议论底下，始终压着一种对母亲的烦乱的怜惜。她老了。而他待她很不好。有时他甚至觉得，在那桩事上他待她坏到了极点，尽管那桩事如今已经了结。那些时辰，前些天里那些杀气腾腾的时辰，他从来没有想起过她。他恨个不休，不曾停下一刻想一想，他即将给她的创伤有多么深、多么可怕。倘若他撞见彼得，杀了他——他非杀不可——他母亲会怎么样？瞧她如今为莉比姑姑伤心，都断断续续不成个样子了！到那时她的伤心又该是什么模样？这念头让他恶心。他苦涩地对自己说，他太累、太乱，若是独自撞上那条该死的蛇，八成会跟他握握手，放他走！他简直不知道自己在干什么。

那一天，各路人马全都换了地方。人要是一遍又一遍在同一片地方打起精神搜寻，似乎断不可能。那是一场十五个钟头的噩梦。除了越来越沉的徒劳感、挫败感、浑身的筋疲力尽和暑热的重压，伍利心里还压着对母亲的那份不安，来来回回地耙着他的心。那天，他跟着搜他自己地界的那一队人，走过那片低洼地——他曾多么虔诚地盼着把敌人埋在那里。而且他几乎从来不被许独自四下搜寻。总有什么人守在他近旁，这样，哪怕他真撞上了那个——那个——人，也断没有机会又快又稳地遂了自己的心愿。

第四天，他们重新聚拢，把几条眼看无望的路线又走了一遍。彼得，不论死活，方圆几英里之内压根不在任何地方。如今连一颗小石子都没有翻不过来的了。上了年纪的人多多少少还算清醒地靠烈酒撑着，年轻后生们可就醉得厉害多了。惊变头一天，对那些过惯了独自锄地的没心没肺的小伙子来说，多少还算一回野餐；对那些过惯了从天亮独自犁到天黑的人来说，多少还算一回调剂。可这股兴奋劲儿正在消散。小径踩成了路，路踩成了宽阔的大道。多少英里人迹未到的沼泽走熟了，多少亩幽秘的矮树丛也踏成了单调。沼泽吞下去的东西，它必扣住不放。高草里若真埋着宝贝，也只有冬天能把高草压倒。人越聚越少了。

先是那些住得远、利害也淡些的村子里的人回去干活了，一再说他们会人人守望，会一直放眼望到又远又长，不放过任何一点消息的影儿。他们抱歉说家里的收成逼得紧。他们走了。随后，族里的人也一天一天回到荒疏了的田里。约翰·麦克劳克林仍叫他的儿女们继续搜寻；至于乡绅，他发誓绝不停手。他那股狩猎的血性全上来了。此后又有九天，伍利和他父亲从一条条不可能的线索追到一个个愚蠢的猜想，来来回回，没个完。伍利越来越确信，彼得不过是回他来的那个地方去了。可是在那条一个过路人就能叫一天显得不寻常的路上，他是怎么走脱的，他想像不出来。这透着一种魔鬼般的狡猾，一种不容小觑的深沉心机，一种叫正派人血液发烫的死缠不放。对他那祷告着的母亲，他一口咬定彼得活着；对他那担惊受怕的妻子，他一口咬定彼得准死了。他这辈子的全部心愿，就是想知道这两句话哪一句是真的。

===Original===

Their wheat called them, at length. It was almost their year’s income, and to its whitening invitation they must listen. They took down their cradles, and fell upon it. Then they together went and harvested poor old Uncle Keith’s crop for him. He was no farmer at any time, and now too weakened by sorrow to save his wheat. Libby kept her bed for days together, and for many days Isobel McLaughlin hung over her, trying to save her sanity.

However much Chirstie shrank from it, she had to leave her mother-in-law’s well-filled house and go back to the loneliness of her own. Her harvesters must have food cooked and ready for them. Sometimes one of Wully’s little sisters stayed a few days with her, sometimes a little brother. Wully had told his mother simply that since the day Chirstie had fainted there alone on the Fourth of July, he wouldn’t have her left without company. His mother had listened simply, searchingly, wondering unhappily about many suggestive circumstances.

And all the time Chirstie kept insisting she wasn’t afraid. Not she! No indeed! But she never got Wully to believe her. He knew why she brought lunches so often to the field, and why she loitered about with him, forgetting her housework. He saw why she had suddenly become so keen about shooting, why day by day she potted away at worthless small birds, which formerly her pity would never have let her shoot. Let her say what she would, she was so much afraid that her very eyes had changed. Never before had they had that way of shifting instantly under her long lashes. Never before since she had been his wife had they had that haunted expression. She was bitterly afraid, and he was unable to reassure her. He could do nothing. It was as if some invisible unconquerable rattler crawled about in that little house where his wife and baby had been so happy. It seemed that all his safety lay in crushing down a great, uplifted club upon an intangible enemy.

The green months passed at length, and the golden ones were all but gone. John went back to Chicago, and the young children started back to school through goldenrod and wild sunflowers, down paths with fuchsia-colored wild asters, amethyst, blue, and pink. Chirstie was alone, perforce. Occasionally she had a visitor. Aunt Libby came oftener than anyone else. She was better again, able to spend day after day on horseback, going about from neighbor to neighbor, and calling, as she went, to ease her heart in the lonely places, “Lammie, Lammie!” She came often to Wully’s to see Bonnie Wee Johnnie. She had taken a notion that he was like her Peter. He ran about now, and it seemed not strange to his mother that a woman should ride miles for the pleasure of watching him. She taught him carefully to tolerate Aunt Libby’s extravagant caresses. Wully’s sisters were entirely indignant when they heard that Aunt Libby thought the baby looked like her son. But as they afterwards remarked, it was just like Aunt Libby to say that the prettiest child in the neighborhood resembled her blessed Peter.

===Chinese===

到头来，麦子把他们唤了回去。那几乎是他们一年的进项，麦色渐白发出的邀约，他们不能不听。他们取下摇架钐刀（cradle），扑向麦田。随后又一齐去替可怜的老基思叔叔（Uncle Keith）把麦子收了。他什么时候都算不得个庄稼人，如今又忧伤得没了气力，救不下他的麦子。莉比一连多少天卧床不起；一连多少天，伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林守在她床前，想保住她的神智。

克丽丝蒂再怎么不情愿，也得离开婆婆家那满满当当的屋子，回到自己家的冷清里去。给她家割麦子的人，总得有做好的饭菜等着。有时伍利的一个小妹妹陪她住上几天，有时是一个小弟弟。伍利跟他母亲说得简简单单：自从独立日那天克丽丝蒂独自一人在那里晕倒，他就不许她身边没有人做伴。他母亲听得简简单单，目光却探得深深，为许多耐人寻味的情形暗自难受。

这期间，克丽丝蒂一直咬定她不害怕。她才不怕呢！当然不怕！可她始终没能叫伍利信她。他明白她为什么三天两头往地里送午饭，为什么守在他跟前磨磨蹭蹭，把家务活儿都忘了。他看得出她为什么突然迷上了打枪，为什么一天又一天举枪轰打那些一钱不值的小鸟——从前她心慈，是断断不肯朝它们开枪的。任她怎么说，她怕得连眼睛都变了。从前，她那双眼睛从不曾在长睫毛底下一碰就那样飞快地躲闪开去。自打她做了他的妻子，那双眼睛从不曾有过这种惶惶无主的神情。她怕得苦，而他却宽解不了她。他什么也做不成。就仿佛有一条看不见的、降不住的响尾蛇，在那一小幢他妻子和娃娃曾经过得那样快活的小屋里游走。他仿佛觉得，自己全家的平安，就系在把一根高高抡起的大棒，朝一个无形无影的敌人狠狠砸下去的那一瞬。

青翠的月份终于过尽，金黄的月份也所剩无几。约翰回芝加哥去了，小孩子们也返校了，一路穿过一枝黄花和野向日葵，沿着小径走下去，路旁开着倒挂金钟色的野紫菀，还有紫晶色、蓝色和粉色的。克丽丝蒂无可如何，只得独自在家。偶尔也有客人来。莉比姑姑来得比谁都勤。她又好起来了，能一天接一天骑在马背上，东邻西家地走动，走到荒僻处，就放声呼唤，好让心里的憋闷松快些：“小羊儿，小羊儿！”她常到伍利家来看漂亮的小约翰尼。她认定这孩子长得像她的彼得。这孩子如今满地跑了；一个女人骑马跑上几英里，只为看着他取乐，在他母亲看来，也算不得什么怪事。她小心翼翼地教他受得住莉比姑姑那过分热络的爱抚。伍利的姐妹们听说莉比姑姑觉得这娃娃像她儿子，气得不行。不过，正像她们后来议论的那样，说这一带最标致的孩子像她那宝贝彼得——这话也只有莉比姑姑说得出口。

## XX / 第二十章

===Original===

The corn was husked. The year’s work in the fields was over. Wully had sold from sixty of the acres for which his father had paid two hundred and ten dollars in sixty-four, wheat worth three thousand and sixty dollars. He had his house all paid for now. He owned three hundred acres of land, some of it a bit farther west, where a bushel of wheat still bought an acre of the faithful soil. His little pines had grown steadily, and his orchard, now that the grasses and weeds were frosted, was visible to the naked eye from the house, a lot of little switches ready to stand bravely against the gales. Everything prospered with him. Everything, except for that shadow of evil that clouded their lives hatefully. Every day Wully’s mind dwelt futilely upon the problem of Peter Keith’s fate. And Chirstie’s eyes, he observed, still shifted apprehensively under their tender lids.

And what was he to do now, when he must go to the timber for his winter’s supply of wood? When he must leave early in the morning, and return at nightfall? He couldn’t leave her alone. He had remarked to one neighbor and another that he wanted some man to bring his wood home for hire. But he found no man willing to do his work. Chirstie would have to take the baby and go to her father’s or his mother’s. She didn’t want to do that. Either Wully would have to take her back and forth daily﻿—and that was a difficult thing under the circumstances﻿—or else she would have to stay away for days together, and then Wully would come home to a cold house and no food ready. They dreaded those days.

He finished the corn on a Wednesday, and on Thursday they were to have a great lark. They were to go to town together for the first time. He had a wagonload of prairie chickens to sell, which ought to bring at least ten dollars﻿—silly birds he had caught almost without effort as he husked his corn. Everything was ready. For one day they would put aside all their misgivings, and be happy together. They were enjoying what seemed to be a second Indian summer, bland days for riding across the country. And there was that spring-seat ready for Chirstie’s comfort. Moreover, she was to have a new coat. Wully had wanted to get her one the fall before, but she had said that there were so many things that they had to buy for their house that they really couldn’t afford the coat. She still protested that she really didn’t need it. But Wully was the more determined because he suspected she wore her mother’s old wrap for the principle of the thing. As if she needed to act humble! He wouldn’t have it!

===Chinese===

玉米掰完了。地里一年的活计到头了。伍利已经从他父亲一八六四年花二百一十美元买下的那些地里，拿出六十英亩，卖出了价值三千零六十美元的麦子。他的房子如今也全付清了。他名下有了三百英亩地，其中有些在再往西一点的地方，在那里，一蒲式耳小麦还买得来一英亩那忠厚的土地。他那些小松树一直见长；他的果园呢，如今草和杂草都经了霜，从房子跟前肉眼已经望得见了，满满一片细枝条，正预备挺直了身子，勇敢地去顶那漫天的大风。他事事顺遂。事事都顺遂，只除那一团凶影，可恨地笼罩着他们的生活。彼得·基思的下落究竟如何，伍利天天在心里空自琢磨。他还看出，克丽丝蒂那双眼睛，在温柔的眼睑底下，依然惴惴地闪躲着。

可如今他得去林子里砍过冬的柴火，这可怎么办？他得一大清早出门，天擦黑才回来。他不能撇下她一个人。他跟这个邻居提过、又跟那个邻居提过，想雇个人替他把柴火拉回家来。可他找不出一个肯干这活的男人。克丽丝蒂只好抱着娃娃，不是回她父亲家，就是去他母亲家。她又不愿意那么办。要么伍利得天天接送她来回——在眼下这种光景里，这可是件难事——要么她就得一连住上好几天，那样伍利回到家，就是一座冷屋子，饭也没一口现成的。那些日子他们一想起来就发怵。

他星期三掰完了玉米，星期四他们要痛痛快快地乐上一场。他们要头一回一起进城。他有一车草原鸡要卖，少说也该卖上十块钱——都是些傻鸟，他掰玉米的时候几乎没费什么劲就逮着了。一切都齐备了。就这一天，他们要把满心的忐忑统统撂在一边，快快活活地厮守一天。他们正享受着一段仿佛第二回小阳春的天时，温润的日子，正宜坐车在乡野间穿行。再说，还有那个弹簧车座，早已安好，专等着克丽丝蒂坐得舒舒服服。况且，她还要添一件新大衣。头年秋天伍利就想给她买一件，可她说，家里要置办的东西太多，实在买不起大衣。她到现在还一口咬定，实在用不着。可伍利愈发铁了心，因为他疑心她穿她母亲那件旧外套，为的是守那份节俭的规矩。好像她还用得着装穷似的！他可不依！

===Original===

The store in which they found the right coat finally was narrow and dark and full of dull necessities, mittens and milk-crocks, grim boots, and grimmer tobacco. Wully hated the clerk the moment he saw him fix upon Chirstie eyes that narrowed expressively. Nevertheless, the odious man brought out from some dark recess behind the main room the very garment they were searching for.

“Put this on,” he urged familiarly. She put it on. It was a green thing, so dark a green it was almost black, and rich-looking, short in front, and falling, mantle-wise, well down over her skirts behind. It had rich fringe on it, and intricate frogs for fastenings. Wully would have forestalled the clerk, and buttoned it for her, but his fingers were awkward and helpless in such a task. So the man did it, standing as near her as he dared. But when she stood forth arrayed, Wully’s annoyance was forgotten. He heaved a sigh of satisfaction.

He saw again with surprise how garments change women. She was scarcely the same being who had walked in, in that faded old dingy wrap. This coat was made for her, beyond a doubt She asked the price.

===Chinese===

他们到底买到那件合意大衣的那家铺子，又窄又暗，堆满了乏味的日用杂货：连指手套、奶罐，硬邦邦的靴子，还有更硬邦邦的烟草。伍利一眼就恨上了那个伙计——他瞧见那家伙眯缝起眼睛盯住克丽丝蒂，那神情再明白不过。可尽管如此，那可恶的家伙还是从店堂后头哪个黑黢黢的角落里，把他们要找的那件衣裳取了出来。

“把这个穿上。”他熟不拘礼地催促道。她穿上了。那是一件绿的衣裳，绿得那么深，几乎成了黑的，看着十分华贵，前襟短短的，往后却像斗篷似的直垂下去，把裙子罩住好大一片。衣上镶着讲究的流苏，缀着繁复的盘花扣。伍利本想抢在伙计头里，亲手替她扣上，无奈这种活计他的手指笨得帮不上忙。只好由那人代劳，他挨着她站，胆子敢挨多近就挨多近。可等她穿戴停当，站在那儿一亮相，伍利心里的恼火早忘了个干净。他心满意足地舒了一口长气。

他又一次吃惊地看到，衣裳是怎样改变女人的。她几乎不再是方才走进来的那个人了——穿着那件褪色的旧外套，灰扑扑的。这大衣简直是照着她裁的，毫无疑问。她问了价钱。

===Original===

“Sixteen dollars.”

She sighed and began undoing it. She would look at some others, she said. The man left them.

“Don’t you like it?” demanded Wully.

“It’s too fine for me. Sixteen dollars!” she commented.

“It’s *not* too fine. It’s becoming, Chirstie!”

“But *sixteen dollars*!” she exclaimed, as if that settled the matter.

===Chinese===

“十六块钱。”

她叹了口气，动手解扣子。她说她想再看看别的。那人走开了。

“你不喜欢吗？”伍利追问。

“太讲究了，我穿不配。十六块钱！”她说。

“它*不是*太讲究。它衬你，克丽丝蒂！”

“可是*十六块钱*呀！”她嚷道，仿佛这一句就把事情敲定了。

===Original===

“Ah, sixteen dollars isn’t going to break us up!” Wully urged, determinedly. “It’s a grand coat. It’s nobby.” He was at a loss to express his admiration for the garment. He only felt vaguely that it looked like Glasgow.

“But sixteen dollars, Wully! The *idea*!”

“You’ll have it, anyway.”

“I will *not*!” She was indignant “Why, Wully, your coat, your overcoat was only ten last winter!”

“But I hadn’t any red dress to match. Nor any feather!”

===Chinese===

“嗐，十六块钱又不会把咱们吃穷！”伍利执意相劝。“多体面的一件大衣。真神气。”他找不到话来表达自己对这件衣裳的倾心。他只是模模糊糊地觉得，它看上去就像格拉斯哥。

“可是十六块钱，伍利！*亏你想得出*！”

“反正这大衣你买定了。”

“我*才不*！”她气恼了。“真是的，伍利，你的大衣，你的外套，去年冬天才花了十块！”

“可我没有红裙子来配它。也没有羽毛！”

===Original===

The man had come back.

“If you want something cheap now, for your wife﻿—”

“I don’t want anything cheap!” said Wully, “We’ll take this.”

Chirstie stood examining it inside and out. She was wondering what her father would say to such a coat.

She wore the nobby coat away. Wully carried the old garment. He had been gay, almost hilarious all the morning, ever since selling the prairie chickens so well. And now as he looked at his stunning wife, walking demurely along in such grandeur, his spirits rose higher. He watched people look at her. He chuckled to see them.

===Chinese===

那人回来了。

“要是你想挑件便宜的嘛，给你太太——”

“便宜的我不要！”伍利说，“就要这件。”

克丽丝蒂站在那里，把那大衣里里外外看了又看。她心里正琢磨，她父亲见了这样一件大衣会说什么。

她穿着那件神气的大衣走了。旧衣裳由伍利拎着。打从草原鸡卖了个好价钱起，他一上午都喜洋洋的，简直有点忘乎所以。这会儿，眼看着自己光彩照人的妻子，端庄娴静、一身阔气地走在街上，他的兴头更高了。他看着人们回头望她。瞧着他们，他暗自直乐。

===Original===

They walked down the busy little street. He left the old coat at the hotel. She saw a shawl she admired, and he wanted to buy it for her. But she was thinking how nice it would be for his mother, a little soft fine shawl like that. He wondered that he hadn’t thought of that himself. They bought the shawl, and went on down the street. They came to a place where tintypes were taken. It came over him like a flash.

“We’ll go in and have our pictures taken!” he exclaimed.

“Oh,” she said hesitating. “How much will it cost?”

“Oh, nothing much!” he exclaimed. He made her go in with him. There was a picture, was there, he was thinking, that made Wee Johnnie look like the son of that snake? Well, there should soon be another that made him look like another man’s son. Chirstie had never had her likeness taken. But Wully had had his made in St. Louis, to be sent to his mother. He knew how to walk in and have the thing done grandly.

He sat down in a chair, and put the baby on one knee, paternally. On the other knee he spread out a great hand. Chirstie took her place behind him, her hand on his shoulder, her feather curling down over her hat, her new sixteen-dollar coat, her wine-colored skirts showing bravely. And when that was done, he made her sit down with the baby on her knee, for a picture of just the mother and son. And then a further happy thought came to him. He sat down and took the baby, and cuddled his face right up against his own, and demanded a picture.

===Chinese===

他们顺着热闹的小街往下走。旧大衣他寄存在了旅店里。她看见一条披肩，很是喜欢，他就要给她买下。可她心里想的是，这样一条又软又细气的小披肩，给他母亲该多好。他奇怪自己怎么就没想起来。他们买下披肩，又顺着街往前走。走到一处拍锡版照相（tintype）的铺子门前。一个念头闪电似的掠过他心头。

“咱们进去照张相去！”他喊道。

“哦，”她迟疑着说。“那要花多少钱？”

“嗐，花不了几个钱！”他喊道。他拉她跟他一块儿进去了。他心里正盘算：不是现成有一张相片，照得小约翰尼活像那条毒蛇的儿子吗？哼，很快就会另有一张，叫人看了只当他是别人家的儿子。克丽丝蒂从来没有照过相。伍利却在圣路易斯照过一张，寄给他母亲。他知道怎样大模大样地走进去，气气派派地把这事办成。

他在一把椅子上坐下来，把娃娃搁在一边膝盖上，一副为父的架势。另一边膝盖上，他摊开一只大手。克丽丝蒂在他身后站定，一只手搭在他肩头，帽上的羽毛打着卷儿垂下来，那件十六块钱的新大衣、她酒红色的裙子，都鲜亮亮地展现着。照完这一张，他又叫她抱了娃娃坐在膝上，单照一张娘儿俩的。跟着又来了个锦上添花的念头。他坐下来，接过娃娃，把他那张小脸紧紧贴着自己的脸，非要照一张这样的。

===Original===

“It ain’t usual,” the photographer protested. “I can’t take a picture like that! It ain’t usual!”

“This ain’t no usual baby!” Wully replied chuckling. Who could have made a statement more paternal than that? “I want his face against mine!” And he got the picture taken that way, in the end.

They sought the street again. Chirstie was rather overcome by her husband’s grandness. He had such a worldly air﻿—commanding people about. He kept getting more imperious, more happy all the time though he was entirely sober. After a while, when it was growing dusk, he spied a friend on the street, just going into his office.

“That’s Mr. Knight, Chirstie! You remember! The man that drove me home that time! I’ll take you to see him!” He wanted to show her to everybody.

===Chinese===

“这不合常规，”照相师傅直抗议。“这样的相我可没法照！这不合常规！”

“这可不是个寻常娃娃！”伍利咯咯笑着答道。还有谁能说出比这更有父亲味儿的话来？“我要他的脸贴着我的脸！”到头来，相到底照成了，就照他要的那样。

他们又回到了街上。丈夫这通气派，克丽丝蒂简直有些慑服。他一副闯过世界的老练气派——把人支使得团团转。他人自始至终滴酒未沾，威风和欢喜却一刻涨似一刻。过了一会儿，天色渐暗，他瞥见街上一个朋友，正要进自己的事务所去。

“那是奈特先生，克丽丝蒂！你记得的！就是那回送我回家的那位！我带你见他去！”他恨不得把她引给每一个人看看。

===Original===

They went into an office having not only a kerosene lamp, but a lamp with a rich green shade, most luxurious, most metropolitan-looking. Chirstie was shy, and Mr. Knight puzzled for a moment.

“I’m McLaughlin,” Wully explained. “The soldier you drove out to Harmony, two years ago. I was sick, you remember!”

Mr. Knight’s face lighted up with recognition.

“Come in, McLaughlin!” he said heartily. “I didn’t recognize you! Sit down!” Around a table at one end of the room, men were playing cards, well-dressed men, who paused and looked up, and continued looking at the newcomers. A tall wide bookcase screened off one corner into something like a private office and to this Mr. Knight led them.

“My wife!” Wully said proudly, as he seated them.

===Chinese===

他们走进一间事务所，屋里不光点着一盏煤油灯，还有一盏罩着深绿灯罩的灯，奢华极了，一副大都会的派头。克丽丝蒂腼腆，奈特先生一时摸不着头脑。

“我是麦克劳克林，”伍利解释道。“两年前你送到哈莫尼（Harmony）去的那个兵。我病着，你记得的！”

奈特先生脸上豁然一亮，认出他了。

“进来，麦克劳克林！”他热诚地说。“我没认出你来！坐！”屋子一头有张桌子，几个人围着打牌，都是些衣冠楚楚的人物，停下牌抬起头来，目光一直跟着新来的客人。一座又高又宽的书架把一角隔出来，倒像个单间办公室，奈特先生就把他们往那里让。

“我媳妇！”伍利得意地说，一边安置他们坐下。

===Original===

“Your wife? Your baby? Why, it doesn’t seem possible! How the time gets away! And where did you find her?” he asked, so frankly pleased with her appearance that she blushed more deeply than she had at his first remark.

“She’s from out there! From Harmony.”

“She *is*,” he exclaimed. He continued looking at her. “Well, I always said that that was a remarkable country. A remarkable country,” he drawled.

Wully was delighted. Knight was a man whose opinion was valuable, a prosperous man, a man dressed as men dress in cities, whose interest he felt was not merely assumed for political ends. “How’s your mother?” he went on. He asked about the children, and the crops, and the new town which was to be near them. Finally he said:

===Chinese===

“你媳妇？你的娃娃？哎呀，真叫人不敢信！日子跑得可真快呀！你是在哪儿把她找着的？”他问道，见她的模样，他欢喜得那样坦率，倒叫她的脸比听他头一句话时红得更透了。

“她就是那一带的人！哈莫尼的。”

“*正是*，”他感叹道。他还在端详她。“嘿，我一向说，那可是个了不起的地方。了不起的地方，”他拖长调子说。

伍利高兴极了。奈特是个说话有分量的人，是个殷实人，一身城市人的装束，伍利觉出，他这份关切也不单是为着政治目的装的。“你母亲好吗？”他接着问。又问起孩子们，问起收成，问起将要兴在他们近旁的那座新镇。末了他说：

===Original===

“Well you certainly don’t look much like you did that morning. You were sick. Skin and bones. Do you remember?”

“Do I remember!” exclaimed Wully. “Will I ever forget!” He turned to his wife. “Chirstie, I was sitting right down there by the elevator, where the sidewalk is built up high, you know. I wasn’t sitting, either, I was lying stretched out, to try to keep from throwing up! I thought I’d seen Jimmy Sproul out there, and I’d ride home with him, and when I hurried up to him, it wasn’t Jimmy at all! It just made me sick! And I was lying there when Mr. Knight came along, and began asking me what was the matter of me. He said he would take me home. ‘How far is it?’ you asked, and when I said twenty-six miles, you said, ‘Oh! Twenty-six miles!’ Naturally. That made some difference. My heart sank, as they say. Or maybe it was my breakfast trying to get out. Anyway, I had a pang of some kind. And you said, ‘You wait here!’ And pretty soon along you came with those grays! I tell you I felt better even then. I got better all the way home. Every step. It seemed that morning as if I couldn’t wait another minute to start home!”

“Naturally!” remarked Mr. Knight, looking again with a smile, at Chirstie.

“Oh, I didn’t know her then! If I had known her I’d have started home crawling! Have you got those grays yet?” asked Wully, suddenly curious.

===Chinese===

“嗯，你如今的模样，跟那天早上可大不一样了。你那时病着。瘦得皮包骨。还记得吗？”

“怎么不记得！”伍利嚷道。“叫我怎么能忘！”他转向妻子。“克丽丝蒂，我那时就躺在下面那儿，粮栈升运机房旁边，就是人行道垫得老高的地方，你知道吧。我哪里是坐着，我是直挺挺躺着，好强忍着不吐出来！我当看见吉米·斯普劳尔（Jimmy Sproul）在那边呢，想着可以搭他的车回家，等我急急赶到他跟前，压根儿不是吉米！这一下更叫我恶心！我正躺在那儿，奈特先生走了过来，开口问我到底是怎么了。他说他要送我回家。‘有多远？’你问；我说二十六英里，你就说：‘哦！二十六英里！’自然喽。这一听，情形可就不大一样了。我的心往下一沉，人家常这么说。要不，就是我那份早饭在往外闯。不管怎么说，我那么一阵子说不出的难受。跟着你说：‘你在这儿等着！’不一会儿，你就赶着那对灰马来了！跟你说，那时我就觉出好些了。回家一路，我一路见好。每走一步，好一分。那天早上，我简直觉得一分钟也再等不得，恨不得马上就动身回家！”

“自然喽！”奈特先生说，又含笑望了望克丽丝蒂。

“哦，那时候我还不认得她呀！要早就认得她，我就是爬也得爬回家去！那对灰马你如今还有吗？”伍利问，忽然起了好奇心。

===Original===

“No, I haven’t.” The man smiled reminiscently. “I wish I had, sometimes. A Chicago man came along and wanted them. He was determined to have them. I let them go for a half section of land in Lyons County. I wouldn’t have done it,” he added confidently, “only my son had a baby born a day or two before that. I thought the land would be a good thing to keep for the child. How old is this little fellow?” He snapped his fingers invitingly towards the child.

“Oh, he’s﻿—a year or two. Something like that, isn’t he?” he asked his wife.

“Tut, tut, McLaughlin! You need experience! When they’re young like that the women count them in months. Don’t they, Mrs. McLaughlin?” he appealed.

“How old is your grandchild?” Wully parried boldly.

===Chinese===

“没有了。”那人追忆似的笑了。“有时候还真想它们哪。来了个芝加哥人，看上了那对马，非买不可，铁了心要。我拿它们换了莱昂县（Lyons County）半区土地（half section）。要不是我儿子在那前一两天添了个娃娃，我才不干呢，”他满有把握地补上一句。“我想着那块地留给那孩子是桩好东西。这小家伙多大了？”他朝娃娃捻着响指，逗他过来。

“哦，他嘛——一两岁吧。差不多那样，是不是？”他问他妻子。

“啧，啧，麦克劳克林！你还欠历练呢！娃娃那么点大的时候，女人家是按月数他们的。是不是呀，麦克劳克林太太？”他向她讨证。

“你的孙子多大了？”伍利大胆地回敬。

===Original===

“Oh, mine’s several months. Mine’s﻿—well, he’s got two teeth already!” And they laughed. Wully hastened to safer ground. If he wasn’t careful, someone might ask him when he was married.

“I’ll tell you another thing I remember!” he began. “I got in on that night train, that time, you know, and I went to the hotel where we had always stayed. Sick, I was, you know! I told the man﻿—he’d seen me a dozen times before﻿—that I hadn’t the price of a room. He’d had too much. He never even looked to see who I was. Just saw my uniform and began swearing! Wasn’t going to be eaten out of house and home by a lot of begging soldiers, he said. It nearly knocked me over. I went out to the street. And I couldn’t get up face enough to go some place else and ask for a bed, at first. I just sat around. Then finally I went into the Great West﻿—that’s where we all stay now when we come in. And Pierson there almost began swearing at me because I said I’d pay him later. He didn’t take soldiers’ last cents away from them, he said. He saw how I felt, and he went and got some milk toast made for me. And soft boiled eggs. And then, do you know what he did? He went to a room with me, and when he saw the pillows on the bed, he went and got me a pair of good pillows from some place. I hadn’t slept on a pillow for I don’t know how long! A man notices those things when he’s most dead, I tell you! Milk toast, and pillows, by *Jiminy*! And in the morning he sat and fed me such a lot of breakfast﻿—no wonder I had trouble! I felt as if I’d never get enough to eat.”

Mr. Knight made him go on talking. They sat there till the street was dark. And then Wully led his wife away, right up to the hotel. And then into the dining room. It seemed lordly to her that dining room﻿—an amazing day﻿—and Wully most lordly and amazing of all. It was like a fine wedding trip, almost, that day.

===Chinese===

“哦，我的嘛，有好几个月了。我的嘛——喏，他都出两颗牙了！”两个人都笑了。伍利赶紧撤到安全地带。他一不留神，就会有人问他是什么时候结的婚。

“再跟你说一件我记得的事！”他开了头。“那回我坐夜车到的，你知道吧，就去了我们向来落脚的那家旅店。我病着呢，你知道！我跟那掌柜说——他先前见过我十几回了——说我出不起一间房的房钱。他喝多了。他连看都不看我是谁。只看见我一身军装，就骂开了！他说，可不能让一帮伸手讨饭的大兵把他家吃穷吃垮。这一下几乎把我打懵了。我走到街上。起先，我实在提不起脸面再上别处去讨个床位。我就那么干坐着。后来末了，我进了大西部（Great West）旅店——如今我们进城，都住在那儿。那儿的皮尔逊（Pierson）听我说想往后再给钱，也差点骂起来。他说，他决不掏大兵兜里最后那几个子儿。他看出了我的光景，就去给我弄了些牛奶泡吐司来。还有半熟的煮鸡蛋。跟着，你知道他干了什么？他陪我一道上楼进了房，一眼看见床上的枕头，就不知从哪儿给我取来一对好枕头。我不知有多久没在枕头上睡过觉了！跟你说，人半死不活的时候，才留意这些事！牛奶泡吐司，还有枕头，*老天爷在上*！第二天早上，他坐在那儿，摆给我好大一桌子早饭——难怪我闹出了毛病！我只觉得好像一辈子也吃不饱似的。”

奈特先生还叫他说下去。他们在那儿一直坐到街上暗了下来。随后伍利携着妻子走开，一直走到旅店。又进了餐室。那间餐室在她看来好不气派——这一天处处叫人惊叹——而伍利是里头最气派、最叫人惊叹的一个。这一天，差不多就像一场体面的蜜月旅行了。

## XXI / 第二十一章

===Original===

They had breakfasted together before daylight, and he had gone to load the lumber he was taking home for his father, so that they might have a very early start. In the noisy, untidy hotel office she sat watching in surprise the confusion and the stir. There were crowds of women waiting near her, women like herself waiting for wagons to take them on towards the west, women with bundles and babies, and quarreling, crying young children. Chirstie’s face showed how exciting the scene was to her. She looked from group to group. She considered a foreign woman with a handkerchief tied on her head, whose tiny baby coughed and wheezed distressingly. She longed to say something sympathetic to the stolid mother. But she was too shy. Between caring for her own vigorous son, and watching other women’s children, the hour hurried by. Presently she saw her husband drive up, and get out to tie his horses. But before he had started for the hotel door, a stranger accosted him, and with the stranger Wully turned and went down the street. So she waited on. Two sets of youngsters quarreling drew their mothers into the fray, and Chirstie shrank away from their roughness, thoroughly shocked.

Then, before she had expected him, Wully was standing over her, reaching down for the baby. She scarcely knew him. His face was white. His eyes were shining strangely.

===Chinese===

天亮以前他们一块儿吃过早饭，他就去装木料了——那是他替父亲运回家的木料——好让他们一大早就能动身。在那间又吵又乱的旅店堂屋里，她坐着，惊奇地望着那一团忙乱与喧腾。她近旁等着一大堆女人，都是像她一样等着大车把她们往西送的女人，有抱着包袱和娃娃的，还有又吵又哭的小孩子。这光景叫克丽丝蒂多么新奇，全摆在她脸上。她一组一组地看过去。她端详一个头上包着手帕的外国女人，那女人怀里的小奶娃咳得直喘，听着叫人难受。她很想对那位木然不动的母亲说几句体恤的话。可是她太腼腆。一边照料自己那个壮实的儿子，一边看别人家的娃娃，一个钟头飞快地过去了。不一会儿，她望见丈夫赶车过来，下了车去拴马。可他还没朝旅店门口走，一个陌生人叫住了他，伍利便随着那人转身顺街去了。她只好接着等。两拨小孩子吵架，把做母亲的也拽进了战团，克丽丝蒂嫌他们撒野，直往后躲，心里着实骇然。

后来，还没到她料想的时辰，伍利已经站在她跟前，弯下腰来抱娃娃了。她简直认不得他了。他脸色煞白。眼睛里闪着一种怪异的光。

===Original===

“What ails you?” she cried. “You’re sick, Wully! What’s the matter?”

“I’m all right!” he said sharply. His voice quivered with feeling. He couldn’t trust himself to speak. His mouth was set in a hard line.

She rose and followed him, frightened. She got into the wagon, and he handed her the baby. He climbed up beside her, and they were off. She saw he couldn’t tell her what had happened just there. She could wait—a little.

They were almost out of town now.

“Wully, what’s the matter? Are you sick?”

“I’m all right!”

===Chinese===

“你这是怎么了？”她喊道。“你病了，伍利！出了什么事？”

“我好着呢！”他生硬地说。他的嗓音激动得发颤。他不敢开口，怕自己把持不住。他的嘴抿成一条硬线。

她吓着了，起身跟着他。她上了车，他把娃娃递到她怀里。他爬上车，在她身旁坐下，车就动了。她看得出，出了什么事，他不能就在这儿讲。她可以等——等一小会儿。

这会儿他们眼看就出镇子了。

“伍利，到底出了什么事？你病了吗？”

“我好着呢！”

===Original===

She was more anxious than ever. She waited till the baby was asleep in her arms, and then she laid him carefully down in the little box in which Isobel McLaughlin had taken her babies back and forth to town. Then she turned towards her husband with determination. And hesitated. He looked too stern—too fierce. She sat undecided, wretched, glancing quickly at him and then away. After a few perplexed moments, her face darkened with terror.

“Oh, I know! You’re—you’ve seen *him*! You were like that on the Fourth!”

He turned toward her, trying to speak.

“Yes!” he broke forth. “I saw him *dying*.”

“Oh, dying!” She tried to realize it. “Oh, if he’s *dying*, then we’ll be happy again!”

===Chinese===

她比先前更焦急了。她一直等到娃娃在她怀里睡熟，才小心地把他放平，搁进那只小木匣——当年伊莎贝尔·麦克劳克林就是用它，把自己的娃娃们一趟趟抱进抱出城的。随后她拿定了主意，转向丈夫。却又迟疑了。他的样子太阴沉——太凶。她坐在那儿拿不定主意，难受极了，急急地瞟他一眼，又慌忙把眼睛挪开。心里乱了好一阵，她的脸上罩满了惊怖。

“哦，我知道了！你——你见着*他*了！独立日那天，你就是这副神气！”

他转向她，想开口。

“不错！”他迸出一声。“我看见他*快死了*。”

“哦，快死了！”她竭力领会这件事。“哦，他要是*快死了*，咱们就又能快活了！”

===Original===

He said nothing. His lips worked.

“I won’t have to be afraid now!” She spoke like one overcome by a great fortune. He had never imagined she had been as unhappy as that cry of hers indicated by its relief.

“Dying!” she repeated, tasting the sweetness of the word. Then, suddenly:

===Chinese===

他没作声。他的嘴唇翕动着。

“往后我再也不用怕了！”她说话的口气，像交了泼天大运的人。她这一声里透出的如释重负，叫他头一回想到，她先前竟苦到了这般地步。

“*快死了*！”她又念了一遍，咂摸着这个字的甜味。跟着，突然：

===Original===

“How do you know? Where did you see him?”

She saw his face harden with hatred.

“Wully, are you sure he’s dying? He isn’t dead yet?”

“He’s dying all right!”

After a moment she exclaimed:

“But how did you find him?”

===Chinese===

“你怎么知道的？你在哪儿见着他的？”

她看见他的脸因仇恨绷紧了。

“伍利，你拿得准他快死了？他还没死，是不是？”

“他是要死的，错不了！”

过了一阵，她惊呼：

“可你是怎么找着他的？”

===Original===

“Somebody told me just as I was ready to start home.”

“Oh, that man! I saw that man speaking to you. How did he know to tell you?”

“They were looking for someone to take him out home.”

“Oh, they *were*!” That seemed to have changed the situation for her.

“You mean they asked you to bring him out?”

He didn’t relish her questions.

===Chinese===

“我刚要动身回家，有人告诉了我。”

“哦，那个人！我看见他跟你说话来着。他怎么会想起来告诉你？”

“他们正要找个人，捎他回乡下去。”

“哦，他们*是在*找人呀！”在她看来，这一来情势像是变了样。

“你是说，他们求你把他捎出去？”

他可不爱她这样盘根问底。

===Original===

“Yes.”

“And you wouldn’t do it, would you!” She approved. She clasped his arm with both hands. She rejoiced in her assurance.

His anger flamed again.

“Likely I’d bring him out with you!”

“Oh, we’ll be *happy* now, Wully!”

But after a minute she stirred uncomfortably. He felt her face grow grave.

===Chinese===

“不错。”

“你不肯去，是不是！”她赞许他。她双手搂住他的胳膊。她对自己这份把握满心欢喜。

他的怒气又蹿起来。

“亏你想得出！我还会捎上他，跟你一辆车！”

“哦，这会儿咱们能*快活*了，伍利！”

可过了一分钟，她不自在地挪动了一下。他觉出她的脸沉了下来。

===Original===

“Where was it you saw him, Wully?”

“In a livery stable.”

“In a livery stable!” she repeated. “Dying in such a place!” Dying seemed not so sweet a word now.

“But why didn’t he send word home before? Think of Aunt Libby, Wully!”

“He came in on the train last night.”

“Oh!” she exclaimed, enlightened. “He wanted to get home alive!”

===Chinese===

“你在哪儿见着他的，伍利？”

“在一家车马行。”

“在车马行！”她跟着念了一遍。“在那么个地方死！”“快死了”这几个字，这会儿可不像方才那么甜了。

“可他先前怎么不捎信儿回家？想想莉比姑姑吧，伍利！”

“他是昨儿夜里坐火车到的。”

“哦！”她恍然大悟地叫起来。“他想活着到家呀！”

===Original===

“What’s the matter of him?” she asked again.

“Hemorrhage,” said Wully, as shortly as it was possible to speak. He wouldn’t tell her how he had seen that snake lying bloody, dirty, sunken helpless on a bed of straw. He urged his horses on.

She looked at him. He turned away from her troubled eyes.

After a while;

“Look here, Wully!” she faltered.

He gave her no encouragement.

===Chinese===

“他到底什么毛病？”她又问。

“出血。”伍利说，短得不能再短。他没有告诉她，他怎样看见那条毒蛇躺在草铺上，血污，肮脏，瘫陷着，动弹不得。他把马催得更急了。

她看着他。他避开她那双惶乱的眼睛。

过了一阵，

“你听我说呀，伍利！”她讷讷地说。

他不给她一点回应。

===Original===

“After all, he was Aunt Libby’s baby!” she sighed.

“After *all*!” he sneered. He meant to silence her. She spoke again.

“Aunt Libby was always kind to me, Wully!”

He wouldn’t answer her. He knew what was coming.

She said timidly;

“I doubt we ought to go back and get him. If he’s dying, Wully! And Auntie waiting there for him!”

===Chinese===

“说到底，他也是莉比姑姑的儿子呀！”她叹道。

“*说到底*！”他冷笑着学了一遍。他存心叫她闭嘴。她又说了。

“姑姑向来待我好，伍利！”

他不肯答她。他知道接下来会是什么。

她怯怯地说：

“只怕咱们该回去接他。他要是真快死了呢，伍利！姑姑还在那儿等他呀！”

===Original===

He said never a word.

“He may be dead before she sees him, if we don’t.”

“We won’t!” he almost shouted. That should have settled matters.

“But what’ll you tell her? She’ll ask. She’ll find out you wouldn’t. You won’t can say you saw him dying, and didn’t bring him home!”

That was true. He had begun to think of that. Libby Keith would leave no detail of that death undiscovered.

“Will you say you went away and left him there to die?”

===Chinese===

他一声也不吭。

“咱们要不回去，怕他等不到见着姑姑的面，人就没了。”

“不回！”他几乎是吼出来的。这一句本该把事情了断了。

“可你怎么跟她交代？她要问的。她会打听着你不肯。你就没法说，你眼看着他快死，却没把他捎回家！”

这话不假。他已经开始想到这一层了。莉比·基思是非要把那场死的每一个细节都刨个水落石出不可的。

“你能说，是你掉头走了，把他撂在那儿等死吗？”

===Original===

What else could he say? He certainly wouldn’t tell that for one long rejoicing moment he had stood looking into the eyes that so terribly besought him—those eyes that were dying prayers, ultimate beseechings—and had turned victoriously away. He wouldn’t say that he had told the men who were seeking a ride home for that snake, that he had too heavy a load for so essential a favor. He wouldn’t tell how shortly he had answered them, and how hatefully turned on his heel and departed.

“Wully!” she said, after a little, with conviction, “we ought to go back and get him! We can’t treat Auntie this way!”

“Can’t we!” he exclaimed bitterly. “Giddup!” he cried to his horses.

He felt her wretchedness. He hardened his heart against her sentimentality. Presently she said imploringly;

“We can’t do this, Wully. We must go back!”

===Chinese===

他还能说别的吗？他断然不会说：有那么长长的一刻，他满心欢喜地站在那里，直望进那双这般骇人地哀告着他的眼睛——那双眼睛是垂死的祷告，是绝顶的哀恳——然后洋洋得意地扭过头去。他不会说：他曾对那些替那条毒蛇找车送他回乡的人讲，他的载重太沉，担待不起这样要紧的恩情。他不会说：他答话答得多么简短，又是多么恨恨地一转身走掉了。

“伍利！”过了一小会儿，她拿定了主意说，“咱们该回去接他！咱们不能这样对待姑姑！”

“不能吗！”他辛酸地喊。“驾！”他朝他的马喝道。

他觉出她的悲苦。他硬起心肠，不受她那份多愁善感的磨。不一会儿，她哀告道：

“咱们不能这样，伍利。咱们得回去！”

===Original===

“I will not!” He spoke passionately.

When she spoke again, it was to warn him.

“If you don’t go back, I will!”

“No you won’t!” he cried.

She was silent for several minutes then. He felt her bending down to see if the baby was covered. Then she sat still. She was hesitating. Then after a minute, before he could realize what was going on, she had climbed over the side of the wagon, her foot was on the hub, then, skirts and cloak and all, she had alighted, backwards, stumblingly, from the wagon. By the time he had pulled up the horses, she was the length of the wagon from him. Ignoring him, defying him, she was calling to him over her shoulder;

“He made me do evil once. You made me do evil once. But nobody can make me do it again!” Down the road she ran. “I’m going back to him!” she cried.

===Chinese===

“我不去！”他说得激动已极。

她再开口时，就是警告他了。

“你不回去，我去！”

“你休想！”他喝道。

这以后她静默了好几分钟。他觉出她俯下身去，看看娃娃的被子盖好没有。随后她端坐着不动。她在犹豫。跟着，不出一分钟的工夫，他还没明白过来是怎么回事，她已经翻过了车帮，一只脚踩上了轮毂，接着，连裙子带斗篷，倒退着、跌跌撞撞地从车上下来了。等他勒住马，她已经离他有了一车之远。不睬他，抗着他，她回过头冲他喊：

“他叫我作过一回恶。你也叫我作过一回恶。可谁也不能再叫我去作了！”她顺着大路飞跑。“我要回他那儿去！”她喊。

===Original===

He had never been really angry with her before. Sometimes at first, before the baby had been born, he had grown very weary of her importunity, her determination to make him tell his mother the truth. But of late she had not done that. She had been so satisfactory—so lovely. Now his rage burst forth against her.

“Go back to him, then, if you like him so well!” He hurled the words after her, and drove on.

Even before he heard her cry of protest, he regretted his bitter taunt. Furious with himself, with her, he hurried west. Already he had begun to see the mistake of his sweet refusal. It would inevitably become known that he had seen Peter’s straits, and had refused him so slight a kindness. The whole neighborhood would be asking the reason. He vowed to himself that he would not take that carcass into the wagon with his wife if all the world had to know the reason of his hatred. Such things were expected of no man. He was only human. He couldn’t do a thing like that! And his wife had defied him! She had left him! Ah, and he had taunted her so unjustly, so brutally! But he had never imagined himself saying so cruel a thing to her. He had never imagined her defying him in such a fashion. That was what she thought of him, then. He made her do wrong once! Classing him with that damned—That was all the gratitude she felt for his saving of her! But then, of course, it was an awful thing he had just done. He thought of himself lying sick on the sidewalk, waiting for a chance to get home. He hardened his heart. But he had been a decent man. No violator of women! He would never do it.

===Chinese===

他从来没有真正跟她动过怒。起初，娃娃还没落地的时候，他有时叫她缠得十分厌烦——她铁了心，非要他把实话告诉他母亲不可。可近来她不提了。她那样令人称心——那样可爱。这会儿他的怒火却朝她迸发出来。

“既然你那么稀罕他，那就回他那儿去好了！”他把这句话朝她背后砸过去，赶车走了。

还没听见她抗议的喊声，他已经后悔那句刻毒的挖苦了。他对自己光火，也对妻子光火，催马向西。他已经开始看出，他那番漂亮的回绝错在哪儿了。他眼见彼得的绝境，却回绝了他这样一点小小的恩惠，这桩事迟早会传扬出去。左邻右舍全会追问缘故。他暗暗起誓：哪怕全世界都得知道他怀恨的缘故，他也决不把那具尸壳弄上载着妻子的车。这样的事，本不能指望世上任何人做到。他不过是个人。这样的事他做不来！而他的妻子竟抗了他！她撇下他走了！唉，他还那样不公、那样狠毒地讥诮了她！他从来没想到自己会对她说出这样残忍的话。也从来没想到她会用这种方式来抗他。原来她心里就是这样看他的。他叫她作过一回恶！把他同那个该死的东西归成一路——这就是她对他搭救之恩的全部感念！可话说回来，他方才做的事也确实恶劣。他想起自己病倒在人行道上，等一个还乡的机会。他把心一硬。可他到底是个正派的人。不是糟践女人的那种人！这件事他决计不做。

===Original===

He turned and looked after his deserting wife. He could see her hurrying away from him. He had an idea of shouting to her to come back—of commanding her to come back. But he knew she wouldn’t heed him. He ought never to have said so hateful a thing to her. As if she could want to go back to that—He remembered how she had sat sobbing on the doorstep when he first went to her. He was glad to think of Peter Keith dying there, lonely, shrunken, filthy. He looked again after his wife. She went steadily eastward, running towards the town. But he had the baby. She would be coming back after a while!

He drove on, raging against her, trying to justify himself. He went so far that he could scarcely see her now. He might have gone on home, if there had not appeared on the horizon a team, coming towards him. Its approach was intolerable. Somebody who might know them was coming nearer. Somebody would see Wully McLaughlin riding westward, and presently overtake his wife running east! He turned around abruptly.

===Chinese===

他回过头，望着他那个丢下他走的妻子。他看得见她急急地离他远去。他起过念头要喊她回来——喝令她回来。可他知道她不会听。他原就不该对她说那样可恨的一句话。好像她还肯回到那个东西跟前似的——他记得他头一回上她那儿去时，她怎样坐在门前台阶上抽抽噎噎地哭。想到彼得·基思孤零零、干瘪龌龊地正在那儿死去，他心里竟觉得痛快。他又望了一眼妻子。她一路不停地朝东去，朝镇子跑。可娃娃在他车上。过上一阵，她总要回来的！

他往前赶车，跟她怄着火，竭力给自己开脱。他走得远了，这会儿几乎望不见她了。要不是地平线上出现一挂马车、迎着他过来，他兴许就一直回家了。那车越逼越近，叫人忍无可忍。一个兴许认得他们的人正在挨近。会有人看见伍利·麦克劳克林朝西赶车，跟着又撞上他妻子朝东飞跑！他猛地掉转了头。

===Original===

Facing east, he could just see her. He would quickly overtake her, and order her to get in and come home with him at once. He would never let her go to that livery stable full of drunks alone. He was getting near her.

Then a strange thing happened. He saw her stop and suddenly turn around, and come half running towards him as fast as she had run away. He kept his face hard, unrelenting. He saw when she came near that she was crying softly. She climbed quickly up when he stopped.

“I doubt he’s not dying,” she wept. “I can’t do it! He’s too strong, Wully! He’s tricky!”

She cuddled against him.

===Chinese===

掉头朝东，他刚好望得见她。他要快快赶上她，喝令她上车，马上跟他一道回家。他决不能让她独自一人上那家满是醉鬼的车马行去。他离她越来越近了。

跟着，出了一件怪事。他看见她停住脚，猛地转过身，半跑半奔地朝他来了，来得同去时一样快。他绷着脸，一点不松。她走近了，他看见她在低声地哭。他一停车，她就利利落落爬了上来。

“只怕他死不了。”她哭诉说。“我做不到！他太壮实了，伍利！他刁得很！”

她紧紧偎着他。

===Original===

“Don’t cry!” he had to say.

“I won’t look at him!” she sobbed. “You know I don’t want to go back to him! You oughtn’t to have said that! You know I don’t like him! If you want to know how much I hate him, I’ll tell you! It was me that shot him that time. It wasn’t his foot I was aiming at, either!” She wept unrestrainedly.

“You shot him!” Wully gasped.

“He would come back! What could I do! There was no place to hide. I *shot* at him!”

She had shot him! She had been as desperate as that. He was horrified anew. She bent down to feel the baby’s hands, to cover him more securely. She wanted to say something else, but she couldn’t speak plainly because of her sobs. Yet she managed to urge the horses eastward.

===Chinese===

“别哭了！”他不能不说。

“我不看他！”她抽抽噎噎地说。“你知道我不愿回他那儿去！你不该说那句话！你知道我不喜欢他！你要想知道我多恨他，我说给你听！那一枪是我开的。我当时瞄的还不是他的脚！”她放声大哭，再收不住。

“是你开的枪！”伍利倒抽一口冷气。

“他要回来呀！我能有什么法子！没处躲藏。我就是朝他*开的枪*！”

是她开的枪！她那时已经绝望到那个地步。他重新骇然起来。她俯下身去摸娃娃的小手，把被子给他掖得更严实。她还想再说一句什么，可是泣不成声，说不清楚。她还是设法把马催着朝东去了。

===Original===

“I’ll never look at him!” she cried passionately. “You needn’t think I like him! You oughtn’t to have said that!”

“I know it, Chirstie! I oughtn’t to have said such a thing. But you oughtn’t to have jumped out and run away that way.”

“Yes, I ought!” she retorted, swallowing, choking. “I couldn’t help it. It wasn’t my place to do it. But my husband wouldn’t do his part! Wully, if you hurry now, *hurry* enough, they’ll just think you’ve been unloading. You won’t need to explain! I won’t have you doing such a mean thing. I’ve got enough bad things to tell without that! Hurry!”

===Chinese===

“我永远不看他！”她激动地喊。“你别以为我喜欢他！你不该说那句话！”

“我知道，克丽丝蒂！那话我不该说。可你也不该那样子跳下车就跑呀。”

“就该的！”她回嘴，一咽一哽地说。“我忍不住。那本不是我的分内事。可我丈夫不肯尽他那一分！伍利，你这会儿要是赶紧的，赶得*够快*，他们只会当你卸货去了。你用不着解释！我不容你干这么下作的事。要交代的坏事情我够多了，不必再添这一件！快赶！”

## XXII / 第二十二章

===Original===

They had passed the bridge on their burdened way home. They had come to the place at which Chirstie had so astonishingly defied him. They had ridden together in a silence broken only by the refreshed wee Johnnie’s cooing, as he bounced back and forth in his mother’s lap. Wully looked covertly at his wife from time to time, in awe. She wasn’t thinking now what a nice baby Peter Keith had been. Never once had she turned her face towards what was in the wagon box, to see if it was indeed dying. Returning to town, she had instructed him, womanlike, to be sure that Peter had no weapons concealed, no way of hurting a benefactor. And Wully had unloaded his lumber raging. Caught, he was, trapped. Having to do this unspeakable thing to satisfy the sentimentality of a woman, and to save his secret from desecration. Grimly he had made sure from the doctor that there was no chance of Peter living to reveal what Wully had so well kept hidden. Coldly he had ordered the men at the stable to wash the blood from that face, from that matted beard, as if Peter was their cousin, and not his. Grudgingly he had helped them deposit the bony thing in the wagon. Covered to his head, still as a bag of meal, Peter lay there when Wully McLaughlin drove to the hotel to get his wife. And she had never once turned her head towards him.

And now, when Wully looked at her from the corner of his eyes, his own anger, his bitter hatred seemed a small thing before hers. Her face was as white as marble, and as hard, one might have thought. Her mouth was screwed tight in loathing. She sat perfectly still, looking straight ahead, tragically. She wasn’t thinking of Aunt Libby now. Wully was almost afraid of her﻿ ﻿… afraid certainly to offer her comfort.

They rode west. The sun was high now, and shone dazzlingly over the brown stretches. The horses felt the stimulus of the frosty morning. Wee Johnnie jumped about, chuckling out his absurd little meaningless words. Three miles they went; four miles. From time to time Wully turned to assure himself that his enemy lay still. He would let him die there, without lifting a finger to lengthen his life by a second. The sight of that shape under the old brown blanket inflamed his hatred. He looked, and turned quickly away, remembering always that second time Peter had dared to lay violent hands on his wife. It was that second time he could never forgive, that second time.

===Chinese===

他们载着这副重担往家走，已经过了那座桥。他们来到了那个地方——克丽丝蒂曾在这里那样叫人吃惊地违逆过他。此后两人同车而行，一路无言，只有缓过劲儿来的小约翰尼咕咕哝哝，在他母亲怀里颠来颠去，才打破这寂静。伍利不时偷偷觑一眼自己的妻子，心里怀着敬畏。她这会儿想的，可不是彼得·基思小时候曾是个多招人疼的娃娃。她一次也没有朝车斗里那东西转过脸去，看看它是不是真的要死了。方才回镇上去的时候，她以女人家的心思吩咐过他：务必搜明彼得身上没藏着凶器，别叫他有法子伤着恩人。伍利是满腔怒火地把木料卸的。他这是被拿住了，被套牢了。不得不干这桩说不出口的事，一来是遂一个女人的心软，二来是保住自己的秘密不遭亵渎。他阴沉着脸，从医生那里问了个确实：彼得决没有活着把那桩伍利守得这样严实的秘密抖搂出去的可能。他冷冷地吩咐马厩里那几个人，去洗净那张脸上的血，洗净那把纠结成毡的胡子，倒好像彼得是他们大家的表亲，而不是他一个人的。他老大不情愿地帮着他们把那把骨头安放进车斗。伍利·麦克劳克林赶车到旅店去接他妻子的时候，彼得就躺在那儿，身上一直盖到头，静得像一口袋面粉。而她自始至终没有朝他那边回过一次头。

这会儿，伍利用眼角觑她，觉得自己那股怒气、那口苦恨，摆在她跟前竟显得算不得什么了。她的脸白得像大理石，而且人也许会说，也硬得像大理石。她的嘴厌恶地拧得紧紧的。她坐得纹丝不动，直直望着前方，一脸悲怆。这会儿她心里并没有想着莉比姑姑。伍利几乎有些怕她……至少绝不敢上前去安慰她。

他们朝西走。太阳已经升高，在棕黄的原野上照得人眼晕。马也觉出了清寒早晨的那股提神劲儿。小约翰尼蹦来蹦去，咯咯地吐出他那些可笑又没意思的小字眼。他们走了三英里；四英里。伍利不时回过头去，好让自己确信他的仇人还静静躺着。他要让他就在那儿死去，一根指头也不抬，不多给他一秒钟的性命。旧棕毯底下那个形状，看一眼就叫他的仇恨烧得更旺。他看一眼，又赶紧扭过头去，心里总记着，彼得第二次竟敢对他妻子下那样的毒手。就是那第二次，他永远不能饶恕，就是那第二次。

===Original===

The baby grew restless. He complained fretfully of his mother’s lack of attention. Wully gave him, almost mechanically, the ends of the lines to play with. They pleased him, for a while. Then he turned again to his mother, unable to fathom her sternness. Never before had her hands touched him so coldly. Looking right ahead of her, she would pull that little shawl tightly around him again, after he had succeeded in working his bare arms out of it, tucking him in without a kiss or any coaxing. His eyes studied her face, and found there no thought for him. He stood up in her lap. He put his arms around her neck, and stroked the forbidden feather. She failed even to reprove him. He seized the chance﻿—he put the curling thing into his mouth, and chewed the end of it experimentally. He spit it out in disgust. He sat down again in her lap, and began playing with the frogs on her new coat. He fingered the interesting fringe. He squirmed about more vigorously than ever. He called to her. He put his hands up to her face. She bent down and kissed him, but not as she usually gathered him against herself with warmth. The caress was hard and preoccupied, and he whispered a little. He tried pat-a-caking, to get her to smile upon him. That, too, failed. Wully handed him the whip, and he shook it so fiercely that they had both hastily to rescue their faces from the blows he might have inflicted. Still his mother looked straight ahead.

They came then to a low place. The horses could go only very slowly. The baby adjusted himself to the new motion of the wagon. There was a splashing of mud that made him giggle delightedly. It would have been a choice morning for any baby whose mother wasn’t sitting frozen. Wee Johnnie made the best of it. He kicked, and giggled, and squirmed about.

The horses failed of their own accord to take their proper pace again. Wully had to speak to them. He slapped them lightly with the lines.

===Chinese===

娃娃渐渐不安生起来。他烦躁地抱怨母亲不理他。伍利几乎是下意识地，把缰绳的两头儿递给他玩。那玩意儿让他高兴了一小会儿。随后他又转向母亲，怎么也捉摸不透她怎么这样板着脸。母亲的手从没这样冷冰冰地碰过他。她两眼直望着前方，等他把光胳膊从那条小披巾里挣出来，就又把披巾紧紧裹回他身上，掖好了，不亲一下，也不哄一声。他的眼睛端详着她的脸，在那上面找不见一点儿属于他的心思。他在她怀里站了起来。他伸出胳膊搂住她的脖子，手摸上那根不许碰的羽毛。她连训他一句都没有。他抓住这个机会——把那个卷卷的玩意儿塞进嘴里，试着嚼了嚼那尖儿，又嫌恶地吐了出来。他重新在她怀里坐下，玩起她那件新大衣上的盘花纽扣。他捻弄那排有趣的边穗。他扭动得比先前更欢。他喊她。他伸上手去够她的脸。她低下头亲了亲他，却不像平常那样把他热乎乎地搂进怀里。这一下爱抚又硬又心不在焉，他小声哼唧起来。他试着跟她玩拍手游戏，哄她朝他笑一笑。这一招也不灵。伍利把鞭子递给他，他抡得那样凶，两个人只得赶紧护住自己的脸，躲他那没准儿抡过来的鞭梢。母亲依旧直直望着前方。

这时他们走到一处低洼地。马只能走得非常慢。娃娃也顺应起车子的新颠法来。泥水哗哗地溅，溅得他欢喜得咯咯直笑。对随便哪个娃娃，只要母亲不是这样僵坐着，这都是一个再好不过的早晨。小约翰尼尽力自得其乐。他蹬着腿，咯咯笑着，扭来扭去。

马儿自己没有再回到正该有的步子上。伍利只好开口催它们，拿缰绳在马身上轻轻拍了拍。

===Original===

“Get up, Nellie!” he exclaimed. “What’s the matter of you?”

Wee Johnnie moved his arms exactly as Wully had done.

“Get up, Nellie!” he said. “What’s the matter of you?”

===Chinese===

“驾，内莉（Nellie）！”他吆喝道。“你这是怎么啦？”

小约翰尼照方才伍利的样子，把胳膊一模一样地挥了挥。

“驾，内莉！”他说。“你这是怎么啦？”

===Original===

He said all that, plainly, if not perfectly, and before he knew what was happening, his mother had seized him, and was hugging him up against her, in the good old way, kissing him.

“Get up, Nellie!” he cooed. “What’s the matter of you!”

She had been so surprised, so delighted with her son’s first sentence that she had turned, even kissing him, to Wully, no joy complete unless he shared it.

“Did you hear *that*!” she cried triumphantly, her face blossoming towards him. “Say it *again*, Lammie!”

===Chinese===

这些话他说得清清楚楚，纵然算不上字字周正；还没等他明白过来出了什么事，母亲已经一把攥住他，照着那又好又老的办法把他紧紧搂进怀里，亲个不停。

“驾，内莉！”他咕咕地念叨。“你怎么啦！”

儿子有生以来的头一句话，叫她又惊又喜，她竟一面还在亲他，一面就转向了伍利——她的欢喜，不与他分享，就算不得圆满。

“你听见没有！”她得意地嚷道，脸朝他绽开来。“再说一遍，小羊儿！”

===Original===

And almost before Wully could smile in return, he stopped. He turned around. He thought he heard a groan from his load. He couldn’t even smile at her with that man possibly spying upon them. He looked﻿—and from the end of the wagon that man had lifted his head a little, like a snake, and had seen the smile that Chirstie had turned upon her husband. And Wully﻿—when he saw that face﻿—it was the last thing in the world that he intended doing﻿—but some way, in spite of himself, he achieved generosity﻿—the spoil, it may have been, of ancestral struggle. At the terrible sight of that face, he pitied his enemy. That coward, in his damned way, had loved Chirstie. And in his tormented sunken dying he had seen all the sweet intimacy from which he had been shut out and had sunk back, felled by the blow of that revelation. Wully had foregone revenge. He had forborne running a sword less sharp through his fallen enemy than Chirstie’s wifely smile had been. In a flash Wully saw himself sitting there by the woman, loved, living, not dying, full of strength and generations, while that man, loathed and rejected, was already burning in hell.

The poor devil!

He pulled the horses up suddenly, and gave his wife the lines. He climbed back to lift his cousin into a position less painful. Through holes in the old blanket, straws from beneath were scratching the ghastly face. There was a farmhouse not so far down the road.

“I’ll stop there and buy him a pillow,” Wully resolved.

===Chinese===

伍利还没来得及回她一个笑，就收住了。他回过头去。他觉得自己听见车上那件重负呻吟了一声。那个人没准正窥探着他们，他连朝她笑笑都不成了。他一看——车斗那一头，那个人已经稍稍抬起了头，像一条蛇，看见了克丽丝蒂转向她丈夫的那个笑。伍利——一看见那张脸——那是天底下他最不打算做的事——可不知怎么，他由不得自己，竟做到了宽厚——那兴许是祖辈争斗留下的一份战利品。一见那张可怕的脸，他竟怜悯起自己的仇人来。那个懦夫，用他那种该死的方式，也曾爱过克丽丝蒂。而在他受尽煎熬、一路沉下去的垂死之中，他看见了那全部甜蜜的亲昵——他自己一直被摈在外头的那些——便颓然倒了回去，叫这一层看清当头击倒。伍利已经舍了复仇。他忍住了，不曾朝那倒下的仇人再捅进一剑——那剑纵然锋利，也锋利不过克丽丝蒂那为人妻的一笑。一瞬间，伍利看见自己就坐在那个女人身旁，被爱着，活着，不是垂死，浑身是力气，浑身是子子孙孙；而那个人，被嫌恶，被拒斥，已经在地狱里受着火烧了。

可怜的家伙！

他猛地勒住马，把缰绳交给妻子。他爬回车斗，把他的表弟抱起来，给他换了个不那么疼的姿势。旧毯子的破洞里，从底下戳上来的麦秸正扎着那张灰败骇人的脸。顺这条路再往前走不远，有一户农家。

“到那儿我停一停，给他买个枕头。”伍利拿定了主意。
